president. - at the beginning of december it was announced that the european commission had completed negotiations on association agreements with syria. we never heard from him again. i think that, in the fight against terrorism, it is not necessary to dwell on the links between syria and organizations such as hamas, islamic jihad and hezbollah or on the direct aid it provides them. since the european union has over the years proved incapable of enforcing the human rights clause in all association agreements, it would be appropriate for this parliament to have the opportunity, before debating this issue, to receive from the european commission all the information on how an association agreement with a country such as syria would even be concluded.
mr president, a document is being handed over at tel aviv airport which is intended to prevent movement in the occupied palestinian territories of the west bank and gaza. anyone who wants to go to the palestinian authority territories must obtain written permission, failing which the israeli authorities can deport, arrest and prosecute anyone entering the areas of autonomy. it is, in my view, a very serious act and a violation of the sovereignty of the palestinian authority. the wall prevents palestinians from moving: it is now up to the israeli authorities to decide who palestinians can meet or not; that is, the prisoners are given leave by the prisoners. the same document was handed to european union staff and officials. i therefore ask the president, the european commission and also the council presidency to intervene and to ask the israeli authorities the reasons for this restriction of movement. i'm giving you the document that was collected by several foreign nationals.
mr president, two severe resolutions of this parliament are still allowing the fundamental right of european citizens to the protection of their personal data to be jeopardized. us agencies can access air passenger registry data directly without the data subjects' computerised consent, their right to rectification, or to a court or independent authority being guaranteed. there are no guarantees as to the duration and purposes of the data authorisation and, despite the commission's efforts, the future prospects are not reassuring either: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the data on the number of european citizens booking trips to the united states is stored in us databases for years. but what is the point, for our own security, of keeping the details of all their credit cards, and how are we going to deny other countries tomorrow what we allow the united states today? we had asked for reciprocity in respect of citizens' rights, but the commission is instead proposing, paradoxically, reciprocity in the exchange of data between security agencies. let's be careful! we are heading for a worrying future if we shift surveillance from the exceptional to the everyday, from the individual suspect to the general public, if we turn all citizens into suspects.
the rapporteur. mr president, madam commissioner fortunately both spanish, on this very important point it has already been said: the first was erika in december 1999, the second prestige in november 2002: the commission has already said that it has done a great deal. what we regret is that there is still no comprehensive legislation on criminal protection of the environment and indeed the conflict between the council and the commission has sunk the hopes, at least of us environmentalists, with the famous appeal later to the court of justice. on the other hand, the specific issue of marine pollution has already been mentioned and need not be repeated. the merits of the proposal for a directive are exceptional, as was the work of mr pex, who has considerably improved it. however, i believe that this directive will only have an impact if there is the political will to implement it, also because recent history teaches us that eu directives have almost always been ignored by the member states. the real problem is the unwillingness of the member states to comply with these rules and the fact that, unfortunately, we are now resorting to shelter when there have been impressive disasters. the proposed framework decision is nothing more than a decision which is very complementary to the directive. there is an excellent agreement and therefore both must be accepted by this parliament with all the amendments which have been tabled, which i think are excellent, except for some of my personal reservations about the european coast guard, as this body so proposed with a certain approximation gives us some concern. in substance, however, this directive is going in the right direction, although it should be pointed out that it is not enough if there is no overall framework for the protection of the environment in general, without which even maritime protection will always be a lesser protection. we must return to comprehensive community legislation on the environment as a whole and i believe that the council must show itself reasonable and accept the proposal for a directive on the criminal protection of the environment in general, which, i repeat, has been blocked by an appeal to the court of justice. it would be an excellent step because we would have adequate maritime protection but overall environmental protection which could have an equal degree of protection at criminal level, since there is no doubt that criminal sanctions are what most compel operators in the sector to comply with the rules. i think this directive is a step in the right direction and does a little justice to all operators, because it brings back to full control many operators who were excluded. we know very well that international conventions excluded from responsibility the shipowners and a whole series of people who were in fact directly and indirectly responsible, and we have proof of this in the shipwreck of the prestige, where the blame lies simply with a poor greek ship's master who, among other things, did everything possible to avoid the disaster, while others would be blamed for the erika disaster and its consequences. i therefore agree with both the report on the proposal for a directive and the report on the proposal for a framework decision, and i hope that both will be accepted by this parliament.
mr president, commissioner, i do not think i need to spend many words to emphasize, as has already been done by various rapporteurs and, in any case, by the speakers who have preceded me, the importance of the subjects we are discussing, maritime safety in particular, with which the various measures we are considering in this joint debate are concerned. i will simply say, without adding anything to what has already been said, that i too join in the applause for a very strong, very effective initiative by the commission in this direction, to which we hope that convergence, a common orientation also within the council, the completion of legislation that we need and to which the acceptance of the proposals under consideration today also makes a partial contribution, however concrete and significant, will correspond. as for myself, i have followed as a shadow rapporteur one of the reports which make up this package - the poignant report on the transfer of ships, not only cargo but also passengers, between the various national shipping registers - and i can only stress the positive and usefulness of both the commission's proposal and parliament's reaction as set out in the poignant report, which we fully share, also sharing the suggestions and indications, which were also confirmed this evening, as to the possible acceptance of the suggestions made by both the council and, above all, by the commission, which i consider very important. the text also makes it possible to put an end to divergent interpretations of the various conventions, thus contributing first of all to legal certainty by simplifying the procedure for changing the flag between registers for passenger ships and thus simplifying and reducing the burden of european legislation. what is even more important, in my view, is that it ensures control of the aspects of safety which are indispensable to us, since the proposed procedure provides not only for better cooperation and the exchange of information between national maritime administrations but also, where appropriate, for additional inspections of vessels: the importance of this was underlined in a perspective of overall enlargement, including countries such as malta and cyprus, which, in this respect, by tradition, by productive commitment, by the articulation of shipping and the fleet, constitute an important reference. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur and the commission on their proposal and express my full support on behalf of my group for the positions expressed here.
mr president, commissioner, i believe that the commissioner is right to call for effective action in this area of maritime safety, which has been produced in this parliament. it is a series of measures that complete a mosaic of fruitful work, from the double-hull provision for oil tankers to the blacklisting of vessels that are no longer able to face the sea, to the request to the member states to provide us with a list of safe havens: the commission has already taken a number of measures during this term of office, and we must give credit to the commission for them, but we must also claim our share of them. that is why i am, and we are, convinced of supporting in particular the report by mr pex, in which i would like to stress an amendment which i have heard partly contested here but which i think we should all appreciate the usefulness of the request that a european coastguard should be established as soon as possible, in the time and manner that are appropriate. we cannot ask for more controls, we cannot ask for sanctions if we do not have the means to do so. it would also be a great step forward for a whole series of other problems we have in the european union, not least illegal immigration. i think, ladies and gentlemen, that we must give it a general thought. on the issues of safety - air safety, as we heard earlier, maritime safety, i would add food safety - european public opinion is sometimes much ahead of governments; the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments. we must be encouraged by this feeling of public opinion and also ask the council, which has been somewhat reticent on these issues up to now, not to disappoint such important expectations that have been growing among our fellow citizens in recent years and which can really give us important results for the future.
mr president, i too would like to thank mr herzog for his work with passion and seriousness. i believe, however, that the text produced by the economic committee reflects the situation and prospects for services of general interest in a more appropriate way. services of general interest are a major part of the european economy, which is increasingly a service economy. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council adopt a common position on the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the labelling, presentation and advertising of foodstuffs for sale to the ultimate consumer. i think it would be a mistake to think that we can crystallize the situation of the provision of services of general interest in the context of a framework directive, in the context of new ad hoc legislation. these sectors are not easily defined and are areas of great development: what is a public service today can be a sector with products and services that can be fully market-based. such sclerosis, such crystallization would be a mistake. i believe, as the report on article 19 says, that the rules currently in place for the internal market and competition ensure, in themselves, that in this as in other areas, public intervention, where necessary, takes place within the rules already laid down by the treaties, guaranteeing competitiveness and dynamism to the economy that provides public services. otherwise, we will have stagnation and danger for the european economy.
mr president, commissioners, i appreciate and support the commission's strategy of multilateralism. europe, much more than the united states, needs multilateral bases for the development of international trade. there is one point, mr president, on which i strongly disagree. commissioner lamy said that negotiations on agriculture were being revived and that the european union had stopped being always on the defensive. i think that, politically, that is not true at all. of course, the united states also has problems with agriculture, from a protectionist point of view, as does japan; but europe remains a cornerstone of the world system of protectionism and welfare aid for farmers in rich countries. the so-called reform has not made sufficient progress in this regard. just look at the budgets of the european union: we continue to spend 40% of the union budget to subsidize and protect the wealthy farmers who are a declining sector of the european economy. is it possible that it is not understood that we are discussing the future of europe's international trade? and we have to do it with commissioner lamy and we would be missing something else! and with commissioner fischler? i have nothing against commissioner fischler personally, but we have the representative of the interests of 'agriculture' and not of 'informatics', not of 'industry' of the commission or the council. how is that possible? in conclusion, mr president, agriculture remains the ball in the net in the negotiations on europe's world trade, and agricultural protectionism is hurting europe even more than the third world countries.

mr president, i shall reflect on the sector that is most dear to me - the agricultural sector - because, while i share the policy of guaranteeing the developing countries the maximum benefits of the expansion of world trade, i would like to stress how the european union has already granted so many trade preferences that it has become the main export market for agricultural products. i would also like to think about further concessions and the proposal for duty-free access, because this is not, in my view, the most effective way of helping farmers in these countries, and it would put many of europe's strategic products into an irreversible crisis. as a negotiating tactic i would strengthen the arguments in defence of the community system, precisely to ensure the mechanisms of stability of the world market, knowing that the stability of world prices is an advantage for all the members of the world trade organization. to do this and to avoid bringing the agricultural sector into a structural crisis, i insist that the outcome of the wto negotiations should not go beyond the decisions taken in berlin in the context of agenda 2000 and that price and market support remain a fundamental element of the community's agricultural policy. following the changes, which i believe are very clever, which are being made to the us support system, we need to review our domestic support to see how far it is complying with the gatt agreement and to assess the effect of aid on market prices and the competitive position of commodities. as a final point, it would be important to bring the issue of the enormous challenges facing the european union with enlargement within the wto negotiations. it is a global process which, in my view, must be recognised at the negotiating table.

madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, on behalf of the union for a europe of nations group, i should like to begin by congratulating mr varela on his excellent work. my group of course fully supports your report. i would like to stress two points which we consider particularly sensitive: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the programme and will continue to monitor the implementation of the programme. we all know, after years of testing, scientific analysis and expert opinion, that fish consumption is increasing: fish is good for health, the consumption of fish products whether fresh, smoked, frozen or canned by european citizens is steadily increasing year by year, partly due to numerous and effective advertising and information campaigns. we all know that in our countries various requirements must rightly be met for the marketing of european canned fish products, particularly those of a hygienic and sanitary nature. in order to ensure adequate protection of the health of consumers, the same requirements should also be extended to products from third countries where, unfortunately, health standards are often lax or, in some cases, completely absent. we therefore strongly support and reiterate the demands in paragraphs 8 and 10 of the motion for a resolution, which stress the need to intensify inspections of products from third countries - already provided for in directive 493/91 - and the need to set up community laboratories to protect the quality and safety of foodstuffs for consumers against so-called food piracy. the delicate question of consumer health protection is linked to the equally important question of competitiveness, and hence employment, in the tuna fisheries and canning industry. if, in order to ensure adequate consumer protection, certain requirements, including the hygiene and health requirements mentioned above, which involve considerable investments of money, are made necessary and if the same requirements are not applied to products from third countries, with the inevitable entry into the community market of products at prices much lower than those in europe, the competitiveness of european tuna fisheries and the canning industry is unfairly harmed. the serious risk is the loss of jobs in a sector which has already been hit hard in recent years by a worrying socioeconomic crisis. we therefore note the need to support this sector with all possible regulatory and economic efforts, which is also an important source of employment in other mediterranean regions, including sicily, where tuna fishing and processing have provided economic support for a considerable part of the working population for centuries. we want to accompany this message of hope with the certainty of reality.
madam president, commissioner, rapporteur, we must indeed congratulate mr varela on his excellent work, which, at least in the words of the commissioner, is shared by the commission in its aims and objectives which we all set ourselves. this is certainly a comforting and encouraging fact. there are, in fact, the by-catch aspect and the environmental sustainability aspect of this very important fisheries sector, which have been well dealt with, and there are the other two aspects which - and here i would like to make an appeal to colleagues - we must always try to keep in balance. the tuna fishery is of great importance to our european union in the two sectors of which it is composed: on the one hand, the extractive fishing sector fleets fishing for tuna in our waters and outside the european union and, on the other hand, the important pillar which is the processing sector, which processes tuna from europe but also, as has been mentioned, from asian and other seas in particular. we must certainly pay attention to safety, particularly in relation to these products, but - and i am glad that the rapporteur agreed with me on this - we must not rush into this. we must also be very cautious in relation to customs procedures. with this report we are asking for a study, an evaluation, which the commission will have to make. i agree with the commissioner that the preferential system is a system which is so far yielding results, whereas we have one of the highest duties compared to other countries. however, maintaining the balance between the two pillars of the european fisheries industry - processing and tuna fleets - is an objective that we cannot afford to lose and must pay attention to. i believe that with the wisdom of this house - and this report is a counter-evidence - we will be able to ensure both, and thus a more prosperous and sustainable tuna fishing industry in the european union.
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, two years ago, when parliament began to examine the regulation in question, it seemed unthinkable to define the legislative procedure with a near-unanimous consent. however, with tomorrow's vote and the conclusion of the second reading, we can say that the procedure is essentially closed with the consent of all the parties concerned. it must be said that the commission's text already introduced a major innovation in the legislation on this subject, but the amendments to the original text are by no means marginal. the definition of 'surfactant', additional testing, derogation from authorisation, labelling, better and more consumer information are all aspects that really extend the protection of the environment and human health and consider animal welfare in the context of product development research. it should also be stressed that the text under consideration takes account of the difficulties of small and mediumsized enterprises, for which procedural simplifications have been introduced without derogating from the necessary safeguards. i shall not go into further detail on the text which i hope parliament will be able to vote on tomorrow by a large majority. i would like to thank all the groups and their shadow rapporteurs with whom, over this long period, the text under discussion was worked out in a very constructive dialogue. i think that everyone can derive satisfaction from aspects or parts of the text itself for their constructive contribution and still find themselves sharing the overall text in the evaluations expressed above. i would also like to thank the commission for its willingness to reconsider its proposals and for its commitment to harmonizing a text, which is certainly not easy, to make it fit for purpose. i must say that, even with the commission itself, in the face of a continuous exchange of proposals and an understanding of everyone's needs, a legislative product of mutual satisfaction was finally prepared. it is true that the council also made an important contribution to the text, and in view of the commitment made by the italian presidency just completed, it effectively accompanied the text's progress, even intervening promptly when it seemed that the common position, which was intended to start the second reading, was being suspended. i do not want to forget the commitment and interest of the irish government's representation long before the current presidency took office, showing a real desire to define the text under consideration as best as possible. that said, in view of the long history of our common journey, i refuse to believe that the only point on which there seems to be disagreement can really be a reason for initiating the conciliation procedure. i refer to the so-called revision rule, where the commission undertakes to submit to parliament and the council a text on the anaerobic biodegradability of surfactants and the biodegradability of other components within five years and, within three years, proposals on phosphates. but the problem seems to be not so much that, as the possibility that this will happen in the next legislature, and this is because the formula of ritual about the entry into force of this regulation would in fact prevent the implementation of the above commitment in the next legislature. as you know, compromise texts have been submitted on all of them, and as i said, they are generally accepted. the solution to the above problem is contained within the abovementioned texts. i am sure that all the groups will agree on this solution and i would hope, appealing to the commission itself, that the matter can be resolved happily and that a somewhat problematic process can be brought to a satisfactory conclusion.
the scope of the measure under consideration is clear and is shared. the council's common position provides an acceptable basis for compromise, but some of the issues which were discussed and approved by parliament at first reading have not been taken into account. i therefore also believe that it is desirable to improve this balance in the light of the essential objectives of this legislation: health, consumer safety and protection of the environment. the amendments tabled by my group, in agreement with the rapporteur, are designed to ensure that these objectives are achieved, in compliance with the key principles of the environmental strategy: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council adopt a common position on the proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a serious accident at work. the fact that the scope of the regulation has been restricted to the biodegradability of surfactants contained in detergents does not contradict those principles. i believe that anaerobic biodegradation, the biodegradation of the main non-surface-active organic ingredients and the content and use of phosphates - especially this one - should be the subject of a specific regulation as a complementary matter to the regulation of detergents. we therefore call almost unanimously in committee for a new legislative proposal to be tabled to prohibit these substances progressively or to restrict their specific uses within three years of the publication of this regulation. that is what was agreed in advance between the representatives of parliament and the council in view of the attempt to close the legislative measure at second reading, a package which i consider reasonable, especially for a rapid adoption of the regulation. i therefore believe that the adoption of any other amendment which departs from this compromise offers a rather narrow margin of improvement, if not jeopardising the whole regulation. i therefore argue that parliament should not depart from this balance, and it would be paradoxical if it did so in fact on the grounds of reducing the environmental impact of the measure. finally, i can only congratulate the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs from all the political groups on their availability and commitment.

madam president, as our rapporteur rightly says, defining cultural diversity is difficult and even risky, almost as difficult as defining culture itself. however, the resolution, which is based on a concept which is difficult and risky, does not hesitate, in 47 paragraphs, to claim to regulate, regulate and promote what is called cultural diversity, thus deluding itself, in the age of the internet, of stopping the liberalization and globalisation of culture, information and knowledge. so after having been threatened with death by nationalism, fascism and communism in the past, europe is now threatened with death by legalism. the text of this resolution is a clear example of this. its contents defy the principle of non-contradiction and even elementary common sense and end up in a gross hypocrisy. and hypocrisy makes all forms of cultural production equally meritorious, not only for survival but even for promotion, putting liberal culture and authoritarian culture, the culture of infibulation and that of sexual equality, the culture of tolerance and that of racism and anti-semitism on the same level. in fact, the contradictions in this resolution reflect those of our policy, which seeks allies in the third world to protect it and protect itself against the invasion of american audiovisual media, in the name of respect for cultural diversity, but then to finance, for example, the francophonie in africa with all its might; the culture which, in the name of assimilation, forbids the chador and other religious symbols in school classrooms, and yet has no objection to their authoritarian imposition in other countries; the culture which exalts european democracy, even when it is in disrepair, and which, however, racially considers the third world unfit to receive it; the culture which rejects the export of freedom does not hesitate to collaborate with dictators and tyrants, ignoring today the struggle of islamic dissidents as it ignored the dissidents of the communist east yesterday. i see in all this not a more respectful and fair choice to defend the heritage of european and world humanity, but an amorphous, hypocritical and contradictory protectionism that would like to protect intellectual, national and provincial elites against the dynamism and cultural confrontation that, in its essence, is always... mr president, the report would seem to say that the enemy of culture is the liberalization of markets and the world trade organization, and that the bulwark of culture or cultures is the nation states. it 's not like that . the erasure of cultures and peoples has historically been carried out by nation states and nationalist ideologies. commissioner reding, china has carried out and is carrying out the worst genocides of peoples and cultures: tibetan people and culture, uyghur people and culture. china cannot be brought to this house as an example of fruitful cooperation for cultural diversity, unless we take as a reference for the word 'cultural' the cultural revolution of ancient memory. cultural diversity is not an abstract right; the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of europe, and it is not only the european parliament that has been involved in the debate, but also the european parliament itself. the rules are certainly needed, particularly to combat the destruction of languages, but it is precisely the policies of teaching a single foreign language in our national states or the rules of life of the european institutions that are opposed to the spirit of diversity called for in the resolution: european institutions with monolingual or bilingual agencies that issue reports for the public without even doing so in the official languages of the european union. in the explanation of vote we, the radical members, will recall our proposals for the observatory on language policies for the promotion of the international language esperanto, for the right to free and non-discriminatory international communication, for the preservation and affirmation of linguistic diversity.
mr president, mr president of the council, mr president of the commission, i would like to say on a personal note that i wish the irish presidency all the best. we have much more in common than we could have imagined in the past, perhaps because much of the europeans here today are of italian or irish extraction. we lived with new york and with america our chance to have peace, bread, freedom, work. we had to go there to look for it, and on the other hand ireland and italy also have a choice between the counter-reformist popery, which still weighs on our two societies, and a europe of freedom, of reform and politics and civil and economic. that's all i wanted to remember and put into context. be careful, mr president of the council! europe is again risking, with the vatican vetoes on freedom of research, a very dangerous bloodletting in terms of culture, industry and civilization of its own countries. in short, mr president: pay close attention to the reform, international today; i would also like to draw the attention of the president of the commission to the community of democracies, to which the european union has been either absent or blindly hostile. in geneva, we shall discuss this again this afternoon. there is the commission on human rights. mr president, mr president of the commission, change your course! we are asking you to speak decisively on this subject, as we did with emma bonino, as we did in the past few days with a beautiful speech by president cox in yemen and sanaa, with that charter and declaration of human rights, made by the predominantly arab states, which i hope we will not have to continue for long in terms of supplementing the european union and our states. i wish you well, but i wish you well on the road to a europe of freedom and reform, not on the road to counter-reform and obscurantism!
mr president, mr president of the council, mr president of the commission, we welcome very much the irish decision to commit itself to building a safer europe, one that is ever closer to its citizens and which finally has its first constitution, the fruit of the work of the convention. we are convinced, mr president, that you will have our support in achieving these objectives. ireland has the task of completing the good work of the italian presidency, as you said this morning. mr president of the commission, if this semester is to be a success, however, an important political problem must be solved: what will be the role of the community executive? the answer to this question depends on what the president of the commission intends to do. at the beginning of this year, by attacking the italian presidency, which she had praised in official conferences, you confirmed your intention to intervene in the italian election campaign. we expect you to give us a clear answer to the basic question put to you by parliament: do you intend to continue to lead the executive or have you decided to run for the next european elections? mr president of the commission, you have a choice to make, as mr poettering and mr barón crespo have repeatedly reminded you. europe needs a commission with a full-time leader this semester and next. the council and parliament need a commission which plays its role as the initiator of the legislative initiative more than it has done so far. the negotiations for the election of commissioners for the enlargement countries, the opinion on turkey, the presentation of the union's financial perspective, the support you announced for the signing of the constitutional treaty require balance and a president committed only to brussels and strasbourg.
mr president, i too welcome the irish presidency's priority, after the disagreements on key points in the convention proposal which led to the failure of brussels, to bring europeans back together. the june elections must not be allowed to deepen the union's crisis, instead of giving a new democratic impetus to europeanism. parliament wants to return to the constituent process without delay and appreciates the commitment to reconvene the intergovernmental conference and to act in a transparent manner, with ever closer relations between the union's institutions and citizens. we also share the multilateral approach to a common foreign and defence policy, to a serious partnership with the united states, to the relaunching of the united nations and to the resumption of negotiations on international trade. however, public opinion is looking to the future with concern. the spring council will record the successes achieved but it will also run the risk of weakening the social model without making the european economy more competitive in the world. we must reflect on the choices needed to relaunch the lisbon strategy, but also on the union's financial prospects, that is, on the means europe has at its disposal to meet the challenges clearly mentioned by president prodi, challenges which concern first and foremost the enlargement of the union but, in the longer term, above all the future of the young generations of our continent.

i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i would just like to say that i believe - and that is why i voted for the de rossa report - that monkeys and other exotic species are valuable resources of our planet and that in a modern and developed nature and environmental protection these resources should be protected and preserved for future generations. i believe that the de rossa report is an excellent piece of work in this direction and that it will contribute to the conservation and protection of endangered species. it also points out the need to protect such species and raises public awareness of this. amendment no 2 was adopted positively. i had hoped that the other two amendments would also be accepted. it is not, but at any rate we have a workable solution here, and that is why i voted for it.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as a venetian citizen and therefore heir to a strong historical, cultural and linguistic identity, i would like to remind you of the most serene republic of venice. i hope that all the countries of the european union will adopt policies aimed at decentralising the powers of the state towards local authorities, especially public education, culture and the protection of cultural heritage. we believe that our diversity and our cultural identities are the strong appeal of this europe, which must not be concentrated but must make room for the regional identities of all the peoples included in it. i would therefore like to stress the need to bring back to the territory and to deal with the identities on the territory, so that the great cultural heritage which unites all the peoples of this europe may be an example of democracy and above all of the great strength of our diversity in this european identity.
it is necessary, as the report argues, to arrive at a legal definition of the right to cultural diversity in international law. the lega nord is also very important from the point of view of autonomist and regionalist movements such as the one we represent in this parliament, and it rightly stresses the need to pay attention to the treatment of minority peoples and languages, including, of course, primarily, indigenous languages, also within the framework of enlarged europe. as regards the protection of the languages of minority populations, member states are invited to ensure the preservation of monuments, architecture and even everyday objects, which constitute the material history of these cultures. however, much more extensive and comprehensive action is needed to protect the immense cultural heritage of minority languages and peoples: the european commission has launched a project to develop a european database on literature, art, folklore, books and manuscripts at risk of extinction. in our own padania, for example, there is an immense linguistic cultural heritage ranging from piedmontese to the venetian language mentioned by my colleague gobbo to lombard, ladin and many, many minority languages which are in danger of extinction, for which the adoption of the devolution policy, sponsored by the current italian government, is very important.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) the radical members of parliament voted against the report not only because it hinders the liberalization of international trade and creates cultural relativism and protectionism, but also because it does not seem to take into account the risk of the cultural and linguistic destruction of the nationalists and the state socialists. national policies which harm linguistic diversity in foreign language teaching - that is, the real obligation to learn only one foreign language, namely english - are dominant both in the eu and in the acceding countries. the eu institutions themselves are often only one or two languages. the decision lacks a reference to innovative methods of meeting the communication requirements on a non-discriminatory basis, for example by providing for the use of neutral languages such as the international language esperanto. references to the lack of protection of indigenous and immigrant languages and sign languages are also missing. among the concrete proposals which have already received some support at international level, i would like to mention the proposal by the radical esperanto association, associazione radicale esperanto, to set up an observatory for language policies, as was also recommended recently at the 32nd general conference of unesco. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster is a good one.
in writing. - (it) i agree with mr cappatto's explanation of vote and stress the importance of the osce, the organization for security and cooperation in europe. the osce promotes security by all means other than direct armed intervention and thus also works to promote respect for cultural and linguistic diversity. the osce has two bodies for this purpose, namely the high commissioner for national minorities and the representative on freedom of the media, which have placed - and continue to place - great emphasis on the language issue. most recently, at their meeting in maastricht in december 2003, the osce foreign ministers adopted a resolution on tolerance - resolution 4/03 - in article 10 of which they recall the high commissioner for national minorities and his recommendations on education and languages, including in the field of radio and television broadcasting, and the recommendations on the representative on freedom of the media, the text of which is available at www.osce.org. the representative carried out an in-depth study of media in multilingual societies from 2002 to 2003, using five countries as a starting point: luxembourg, switzerland, the former yugoslav republic of macedonia, moldova and serbia-montenegro. on 13 and 14 september, the osce will hold a conference on racism and xenophobia in brussels, where the issue of linguistic diversity will hopefully be addressed.
mr president, as president ahern said this morning, respect for the rights of the human person is at the heart of european policy. however, despite all the union's efforts, human rights are still being violated in too many areas not only of the world but also within the union itself. the trafficking in human beings, the trafficking in organs, the new forms of slavery that are coming to our attention in the third millennium, the unregulated use of the internet, which in too many cases violates the laws of our countries and becomes a vehicle for child pornography, for human trafficking, for the transmission of information between terrorists who have no respect for human life and dignity, are just a few of the many examples we could give. we must demand from the united nations that those rights which are being violated in so many countries today be respected. the children who are forced to work or become guerrillas to survive are also those children in our rich societies who see their image and bodies used for commercial activities that are contrary to the rights associated with their own age. too much talk and proclamation in the face of urgent needs that require strong political decisions, such as the decision to prevent the use of children to advertise or promote gainful activities and the decision to make certain economic relations with third countries subject to respect for human rights. the countries which do not have a secular system but are bound by the application of sharia, with all that this religious law entails in violation of human rights, must be confronted with clear decisions by the union and the international community. if, on the one hand, cooperation remains a means of being closer to the people, on the other hand, maintaining stable economic relations with governments which violate the principle of human rights would be guilty and would in fact negate all the good words we have spoken in these years. we call on the irish presidency, with the support of our parliament, to stress the need for stronger and more political statements at the next meeting with the united nations.
mr president, mr minister, mr commissioner, thank you for the information, but i would like to ask a question, make a comment and perhaps make a proposal. the question is this: do we agree that when we speak of 'human rights' we also mean civil and political rights? in other words, do we agree with the position of kofi annan who recently declared that democracy is a human right in itself, as is clear from article 21 and others of the universal declaration? the question i pose is very important, and it also emerged as a result of a conference on democracy and human rights, held in yemen a few days ago and organized by "there is no peace without justice", where fifty countries of the arab world issued a statement that states this very clearly. the second point is a comment: it is clear that the geneva commission on human rights is not only a recent instrument - it was set up in 1993 - but a very weak one, and because of this very weakness it has become extremely politicized. it is a body that never has sufficient and clear financial terms of budget, let alone institutional powers or human resources. in short, it is at best a complaints body, but even in this respect it has not done its job properly, precisely because it is made up of opposing blocs, each protecting its own members in its own regional group. commissioner, while it is true that there is cause for concern that the african group is questioning the australian presidency, it is also true - and i must add - that last year europe abstained on the request to vote against the libyan presidency on regional grounds. it is perhaps worth recalling this, commissioner, minister, because if we had had a clearer position last year, not on the country of origin but on the standard of human rights in that country, we might have had a few more cards to support the australian national candidate today. finally, a proposal: i do not think it is possible any longer to go on with simple, albeit necessary, complaints against various countries, from tunisia to vietnam, and we could add a whole list of them. i believe that the problem is to strengthen the institutions in charge and therefore the policy. for example, i still do not understand and i have been asking since 95 why the european commission does not have a commissioner for human rights. it completely escapes me, no one has ever given an answer. finally, i believe that the australian candidacy episode itself reinforces the need to create, within the framework of the united nations, the community of democracy and democracies. only institutional strengthening can move policy from words to deeds: and so far, there have been many words, but certainly very few deeds.
mr president, we appreciate the statement by the president-in-office of the council that we cannot compromise on the issue of human rights. unfortunately, the experience of the european institutions often shows us the opposite. i should like to say a few words about two issues on which i believe europe must regain all its political prestige and spend it in geneva: the first is called the death penalty. the italian presidency's backhandedness in the resolution on the moratorium on the death penalty at the united nations assembly, announced by the presidency and never presented, is a serious fact that has exposed the european union and, above all, has robbed it of political authority, demonstrating that even on a topic apparently so shared and so precious as human rights and the denial of the death penalty as a judicial solution, we are hostage to the petty selfishness of many governments. it is a battle that must be fought politically, starting in geneva. in china, last year, we recorded 3,138 executions; and there are also many executions in many so-called liberal democracies, from taiwan to thailand, from japan to india, to the united states. we cannot approach an issue like this on the level of political expediency and, above all, we cannot imagine that it is only a battle of pure principle and testimony, because it is not, and this is demonstrated by the fact that recently the governor of illinois, we believe also feeling the stimulus, the force of pressure of an international public opinion, decided to commute 167 death sentences. another important point, mr president, concerns journalists, namely the right to speak and not to remain silent, an honest figure for really understanding the quality of a democracy. over the last decade, more than 400 journalists have been killed, and very few of them have been brought to justice. in the last six months we have had 228 arrests, 250 cases of unjustified trials, many of them also in countries belonging to the so-called group of western democracies; the last case was two days ago in turkey. we believe, among other things, that the repression of journalists and press freedom has learned to use more subtle means. violence is being committed and consummated against the freedom to speak and not to remain silent, whether by muzzling, censoring or obscuring, and unfortunately these are practices that have recently found a welcome in our countries, including my own. we would like to convey to the presidency the request for a rigorous and courageous mandate, the political commitment which i would like to try to define on the subject of human rights in just two words, which are the words which this parliament has always conveyed to the commission and the presidency: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council.
commission. mr president, i am pleased to be here today to present the main aspects of the commission communication on immigration, integration and employment, and would like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur, mr moraes, on the excellent quality of his report. this very important commission communication completes our response to the tampere mandate and fulfils the pledge to examine the role of immigration in relation to the lisbon strategy. the communication carries two key messages. the first – which has already been expressed in the commission communication of november 2000 – is that, in the context of demographic ageing and decline, sustained immigration flows are likely and necessary if the european union is to meet the lisbon goals. secondly, the full potential of immigration and its contribution to economic development can only be realised if immigrants are fully integrated both into the labour market and into society in general. given the estimated speed of population decline from 2010 onwards and the results of the national survey conducted by the commission, there is an urgent need to strengthen integration policies, as was called for by the european council in tampere and more recently at the european council in thessaloniki. the commission is pleased that the rapporteur has laid the emphasis on the potential which migrants bring with them. it fully agrees that integration is a matter for society as a whole and that it is a two-way process, based on mutual rights and corresponding obligations, which implies that both the immigrant and the host society must adapt. i would also like to emphasise that we fully agree that we must ensure a european legal framework for the admission of economic migrants and that member states must keep to the promises made in tampere. the commission has taken note of the difficulties encountered when negotiating in the council the directive on admission for employment purposes, and is currently considering how best to approach bringing forward this politically sensitive issue. a green paper, followed by an extensive consultation procedure, is envisaged and we are also undertaking a study on the links between illegal and legal migration, which may provide pointers for the future development of a more coherent legal framework for the admission of migrants for employment purposes. adoption of the directive on the admission of researchers is also envisaged for the very near future. negotiations on the directive on the admission of students is now entering the core phase and i am convinced that this proposal too will shortly be adopted. the communication considers that, in order to ensure successful integration, there is a need for an holistic approach that takes into account not only economic and social aspects – these are very important – but also issues relating to cultural and religious diversity, civic citizenship, participation and political rights. the commission is therefore pleased to note the specific attention paid in the report to the concept we put forward of civic citizenship and the need to promote a sense of belonging. a key condition for the successful implementation of an holistic approach of this type is that there should be improved overall policy coherence and synergies between immigration, integration and employment policies at all levels and across all disciplines and between all relevant actors, bearing in mind that prime responsibility for successful integration ultimately lies with the member states. finally, i would like to stress that i fully agree with, and welcome, the rapporteur's suggestion to establish programmes for the initial reception of persons arriving in an eu member state. we are currently implementing the pilot projects in support of the integration of migrants. there has been enormous interest in these pilot projects and the programme has been oversubscribed to a factor of ten. we allocated eur 4 million and applications have been made for more than eur 41 million. let me end by saying that i believe, and hope, that this communication on immigration, integration and employment will provide a basis for strengthening integration policy for immigrants, which will be crucial both for the development of the eu economy and for the promotion of social cohesion in the next few decades.
the commission has also proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. mr president, i am replacing the rapporteur, mrs anna terrón, who had to return to spain urgently. i fully agree with the speech by commissioner vitorino and the report by mr moraes, in the light of the opinion adopted by the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs, which stresses the following points: the council's delay in adopting the legislative instruments presented by the commission to implement the tampere programme, a delay which was fortunately partially overcome in december with the adoption of the important directive on long-term residents; the need to maintain a comprehensive vision of immigration policy linked to employment and social integration policies; the usefulness of agreements with countries of origin to ensure the transfer of acquired social security rights; the need to counter illegal immigration with repressive policies, directed not against irregular immigrants but against the traffickers who exploit them, and to counter undeclared work without distinction between immigrant citizens; lthe usefulness of facilitating legal access for immigrants to the labour market in order to reduce the demand and supply of the clandestine market; the opportunity to combat discrimination and to pay particular attention to the precarious situation of immigrant women. in particular, the opinion stresses the need to create a form of citizenship - known as civic citizenship - which would enable those who are permanently resident in the union from third countries to have rights and obligations in the economic, social and political fields, including the right to vote in local and european elections, as the european parliament has repeatedly called for. only in this way will all workers and residents be able to feel part and parcel of the community's destiny, share its values, contribute to its growth and to peaceful and harmonious coexistence.
the committee on petitions has already adopted a number of amendments. mr president, i fully agree with commissioner vitorino that immigration is a two-fold process, with rights and duties, which must be met. this problem has other aspects too, among which we must certainly examine those of income, housing and the enjoyment of freedoms, in short, integration into our european society. i think it is about turning, in this case, these guest workers into citizens. but what kind of idea of europe are we projecting to the outside world, mr commissioner, if, as we enter the union, the schengen system, instead of facilitating the creation of an area of freedom, security and justice, is often gradually transformed into a formidable instrument of control or computer filing? the problem of housing, the problem of not wanting to see, the tendency to focus and crystallize our fears in the media into ghosts that concern, precisely, very misunderstood aspects of the cultural identity of our societies. this is often a reductive image. the report, with which i fully agree, should perhaps have dealt with some of the issues which are well spelled out in the commission's communication, namely civil citizenship, the guarantee of certain basic duties, fundamental rights, respect for diversity, the fight against discrimination, women, the family, civic, cultural and political life, participation, education and, not least, religious practice. i believe that it is necessary to take account of these aspects, precisely in the light of the legal tradition and the humanistic values which constitute the heritage that is still shared by our society. we must reject, commissioner, the reductive tendency to regard immigrant men and women as mere labour from a purely economic point of view. this argument must be rejected in the strongest possible terms: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to promote the development of the european audiovisual industry, and has recently launched a new initiative to promote the development of audiovisual products.
mr president, the moraes report on the commission communication on integration and employment is an example of how good policy can never be built on the basis of good intentions alone: we learned it from a certain machiavelli. in this case, the good intentions are to advance the integration lines defined by the tampere european council. the report thus contains declarations of intent which are certainly common sense, for example by pointing out that the still too widespread practice of uncontrolled, undeclared work by illegal immigrants is an objective incentive to illegal immigration. however, it is also stated that the current political climate in relation to asylum and illegal immigration would be - think about it - a possible obstacle to the establishment of cooperation in the field of immigration management and integration policies. this is a clear contradiction, which can only be explained by a very ideologized view of the migration problem, of which we have had some rather obvious examples in the speeches that preceded me. this view can only lead to statements that have nothing objective to do with reality, when for example it is assumed that the opposition to illegal immigration/human trafficking translates into a repressive policy against immigrants. it is this unsecular, ideologised, misleading vision which can distort the increasingly clear and defined objectives which have recently been identified and pursued by the european union, especially during the italian presidency. it is not possible to share a vision of civil citizenship with administrative and even european voting rights, through which the european union should somehow replace individual member countries in the recognition of one of the rights - the right to an active electorate - on which, in our view, in a true democracy, only the people, and not the bureaucratic and technocratic, globalist, european superpower, can decide.
mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr moraes, for his availability and cooperation, and i would also like to thank him on behalf of my colleague, mr mario mantovani, who would have liked to be here and speak but was urgently called back to italy. the dynamics of immigration in the union are part of an overall framework which takes account of the benefits it brings to all stakeholders. the irish presidency also mentioned this this morning. migration flows must be managed in such a way as to serve both the interests of the host country and the country of origin. the management of these flows can in fact be a response - as has already been said - to the democratic and economic evolution of the union. the integration of immigrants - legal immigrants - who are a resource must be improved, but immigrant populations must respect the fundamental values of the host country. the proposal is to guarantee the right to vote in local and european elections to third-country nationals legally resident in the union. it is clear to everyone that such a question must remain within the strictest subsidiarity. i would like to thank the rapporteur in particular for the compromise reached with the governments of the member states in establishing a system of quotas for legal entry into their own countries, and thus also into europe, to be offered to the countries of origin and transit as one of the main reasons for negotiation and agreement on readmission. the question is very important to achieve this kind of cooperation. in any case, it is clear that this complex problem, together with the planning of the policy of managing migration flows, must be brought as soon as possible to the european level. the rights of immigrants must be recognized, but they must also be accompanied by a policy of regulating flows which cannot but be rigorous. finally, italy is the second largest european country in terms of the number of immigrants and the only country in europe with a very long border line to the east and south. the whole of europe must take account of this very strong migratory and social pressure, its weight and its wealth, because the real problem arises in italy but, immediately and automatically, it is transferred to all the other community countries.
mr president, i too welcome the attention with which this parliament and the commission have begun to examine a subject which is now of interest to all the countries of the union. emigration has always been a necessity, often a hope, rarely a choice. i say this from a country that has lived with the problem of immigration for many, many decades. in recent years, the issue of migration to many european countries has taken on a bitter and often dramatic connotation. i think it is necessary and useful for the various member states to adopt a common position on this subject, because i cannot imagine any positive scenarios unless all the countries of the union adopt the same rules. but i also think that this is one of the most delicate issues, one of those issues that must be governed, perhaps more than others. and to govern them means to deal with the issues of visas, employment, health, the rights and duties of citizens, citizenship. i believe that the right to vote, also at local level and not only at european level, must be a consequence of these rights and not a precondition, as has sometimes seemed to emerge in some debates in recent years. the anxiety about integration must not make us forget that our first duty, in my view, is to create the conditions for support in developing countries so that everyone can continue to live in his or her country of origin. our second duty is to encourage the return of immigrants to their countries of origin as instruments of development and growth for their own countries. the third point is that we must see migration as a matter of national interest for the old europe, which is impoverished by low birth rates and a shortage of workers. this being so, there is no doubt that we must have control mechanisms to enable us to combat illegal immigration and, in the member states, disregard for the rules of the host country.
mr president, this report is timely and very much awaited in preparing the strategies in line with the thessaloniki european council on the development of a common policy on illegal immigration, smuggling and trafficking in human beings, external borders and the return of persons staying illegally. the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has adopted a position on illegal immigration policy by approving this own-initiative report by mr pirker, and i congratulate him on the results he has achieved. we are all convinced that we support the creation of a european border guard corps, financed at community level, for the creation of which a new legal basis would be required. in proposing this solution, the proposal to set up an autonomous agency for border controls is automatically rendered obsolete. it is also a shared view that the community's borders must remain open to people fleeing forms of repression and therefore applying for asylum in the member states of the union. all are also agreed that it is the exclusive right of the member states, as a consequence of subsidiarity, to establish qualitative characteristics and definitions of legal immigration, but a common policy on social integration is needed, which is now an indispensable problem in an area of security, common policy, social justice and economic justice. finally, it is right to note that the debate in the committee on civil liberties has led to the conclusion that bilateral readmission agreements between member states and countries of origin and transit should, if possible, be replaced by genuine community agreements, possibly recognised by all member states without exception, in short, without opting out. finally, in this common area of european engagement on immigration, i would like to mention - as the rapporteur also did at the end of his speech - an initiative which has complementary value in this effort to combat illegality. the regulation, which entered into force on 1 january this year - a few days ago - provides eur 250 million over five years to finance third countries' efforts to combat illegal immigration in the places where irregular flows originate. the aim of the initiative is to inform, train and help people intending to emigrate to fully understand the value of legal immigration and all the risks of illegal immigration.
mr president, mr pirker's report, which i welcome, clearly shows the need to finally establish a common european policy against illegal immigration and human trafficking. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. an effective policy of coordination, the harmonisation of certain procedures and a timely exchange of information would make it possible to combat illegal immigration and many other related criminal phenomena more effectively: the european union has a long history of supporting the fight against drugs and human trafficking. it is worth remembering that an uncompromising policy to combat illegal immigration would not only give european citizens greater confidence and security, but would also allow the full realisation of the rights and opportunities for all legal immigrants. it is worth stressing, however, that the european union's external relations with all those non-community countries which wish to cooperate effectively in the fight against illegal immigration must be strengthened, and the so-called preferential quota policy must also be encouraged for those countries. finally, i do not think it is rhetorical to say that we must make every effort to pursue a convinced policy of cooperation and development, which will help to contain immigration more generally, to curb the flight of people and therefore of the labour force, but also the so-called brain drain from the countries of origin, precisely in order to avoid not only human impoverishment but also the condemnation of the developing countries to a certain and irreversible economic and social decline.

- the next item is the report (a5-0470/2003) by mr zappalà, on behalf of the committee on legal affairs and the internal market, on the proposal for a european parliament and council directive on the recognition of professional qualifications (com (2002) 119 - c5-0113/2002 - 2002/0061 (cod)).
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as rapporteur for the european parliament, i have the duty to thank the commission and commissioner bolkestein for having initiated a legislative proposal of this importance. there are tens of millions of professionals in the union. they regulate and direct the daily life of all citizens. the professionals are the most important productive social category in existence. their work is the sum of culture, training, experience, innate creativity, a spirit of assertiveness, a capacity for confrontation and a sense of responsibility. it is the social category that needs to be cared for and safeguarded most, and that needs certain rules to be respected most. each state shall organise, in its own legitimate autonomy and as a consequence of its social and cultural history, its own training courses and its own access to the various professions. the union must ensure free movement and freedom of establishment, equal rights of access to employment, competition, safety, health and freedom, in the full realisation of the internal market with equal guarantees and certainties for both professionals and consumers. the daily reality is that there are profound differences within national systems and striking differences between states. the complexity of the subject and the different incidence of the various professional activities, partisan interests, errors of political assessment generate overall confusion and guerrilla warfare within the political and professional class. the latter must learn to pursue a common purpose in its own specific interest and that of the consumers who depend on it in their daily lives. the political world must abandon the superficial and partial view of the problem and must put the whole sector in order. the approach of certain political forces, of certain party representatives, the ignorance coupled with the arrogance of those who confuse art with themselves and others are damaging to the professional world and the community. i would ask the commission, which i thank again for having drawn up the proposal, to consider parliament's amendments not as a counter-proposal but as an exercise of the legitimate power and duty of the legislator, in the exercise of an active and democratically expressed representation, and i therefore ask for all possible cooperation in the interests of the union. the professional is an atypical service provider precisely because he produces more with his intellect than with specific means. he provides services which are not equal and often not comparable, and the principle of competition cannot be applied to the sector with ease, precisely because, in addition to price, professional performance is above all the fruit of intellectual creativity which is rooted both in basic culture and in the liveliness of the brain. professions and intellectual professions are not the same; the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. access to certain professional activities is regulated by the states in accordance with local history and culture, but also in accordance with minimum consumer liability and safety guarantees. harmonizing is not the same as photocopying. the british system should not be exported but harmonized with the other systems to achieve automation and certainty within the union. no one represents everyone, only the legislator has overall responsibility. we have a duty to legislate in the interests not of some arrogant lobbyists but in the general interest: listening to everyone and deciding for oneself is the first duty of those who have to work for the collectivity. the recognition of five levels of qualifications, initial continuing training, the free movement of professions and not pieces of paper; consultation of the professions; the platforms proposed by recognised organisations; compliance with authorised national structures, whether orders, colleges or associations; definitive pro forma registration; equality of rights and duties; legal certainty; the elimination of bureaucratic loopholes; mutual recognition of the professions used in individual states, including, where necessary, at higher levels of the corresponding qualifications; compliance with insurance, social security and trade union rules are the qualifying arguments of the proposal. any position to the contrary is partisan rhetoric and a desire to prevent progress in the internal market and in such an important sector. any position to the contrary risks appearing as a flattening towards party lobbies that do not operate in the collective interest. the directive is important regardless of the individual content. i don't put up barriers; the whole is more important than the detail. i hope that everyone will see themselves in a european perspective and not in lobbyist positions.
the committee on petitions has already adopted a number of amendments. mr president, the european commission has repeatedly stressed the need to create a 'europe of knowledge' serving the european citizen's market, a 'europe based on dynamic knowledge, more competitive but also promoting quality basic education and training, especially by guaranteeing the mutual recognition of qualifications and professional qualifications as a concrete and tangible proof of european citizenship. this requires the elimination of all obstacles to the free movement of citizens, the mobility of workers and the professions in the european single market. however, this has led to some major difficulties which i will list without wishing to be exhaustive: the lack of a genuine spirit of cooperation, help and understanding; the complexity of existing national legislation; the reluctance of national authorities; the preference given to the criteria of training and apprenticeship over the examination to verify the real equivalence of training for the purposes of the recognition of diplomas; the absence of academic recognition of diplomas and other courses of study. the report by mr zappalà, which i approve and which i consider to be very balanced, has dealt with this question in a constructive and pragmatic way in the context of an increasingly united and united union, in which the european citizen will play a leading role, particularly the professionally qualified citizen. however, as i have said, there are still some situations and problems which need to be resolved and, as a committee on petitions and personally, i believe that, beyond any declaration of principle, there is a real need - as has been done here, for example, for consultation - to create effective instruments of control and promotion for the transposition of the various community provisions on the legal systems of the member states, to optimise the community system itself for the recognition of professional qualifications and academic qualifications, with the aim of making it clearer, faster and more accessible to the beneficiaries. in addition, greater attention should be paid to new information technologies, to make people aware of the need for lifelong learning. the committee on petitions has received a very large number of petitions on this subject, and this is a testimony to the fact that this situation must be tackled immediately if europe is to be truly open to culture, to the professions and to the citizens of europe.
mr president, i am speaking without having heard the debate because i have been busy in the bureau. i have been leading the committee's work for a long time and i can say that the subject has been thoroughly and deeply studied and has played the role that such an important measure should have played, one of the most important measures that parliament is dealing with. we started with a hearing which lasted a whole day and brought to our attention the problems which were discussed in depth by the rapporteur and the other members of the committee - mr zappalà, mr lehne and others, whom i thank - and through various debates and speeches we have come to the conclusion of today. we have a great ambition: we want to regulate the intellectual profession. i believe that we are actually opening up europe to a sense of citizenship and cultural organisation, because europe is made through the single currency and through all the initiatives we are taking forward, but europe is made, above all, by giving citizens an awareness of an intercommunication which concerns, precisely, the intellectual professions: the possibility of practising at a higher level in the various states and organisations within europe. mr zappalà has brought this directive forward by finding a balance - i must say - different from that proposed by the commission, a balance closer to the interests of the professional world and of the public, who are concerned above all with the quality of information. we need to qualify the professions at a higher level so that, within the states, the professional is accepted precisely for the qualification he possesses. i believe that by making this contribution we are making a great contribution to europe and a service to the citizens. i also believe that the intercommunication between the various european countries is determined at this level: the intellectual professions have always been in history - not only in italy but, i believe, in all countries - a strong point of reference to qualify a country. finally, i believe that the liberal profession, once it has a uniform definition within europe, can make a contribution that can perhaps only be surpassed by the constitution, once ratified. it is, however, an important contribution to the constitution and to european citizenship.
