mr president, my colleague medina ortega said: collective management companies are not monopolies; they are unions which serve to protect a weaker party against a stronger party. this is not always the case, and i think it should be borne in mind, as the rapporteur does in her report, that a trade union is a trade union, whereas a monopoly trade union, to which rights and duties are bound by law, is a monopoly. i do not think that the situation of monopolies, whether in law or in fact, of collective management companies is healthy because it exposes them to the risk of abuse, even serious abuse, for example, of amateur artists. i don't know the situation in the rest of europe; i know that in italy the amount owed by amateur artists to collective management societies for copyright is a real tax, which is excessive and which benefits, instead, the great organized artists. so a monopoly union is not a good union because i believe a good union is as free as possible. the rapporteur therefore says that we must be careful to ensure that monopoly situations, whether de jure or de facto, do not result, as is often the case in italy, and i believe also in the european union, in unnecessary abuse and taxes for amateur artists. if one adds to this the fact that, for example, copyright exemptions for disabled persons are only available to associations of disabled persons and not to individuals, there is a risk of corporatising copyright relations and the end user does not benefit from such situations. the rapporteur deserves our congratulations for her work.
three brief remarks, mr president: the aim of constitutional reform is to make the european union more democratic and more effective, not to calculate the balance of power between member states. the intergovernmental conference has been stuck on this point, and it is clear that the decisionmaking method which allows the veto is the real cancer of the european union, the one which kills all forms of community solidarity. for most of my group, the text adopted by the convention was or is a step forward, but certainly not a revolution. i am sure that there is a fairly large majority in parliament on this, but it seems to me that there is a fundamental ambiguity in the words of the presidency and in our debate. we are all optimistic and hopeful, we encourage the irish presidency to do its best, but what are we encouraging it to do? we even refrain from any parliamentary initiative which might disturb the presidency, we refrain from celebrating the 20th anniversary of spinelli's formal adoption of the convention. again, what are we encouraging her to do? it's not possible! there is an ambiguity, there is a contradiction! it seems to me that we cannot simply talk about the glass half empty and the glass half full. i would like to hear from the irish presidency about the five or six points on which agreement was reached in naples. i want to know what you're going to do, what you're going to try to settle on: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. i understood from what you said at the beginning of your presidency that the 82 points did not exist. so , what 's up ? mr president, the resolution proposed by the committee on constitutional affairs indicates 1 may as the date for the successful conclusion of the igc's work. i want to explain why. the constitution must be defined before the election campaign begins, first of all to encourage voters to go to the polls - otherwise we will be disappointed and abstain - and then to submit our fundamental law to their judgment, as is our democratic duty. i would add, for the sake of clarity, and in line with what mr duff and mr frassoni have said, that for us there is only the draft convention on the table. the 82 points mentioned by the president of the italian council, or the 45 amendments i am hearing about now, have been carefully examined by us: there are many elements of confusion, ambiguity and backwardness. there is no consensus on these matters - at least not on the part of the european parliament - and i ask you, mr president, to take them seriously. mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, commissioners, most of the members have spoken of the beautiful europe, or the europe which should become a little more beautiful. i will speak to you about the cowardly, vile, ignoble europe, the europe that mr de villepin spoke of in moscow two days ago, the europe that says nothing in the face of open war in chechnya. ten days ago, tired of this silence, i began a hunger strike, not to ask for the moon, not out of a mortiferous spirit or anorexia, as my colleague nicholson of winterbourne said. i think it is our europe that is deadly, the europe that does nothing in the face of chechnya. mr president-in-office of the council, i would like to ask you and the commission one small thing. we have an article 14 (3) of the ec treaty which allows the european union to make blacklists of personalities who cannot enter the territory of the union. but it also makes it possible to draw up white lists, i.e. lists which would allow personalities, in this case the chechens, to move and reside in the territory of the union to assert the reasons of their people, the reasons of a "genocided" people. mr president-in-office, if, during your presidency, you could, with the help of mr vitorino, get this reform adopted, i would be grateful.
mr president, commissioner, i should like to begin by making a general comment. i am addressing myself to you, president cox: this is the first time in 25 years that the european parliament has not been able to discuss the annual report on competition policy, which, in an area that is increasingly central to european policy, is one of the most important documents published by the commission. in recent years, the debate before the european parliament on the annual report has always been the most important - if not the only - forum for democratic scrutiny of competition policy, which, although having an increasing impact on the lives of european citizens and businesses, is one of the matters on which the parliament has only an advisory role. mr president, we have repeatedly asked - and we have done so during the work of the convention - for the european parliament to take part in the decisionmaking process on competition policy. for the first time in 25 years we have not been able to hold a debate on the annual report presented by the european commission. it is only because of this cover - because that is what it is - and the availability of commissioner monti that we have a debate today which is improperly trying to substitute for what should have been, in a clear and precise way, the debate by parliament on the broad lines of the commission's competition policy. i believe that the conference of presidents has taken on a serious responsibility: there was a misunderstanding that this report - for some reason - was no longer to be considered as compulsory but as an own-initiative report. the conference of presidents did not want to remedy the situation that had arisen, and so today we find ourselves with a debate that is half-hearted, with a parliament that is half-hearted precisely on competition policy. i think this is a very serious matter which should be reported and stigmatised in this house. i would like to make three points on the substance of this resolution, mr president, mr commissioner, which in fact transfers to the plenary some of the report for which i was appointed rapporteur, which was adopted in committee. first of all, this report welcomes the commitment that commissioner monti and the commission have made to implementing a major programme of legislative and organisational reforms of the directorate-general for competition. last week the council gave the final go-ahead to the new merger control regulation: as rapporteur for parliament's opinion on this subject, i can only regret that the amendments which parliament had suggested, and which i consider important, have not been adopted, particularly as regards the automatic transfer to brussels of some of the most important mergers and concentrations and as regards the powers. two further highlights, the first of which concerns the national samples. i agree with what my colleague from the green group said a moment ago: in europe, the process of liberalisation of crucial sectors such as energy is affected by the policies of the national champions. we have national champions in certain sectors - not only energy, but also telecommunications - which are often still national champions in public ownership, where the state is playing a double role, as controller and as direct actor, direct manager of companies. in my view, however difficult this may be - we are dealing with the big countries of the european union, very large and very powerful companies in germany, france and italy - the commission must keep a high guard and, if possible, try to achieve more positive results than those achieved so far. another point is that of the professions: the european commission has launched a new initiative to promote the development of the european social model. in many countries, growth in this sector is stifled by persistent corporate constraints, which disadvantage consumers and benefit professionals. finally, a more general comment on competition policy, also in relation to the amendments which have been tabled to the resolution we are voting on tomorrow. i believe that commissioner monti's policy has one single, crucial, very important objective: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure fair competition, to ensure competition in open markets for all european companies. it would be a grave mistake and an illusion to think that the choices made by commissioner monti should directly affect specific social or employment objectives. the best way to ensure jobs, growth and resources for social interventions is to ensure economic growth and competitiveness in europe.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i do not hesitate to call the adoption of this single sky regulation a historic milestone, which is the result of the work of myself and my colleague mrs sanders-ten holte, as well as of the work of many other members who have contributed over the past two years, in making it a fundamental commitment for our parliament. i think it is important because it allows us to extend our philosophy, our challenge for european integration, to our own skies, and it allows us to achieve two fundamental objectives: the first, immediate, is the construction of an integrated airspace, with the same rules, the same guarantees; secondly, it also allows us to revise the concept of sovereignty to make it more fully compatible with the process of european integration in which we are investing our passion and resources. the first objective that this regulation has certainly achieved is that of safety. a list compiled a few days ago lists the last 18 air accidents, the last 18 major air disasters that have occurred since september 11, 2001: in all, we counted 2,300 deaths. none of these disasters are linked to acts of terrorism; are air accidents related to problems concerning flight safety. well, in that sense our rules of procedure make a fundamental contribution. if air traffic continues to grow at the same rate as it has in recent years, and if we do not take action on safety, as we are trying to do with this regulation, then in 2015 - the forecasts say - we should have a disaster of this magnitude every week. the single sky is a fundamental contribution, one which it also seeks to recover in terms of organisation, efficiency and common sense. today there are 41 different air traffic control centres in europe, each often with its own rules, its own routes, its own corridors, its own flight quotas. from rome to brussels, he took over seven different flight centres, and the transitions are not always smooth: i think that the commission should be able to take the necessary steps to ensure that the european parliament is not left in the lurch. this regulation puts security back on the agenda of european policies. another priority which we believe the regulation has achieved is that of cost and time. the growth in european traffic will take us from seven million flights in 1997 to twelve million flights by 2010, and today one in five flights is delayed by more than 20 minutes. it is clear that efficiency in terms of time and costs makes our companies competitive in the market: the united states is about the same size as europe, but it has twice as many planes in the air and far fewer delays. how have we tried to address these points, these issues? we have tried to do so thanks to the passionate and conscious contribution of the commission, in the person of commissioner de palacio, and the good sense shown by the council at this difficult stage of the conciliation. there are three points i want to make quickly: the first of these concerns, of course, cooperation between the civilian and military sectors. in the compromise which was finally reached, it was decided to make an explicit reference to the declaration of the member states on military matters. in this declaration the member states acknowledge that the safe and efficient use of airspace can only be achieved through close cooperation between civil and military authorities. we consider it a milestone on the road to european integration: for the first time, the military hierarchies of all 15 european countries are realizing that the skies of europe are not the property of anyone and that a balance must be found - the highest possible quality - between the defence needs of all the member states and the security needs of the users, the citizens of europe. the second important point is the role of eurocontrol. we have tried to take on and to enhance, in terms of experience and efficiency, what eurocontrol represents and can represent in the construction of this single integrated european area. and to achieve this goal, cooperation was needed and no duplication of roles and functions. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. it has been agreed that eurocontrol will be able to attend the meetings of the single sky committee as an observer; we are therefore moving towards a phase of strong synthesis in the contribution that eurocontrol can make to us. finally, the sanctions were provided for, at the request of parliament, on the basis of a basic principle taught by beccaria: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. mr president, i believe that the merit of this regulation - and of the european institutions which have helped to bring it about - is that it has once again overcome certain national selfishness and prudence. a single european sky from 1 january 2005 will be a clear confirmation that europe is growing, that the process of integration is growing, that this process is also synonymous with quality and security for our lives.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to use these two minutes to commend the work of the rapporteurs, who have been so tenacious and so present on a complex and nuanced subject, but who have never abandoned or lost sight of the underlying objective; i would also like to congratulate all of us in the committee who believed in their work and supported it, and i would also like to congratulate the commission which supported us and also enabled all of us to reach a result for which i am very pleased. it is a satisfaction, ladies and gentlemen, which is not merely technical. some have already said that we could certainly have done more, we could have had more, we could have pushed this level of harmonization further, but i would invite you to reflect on the satisfaction we must have from a political point of view: we have succeeded in breaking a taboo, because from now on we will speak of a single sky in the presence of a provision, a single sky horizon. of course, not yet to the level we want and hope for, but still with a reality that we have used. the satisfaction is even greater because this measure is part of a framework that has already seen other measures: i would remind you that the council has approved the report on denied boarding and that - i very much hope it will be approved - which mrs maes is following on aircraft safety. i believe that we must also be satisfied because this is the europe that the citizens understand best, the europe that they feel close to, because it is the europe that increases their security, increases their rights, increases the satisfaction of meeting the needs they express. i believe that the citizens will be our main allies when, on this subject too, we want to go ahead and achieve those results which are not within our reach today but which we can certainly achieve in the coming years.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i too would like to express my full and unconditional satisfaction with the outcome of the conciliation committee. i think that the positive prospects which have been set out here certainly outweigh the perplexities which the debate has had in previous years in committee, in the council and in the commission, and which have also been expressed today. what i would like to stress - and here i agree with what my predecessors have said in their speeches - is the political aspect and the emphasis on the historic nature of this agreement: we must not forget that this is the same emphasis with which we stressed the abolition of land frontiers ten years ago, which seemed a miracle at the time and which gave the impetus to true european integration. the agreement reached is undoubtedly a balanced compromise in the face of the many difficulties and selfishness of the various sectors which had rightly established their interests and their competences over time. the result is therefore a result that is due to all of us, but i would also like to express my satisfaction with you, madam commissioner, for your tenacious efforts to achieve this objective together with the rapporteur, the shadow rapporteur and, as far as we are concerned, also the members of the committee. the result is satisfactory: the new role of eurocontrol and military and civil cooperation have been highlighted, but i would also like to stress the interoperability of the european air traffic management network and the no less important issue that you, madam commissioner, have highlighted, namely the certification of service providers. the same positive point - and i would like to say that i agree with it - was made by the rapporteur, mr fava, and it concerns the sanctions against service providers and airlines which violate the rules of the single sky, in order to provide safety objectives not only for our european citizens but for all users, for all those who fly in european skies.
mr president, we certainly share the rapporteur's view on the satellite system and a civil satellite system, but i think we should not be under any illusions about the possibility of clearly separating civil and military uses, especially the military implications of civil technologies. and now, less than ever, we can delude ourselves that there is a separation between civilian and military uses in dictatorial countries like china, which are making exactly civilian tools for locating people into tools for widespread repression, which is made possible by new technologies, especially new satellite technologies. that is why i think the report should have expressed greater concern on this point. it welcomed the conclusion of negotiations with china on the participation of that country in the galileo system. in fact, the military risks are not abstract risks, but risks which the european commission itself has identified as useful from a european point of view for its own defence. i would like to quote a document - a commission position paper - of 31 december 2001, which stated: if the galileo program is abandoned, we will in the next twenty or thirty years lose our autonomy in defence; and again, on 12 march 2002: although designed for civilian applications galileo will also give the eu a military capability, and the directorate-general indicated the usefulness of a separate galileo signal for supporting the military market. well, considering the same definition of the european commission's directorate-general, as far as our technology is concerned, it is good to know that this also applies to china. the problem is not to sabotage the galileo system; the problem is to take these risks explicitly into account and to take appropriate measures - through regulations, procedures and possibly sanctions - to prevent these technological consequences, which are partly inevitable and physiological, from being extended beyond the acceptable. otherwise we should think that the speed and ease with which the agreement with china was reached is part of a wider political strategy of the european union. i apologize for my rambling and for calling into question the government of the rapporteur's country, but the attitude we have seen in recent days from chirac and de villepin towards the chinese communist regime is, i believe, a worrying sign of a very clear political and strategic direction. if adequate countermeasures are not taken, there is a real risk that the military of a dictatorial, undemocratic, illiberal country, which knows how to make military use of the secret services and spy on civilian structures, which our intelligence structures also do, will have very positive technological consequences in the coming years.
mr president, the importance of this directive has already been stressed by those who have gone before me, but in recent years it has been emphasized and taken into account in abundance precisely in order to achieve the result we have achieved. i shall not therefore dwell on the importance and content of the subject, which have already been the subject of many meetings and debates. i would just like to take this opportunity to make two quick remarks: the first is to emphasize that this directive comes into force at the very moment of enlargement, so that there will be more peace of mind, not only for the 15 states of today, but also for the 25 that will be part of our union in a few months' time. i welcome what commissioner bolkestein said about quality. i have tried many times as rapporteur to emphasize the problem of quality. i know that the commission is moving in this direction with other initiatives and so i hope that, in the award of contracts according to the principle of price/quality, quality is always the best part, especially in the performance of professionals. having spent four years as rapporteur on this subject with many other colleagues, i conclude by expressing my sincere thanks to all those who have been responsible for such an important achievement for the european union: the president of the conciliation committee, to whom i have devoted a great deal of my time, because she was very good in the committee in conducting the final part; mr president, on behalf of my group i would like to express my satisfaction with the work done by the committee on budgetary control and the rapporteur, whom i congratulate. i cannot, however, fail to stress that the picture emerging from the reading of the report is absolutely worrying: the image of a european commission closed in secrecy and lacking transparency, which does not communicate with the other institutions, is emerging. the commission is stratified by a bureaucratic complexity of functions, delegations and tasks, which in fact results in a lack of assumption of administrative and political responsibility, both by the directors-general and the commissioners. the example of eurostat, which has not yet been fully taken on board, is a good example in this respect. in fact, we have seen several times in recent months an attempt by the commission to close the case hastily, to dismiss it as if it were nothing, as if parliament's requests for clarification were in fact a great nuisance, as if the commission were a caste of untouchables above all, including parliamentarians and citizens. it is also surprising that president prodi, who is so used to strictly enforcing the rules - i am thinking, for example, of the appeal to the court of justice on the ecofin decisions on the stability pact - should have taken such a light-hearted approach to a scandal as odious as the one involving eurostat. i would like to conclude, mr president, by saying that, beyond the candid and vague statements of principle made by the commission - statements which we have heard again this evening - we are waiting for a strong signal, a rigorous reform which will really change the system.

- the next item is the oral question (b5-0003/2004) to the commission on european space policy.
mr president, i appreciated the timeliness and quality of the action plan for the implementation of a european space policy which was presented to us. this policy was revived in the days when the united states was redesigning its strategy and in the days when esa and nasa were recording extraordinary successes that excited the whole world. space policy is crucial for international research cooperation, for the lisbon strategy, to ensure europe's autonomous access to space. the plan we are discussing has a horizontal dimension, i.e. it covers many community policies, from agriculture to communications. after the galileo project, we are waiting for the global monitoring project for the environment and safety. i agree with the desire to pool investment resources to achieve goals that no country can achieve alone and to coordinate research, technological innovation and industrial policy more strongly. i would, however, draw the commissioner's attention to two points: the first concerns the impact on the european programme of the new strategy decided by the world's leading space power, with which we have deep ties of cooperation. the second concerns the contradiction between europe's ambitions and the resources needed to carry out the most important part of the space programme, and this seems to us to be the question that the white paper left open. the issues which shape this programme concern the constituent process as regards the powers for space and common defence, but also the political choices, both national and community, which are required to make the european economy more competitive.
commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the various points raised by this oral question demonstrate the growing interest of parliament in the establishment of a european space policy that is commensurate with the union's ambitions. i thank parliament for its unwavering support on these issues and would like to recall that it was thanks to this encouragement that the commission launched a wide-ranging debate in europe on the future of the space sector, in which some of the members of parliament participated directly. following this debate, the commission adopted a white paper, some of whose important actions are discussed in your question. to develop space technology, the commission proposes to increase public spending in this area, after drawing up a master plan presenting all the missing technologies, the actors involved and a timetable of measures. the 6th framework programme can already contribute to this, in particular through the thematic priorities 'aerospace and space' and information society technologies. the commission will examine the possibilities of pursuing this form of development in the next framework programme. as regards access to space, the commission, in close cooperation with the european space agency, is supporting the launch of a number of measures, including the improvement of launch service offerings from the guiana space centre. this action has already been selected as a project under the 'quickstart' growth initiative and was presented to the last european council and adopted. work on defining the financial package has begun in liaison with the european investment bank. then a multilateral discussion with the other space powers was launched. this discussion should lead to a proposal for a regulatory framework acceptable at international level. finally, the commission is considering direct intervention in the maintenance of basic terrestrial infrastructure as part of the future european space programme. in the case of galileo, the possible role of the private sector should be that of final operator, following a selection procedure conducted by the galileo joint undertaking. the commission will also start bilateral negotiations with india. and a framework agreement has just been signed between the european community and china. the commission is expected to adopt in the coming days, i.e. on 3 february, a communication based on a detailed report prepared with the european space agency and the member states, drawing lessons from the initial phase and presenting an action plan for the global monitoring for environment and security (gms) initiative. for the launch of the next deployment phase, the commission proposes the gradual establishment of a formal coordination structure which could draw on the experience of galileo. the envisat satellite is admired by everyone for its ability to monitor the environment and is a wonderful example of earth observation, for which we are at the forefront of development. the establishment of this structure for gms will be preceded by a lighter and interim solution, in accordance with the provisions of the european community/space agency framework agreement. i would like to remind you of the importance of parliament's opinion in order to conclude this agreement between the european community and asean. the commission is also developing a policy on access to space and launchers, for which the installation of the soyuz launcher at the guyanese space centre is a concrete example of strategic collaboration between russia and europe. the final decision on this programme is expected to be taken at the european space agency council on 4 february. the commission suggests in the white paper that the union should provide for the maintenance of launch facilities and the financing of work and research to develop a future space transport system. finally, in the white paper, the commission suggests that activities related to the international space station should be continued, as this would improve europeans' experience of extended stays in space. in this connection, i was pleased to hear mr savary's suggestion, which of course concerned the whole of europe and proposed that astronauts from the future acceding states should be associated. the international station can also be seen as an indispensable intermediate step before embarking on future exploration of the solar system. you referred to president bush's statement. this calls for a thorough analysis. it has, of course, implications for us, given the investments made by the european side, particularly in keeping the international space station accessible to european astronauts. so we should not sell the whole process without having the assurance that this process, in which we are strongly committed, will be maintained. you also know that -- and this brings up a thought -- the european satellite that observes mars has had some remarkable results, because you've seen these pictures that show the possibility of ice on the moon. it was european technology that showed this, although, unfortunately, the beagle did not give the same result. on the other hand, spectral analysis of mars is a european technology of the highest order, of which we can be proud. finally, from this, there is also the idea of seeing how we envisage manned flights, so that we do not remain outside the race towards these objectives. the commission, in collaboration with esa, will set up in 2004 a group of experts which should enable the european union to define its position on manned flights. i have summarized the main points. i am very pleased to conclude by telling you that the commission is including space and space research in its reflection on the future financial perspectives which are at the heart of the commission's debate and which parliament will, i think, take the lead on 10 february. we shall give the first outline of the financial perspective. and in the context of the financial perspective, it is clear that research and innovation programmes must have increased resources, inter alia to support a space policy which is crucial for technological progress and europe's place in the world.
- thank you, lieutenant. i have received a motion for a resolution pursuant to rule 42 (5) of the rules of procedure. see the minutes. this meeting is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow at 10.30 a.m.
- the next item is the oral question (b5-0004/2004) to the commission on the clothing sector.
the commission shares the concerns about this issue and is taking the necessary steps to respond to them. in view of these major challenges in the textile and clothing sector which will persist in the years ahead - and in particular, as you have stressed, the impact of the elimination of import quotas on 1 january 2005 - the commission has reviewed its policies and instruments with a view to identifying measures or lines of action which could strengthen the competitiveness of the textile and clothing sector. these are reflected in the communication you have highlighted, the communication on the future of the textile and clothing sector adopted by the commission on 29 october 2003 and welcomed at a council meeting on competitiveness on 27 november 2003. as you know, the union's strategy for international trade and for doha aims at reciprocity for textiles and clothing. together, these policies and measures aim to provide industry and those working in the sector with a clear, predictable and coherent framework, allowing for easy planning of strategies and investments in the medium and long term. the commission welcomes the support for these initiatives and the ideas contained in the resolution on the future of the textile sector which parliament will shortly adopt. the commission will examine carefully the new ideas mentioned in the text of the resolution. some of the recommendations contained in the communication could be followed up by concrete proposals in a relatively short time. the commission has also taken the initiative of developing a new approach to the problem of the environment. in order to assist the commission in these investigations and in the follow-up of these issues, the commission will set up a high-level group with the task of formulating recommendations aimed at improving the conditions of competitiveness. the aim of this high-level group is to contribute to the process of implementing industrial and trade policies in the textile and clothing sector of the enlarged union. the group will participate in the monitoring of economic development of the sector at regular intervals, carried out by the commission services. it will provide reports and recommendations to the commission and other decision-makers on the proposed initiative, in order to facilitate the adjustment of the sector to the different challenges it faces. these reports should include recommendations on key issues affecting competition in the sector. this high-level group will be composed of commissioners, ministers, business leaders, and ceos or presidents of federations linked to the textile and clothing sector. it will deal with the following themes: innovation, research and development. in this respect, i was pleased that you emphasised the role of research and development. indeed, i recently had the opportunity to visit a textile company which has made a considerable effort in research and innovation and which has become competitive in the chinese market, whereas it was in a difficult situation a few years ago. with a much more efficient dye patent and with increased productivity management mechanisms, it has become competitive in a very specific "niche". in fact, the services say that exports to china increase when you have these new industrial capacities. so it is clear that there is a future when we develop this research and innovation in a field where we have know-how. another theme is the issue of intellectual property. mr berenguer fuster is quite right when he says that this question must of course be at the heart of our thinking at international level, because all these new processes can be fragile if we do not protect them with strong patents. research and development is certainly an important factor. we are also considering setting up a technological platform for the textile sector, with the main players. the other themes are education and training, regional policy. in this connection, mrs bastos, you have stressed the problem of the structural funds. my colleague mr barnier, when he proposes the new mechanisms for the structural funds, says very clearly that they will have to contribute, even more than in the past, to the growth process, the so-called lisbon process. research and innovation, training in sectors such as textiles, will be more prominent and stronger subjects in the next structural funds, if the regions wish it, of course. international industrial cooperation, labelling and other trade-related issues can all have an effect on the impact. this high-level group will start in february 2004, i.e. practically tomorrow, addressing in particular the objectives and expected results, and will be followed by at least two plenary sessions before july 2004. the commission will present a first report on the work of the group to the council in july 2004. i think that the problem is being taken into account. the european institutions and the member states will be kept informed of the work of the group and the implementation of the conclusions. the commission is aware of the need to move fast, since the aim is to implement all this before the end of 2004. by the end of february, a scoreboard will be drawn up by the commission, indicating the various proposals, measures and actions in trade matters resulting from the communication, and giving a timetable for their implementation. this scoreboard will be shared with parliament and the member states. you also raised the question of the budget to be used for the implementation of the measures proposed in the commission's communication. we intend to use existing community programmes and investments to implement the concrete measures adopted, as we need to move quickly. each specific measure will be implemented by the appropriate commission services responsible for the field concerned and using its own budget. i can tell you that in the field of research, in the calls for proposals of the framework programme, there is potential for interesting projects, and i believe that this is also true in other sectors. i think i have shown the commission and parliament's interest in this strategic sector of our economy.

- mr president, we are pleased that this report comes at the very time when kofi annan is speaking to us in the next few hours. i think it is important to state in the report before us the importance and the role we want to see attached to the united nations, which everyone is now calling for an intervention, an initiative, an active role in iraq - whereas for months this role has been blocked by two parties who were in some way very afraid of the role of the un and who thought that it might not be as effective as it should be - so that the exit from iraq does not turn into a nightmare, as it sometimes has been in the past. we are particularly pleased that the report mentions an important initiative which could be led by the union. i am referring to the idea that within the united nations - whose difficulties and burdens we have seen when, last year, a person from a country such as libya, which is certainly not at the forefront of human rights, was appointed as head of the commission on human rights - the democracies of the un countries can come together and find each other, and that they can devise common actions in the field of promoting democracy. this is an initiative in which the european union could play an important role in the united nations. at least that is what the italian radicals are advocating. they urge commissioner patten to urge the member states that, already at the next meeting of the commission on human rights in geneva, there can be a first, symbolic and concrete meeting of the community of democracies.
we must give strength and value to the un, we must give strength and value to the european union, a strength which should derive from its capacity to be a reference point for political, moral, ethical, economic and social practice. the un and the european union have risen from the ashes of the destruction of world war ii. the european union and the united nations should therefore be based on the rejection of war and the eradication of poverty. commissioner patten is right: not just peace, but peace with justice. unfortunately, however, peace and justice are not so central. the european constitution speaks of promoting peace, but does not take the rejection of war and the negotiated settlement of conflicts as central. in recent years the un has been degraded, if not only by the military policy of a fundamentalist president, the president of a country which still practices the death penalty in many of its states and rejects international bodies, not least the international criminal court. war has become legitimate again, militarism is permeating minds and hearts. i do not underestimate the important commitment of mr laschet and all the members of the committee on foreign affairs. e a clear message, alongside that of the sakharov prize to kofi annan, to give value and a role to the european union and the united nations; but it is essential that security for all, poverty reduction, justice and peace are real practices and not just empty claims. the un and the eu, together with other countries, must restore confidence and, above all, respect for international law.
mr president, i must thank mr laschet for this report, which is very inspired and has great prospects, but we must also build the intermediate path between the realisation of the prospects and the present situation. first of all, i have to say that i had a great experience of working with the united nations when i had contact with its deputy secretary-general for a report i made to the acp-eu joint parliamentary assembly on the protection of children's rights. i think that mr laschet's proposal represents the first stage of a reform which the un must undertake. after the logic of yalta, the logic of the blocs, has been overcome, we must affirm the growing multilateralism. we must promote new nations to world responsibility, without removing the instruments of memory for those who have promoted world peace; and the right of veto is one of those memory tools. the security council must be enlarged, and new permanent members without veto rights must be considered: the european union, australia, argentina, brazil, egypt, germany, japan, india, italy and south africa could be among these. the number of elected members of the security council must also be increased, and then the agencies must be reformed to make them more operational on the ground; the promotion of human rights and the affirmation of the principles of democracy must be accelerated. we must imagine that the united nations is aimed at the protection of persons, since the concept of 'nation' is invoked, not the concept of state, and the concept of nation presupposes the protection of the person. it is urgent to start this process of defending the individual because today terrorism is rampant: today it is al-qaeda and bin laden, tomorrow it could be something else. certainly a reformed nato could be the international police force the un might need. division, exclusion, they create war. the european union is a political union, but the philosophical concept of the union promotes important goals of peace.

mr president, commissioner, rapporteur, when we talk about support for the development of the business sector in cooperation policies with third countries, particularly developing countries, it is perhaps useful to recall what the peruvian economist hernando de soto says in his book the mystery of capital: one of the main obstacles to economic development, the missing ingredient, in countries that are actually said to have considerable wealth, is the absence of clearly defined property rights, and therefore protected with the certainty that is necessary to attribute to these rights a real economic value. it is therefore clear that the most valuable contribution that can come from the european institutions - and i would say, in general, the most important contribution of the developed countries - is to guarantee legal infrastructures that safeguard legal certainty, to support and accompany processes towards democratization and the recognition of the principles of the rule of law. the commission document only mentions this issue, which i consider to be central, and the report of the committee on development, unfortunately, omits this reference and focuses on the dramatic consequences of the policies promoted by the international monetary fund or the world bank. i do not think it is correct, even in practice, to blame the imf and the world bank for certain failures; i am thinking of the case of argentina, where it is becoming increasingly clear that the failure, the default even of the state itself, is attributable not to the imf or the world bank but to the level of corruption, to the level of statism present in that economy. it is said that one does not want to make ideology, but i fear that in reality one does. it's good to consider all the options; but i believe that the political option we have to offer is that of privatising companies to seek efficiency, investment. we want services to reach the population, and to reach them in quantity and quality to meet needs that are not being met today. i believe that asking corrupt and inefficient public companies to provide this service is wrong. the privatisation option - as in europe, so in the developing countries - is the politically desirable option.

mr president, i would like to begin by congratulating mr bowis on the professionalism, the determination and the timeliness with which he has completed this important report. i also congratulate the rapporteur on the efforts he has made in the negotiations with the council to reach an agreement at first reading. i believe it is of the utmost importance that the centre should be operational as soon as possible, in order to ensure that health surveillance and early warning system which the union must have in order to adequately meet the challenges posed by the forthcoming enlargement and by a globalised world, which is increasingly vulnerable to the spread of diseases that are still unknown to us: sars in 2003 and the very recent avian flu are concrete examples of this. i therefore consider that 1 january 2005 is the deadline for the launch of the centre. another point i would like to stress is the necessary task of updating and training all the health personnel in the countries of the union, which must be taken over by the centre. with the entry of ten new countries, the union will have to aim to provide all health systems with the tools, means and knowledge to generate effective synergy between all the member states and thus ensure the coherence of union action that the centre aims to achieve. i conclude by recalling that the ability to respond adequately to an international health threat depends to a large extent on the early adoption of preparatory plans for rapid, coordinated action at european level. the centre will have to provide this indispensable service.

mr president, on the economic front i can only congratulate mr garot on his detailed and precise report. the commission has also been asked to present a report on the implementation of the new financial perspective, which includes a number of themes and requests that we have made to the commission during the current legislature: for example, the establishment of supply chain contracts to raise the quality level, if necessary, but still to ensure the quality of the agricultural world; access to the european market for products from third countries subject to the same standards as european products; a crisis management system. in addition, it is necessary to reflect on the fact that the fall in agricultural prices has in fact had no effect on consumption. however, what i have said is purely theoretical, because - and all my colleagues in the committee on agriculture know my position on the proposal for a mid-term review - i believe that we have assumed a very great responsibility, the consequences of which will only be analysed in a few years' time, since the formula chosen for regulating markets and ensuring price stability - direct, fixed and uncoupled support for production - will lead to a progressive dismantling of the agricultural sector. we have made choices in a more foreign policy perspective than in cancun. we have seen the results in cancún and we have seen the resumption of negotiations in geneva. i believe, moreover, that some of the figures in the garot report - the 7% increase in agricultural prices from 1995 to 2002 - refer to historical prices and do not take account, among other things, of the inflationary figure which has a strong impact on agricultural prices. the overall situation of agriculture in the european union in 2011 is, in my view, much, much more delicate and with much more complex rules than what mr garot is presenting.
