mr president, i welcome the commission's proposal - even if it is very small, and i agree with those who have stressed this - not to go below what has been agreed, which is already a minimum minimum in relation to a europe which has grown, which will grow further and which has the right ambition to extend the scope of its political action beyond institutional boundaries to embrace a wider horizon. from this point of view, it is undoubtedly important to support such an approach. but, as others have pointed out, at the point where the european debate is now - so threatened by the tendency to want to do more and more, to want to 'marry the dry figs', as they say in italy - the commission should have gone a step further. the commission and its sapir report have shown us how much we really need to change our investment and budgetary priorities in order to be competitive and to give europe the hopes and objectives to which president prodi referred. but the sapir report has been kept in a drawer because it scares a lot of people, it scares a lot of governments, that they don't want to hear about some issues. well, when the commission has made a habit of touching the sensitive threads, it has done so with the stability pact. for example, there is this agreement which, while we are discussing the cap, we have until 2013 a commitment not to touch, which seems to be written in marble. well, that was supposed to be part of today's communication.
mr president of the commission, it is difficult not to share the scenario you have presented to us today. i have two doubts, which i'm sure you'll be able to resolve: firstly, i come from a long militancy in a party which should surely remind you of something, during which i was taught that too much realism in politics often means the beginning of a defeat. it seems to me that your request to stop the transfer of gdp at 1.24, i.e. at the present rate, which is still for 12 countries, when we are now 25 and will probably grow, is a position which lacks not only ambition but also courage, and this is on the assumption that there are two assessments: firstly, we have approved a public works intervention programme, the 'quick start' programme, which should relaunch the idea of europe and a single market, in which we need the involvement of the member states and resources, so i find the position of the six states which have asked for a reduction in the transfer very difficult. secondly, in any negotiation, to take a position knowing that some of the states are starting from the reference to 1%, seems to me to be a rather complex negotiating phase from a tactical point of view. the second doubt is about timing. the current financial situation is scheduled to end in 2006; the commission must submit a proposal by july 2005 and agreement must be reached by may 2006. so, what's the reason for the urgency? why are we speeding up in the face of a parliament whose mandate is about to expire? as mr wynn rightly pointed out, our parliament has a very important role to play, but it will be played by the parliament which will be elected on 13 june.
- mr president, there are strange things happening in the way we handle our issues. in december i asked a question about croatia. it was referred back from the january part-session to this part-session. i had a good faith belief that she had been sent back because mr verheugen should have been with us at that session and because other colleagues had also asked questions about croatia's accession. it's not! in addition, not only do i find my question at the end of the list and not at the top, which would have been logical since it was withdrawn in january, but i also know that it will not be possible to get an answer in the sitting. and i obviously couldn't ask another question for the month of february. i would like the services to record this fact and to put it in the minutes of this sitting, because i think that is really not very correct. there are many very shady things going on around the accession process of croatia. there are countries blocking this process, and i will name them: great britain and the netherlands. there is an urgent need for a debate to clarify all this.
commission. mr president, the european parliament scheduled this declaration from the commission only a few days ago when the future of thyssen krupp's ast terni plant was at stake. the decision to close magnetic steel production would put 450 jobs directly and larger numbers indirectly at risk. further job losses in the region already affected in the past would be a most unwelcome blow. it would be particularly unfortunate, in view of the long tradition of steel making in this region, the quality of the product produced at terni and the skill levels of the workforce involved. therefore it is with some relief that i heard reports today of an announcement by the italian minister of productive activities, mr marzano, that the deadline of the decision on the future of the ast plant has been postponed to a date yet to be defined. it is precisely because of concerns such as these, regarding the social consequences of corporate restructuring, that the commission has sought to promote a response at community level to the issue of how corporate restructuring is managed. we sought, with the active involvement of the social partners, to develop and promote issues that seem relevant in the present case, investing in people on a permanent basis, developing employability through training and retraining, seeking alternatives to closures and redundancies, redeploying, wherever possible, workers affected by restructuring operations and anticipating market and technological developments. i should like to remind you that two years ago the commission launched a consultation with the social partners, particularly on this issue of restructuring, because unfortunately, there were many problems in many different member states. the commission presented a report with some proposals. we have the response of the social partners in october and we believe that through dialogue and agreed measures, particularly on issues to which i have already referred, everything that supports employability, the social partners can play an important role. it is my sincere hope that in the window of opportunity that now appears to have opened all such possibilities will be given full consideration to see what contribution they can make to alleviating the crisis faced by the terni plant.
mr president, commissioner, europe cannot remain silent in the face of what is happening in one of the most important steel sites in the union: without prior notice, the thyssen krupp group decided to suspend the production of magnetic steel at its terni steelworks. this is an unjustified choice and a betrayal of an agreement signed in 1994 when the company was privatised. as you have mentioned, madam commissioner, the german company is preparing to lay off hundreds of workers, because it prefers to protect other european sites and invest outside the union, in south korea. thanks to the intervention of the italian government - you mentioned, commissioner, the intervention of the minister for productive activities, mr marzano - and the extraordinary mobilisation of all the local institutions, citizens, political parties and workers, thyssen krupp has decided to postpone the closure decision. the european parliament and the commission cannot, however, fail to raise their voices to help resolve a crisis which is not, mr president, commissioner, an italian problem, but one which affects the whole of europe. that is why we ask that the commission, when presenting the third report on economic and social cohesion on 18 february, clearly state its proposals for protecting the industrial sector, and in particular the steel sector, in the context of the new cohesion policy which will start on 1 january 2007. i am also convinced that the commission's action in the arms race with the united states on steel production market shares must also be supported. i would like to reiterate that the problem is not only italy's, nor is it a purely contingent matter, but that it is the general problem of steel in europe. the crisis does not affect just one company, but many, and i think the commission is paying the utmost attention to its relationship with the united states. we cannot, in fact, treat with carelessness and superficiality such a delicate subject, on which the fate of thousands and thousands of jobs and, consequently, of thousands and thousands of families is at stake. as regards the italian case of terni, i believe that the commission should take action on certain anomalies. today, the terni steelworks, contrary to the decision of the merger control commission in december 1994, are the exclusive property of thyssen krupp, and this is a clear example of a merger: the group is now the only producer in europe of grain-oriented magnetic steel. does the commission not consider that there are potential distortions of competition? moreover, thyssen krupp's italian operations have earned it community aid, since the area of terni, where the steelworks are located, is an industrial decline zone, which falls under objective 2. does the commission not therefore consider that it is necessary to verify whether community aid has been used correctly by thyssen krupp? does the commission not consider that it is necessary to examine whether thyssen krupp has received additional state aid in breach of the competition rules? the investments in south korea, announced yesterday, are causing us concern: i would not like european funds and any funds provided by the german government to be used not to grow the european economy or to create jobs and development in europe, but to be used in south korea, for the exclusive interest of a company which - i repeat - has not kept the agreements signed at the time of privatisation with the italian side. thousands of workers are now waiting for the commission to take action to ensure that eu rules are respected. i had the opportunity to listen to president prodi and commissioner monti a few days ago and i can see in your speech, madam commissioner, the will to go further, to protect the interests of workers, to solve a problem which, i repeat, is not a question of a territorial reality, of italy. i am sure that the president of the commission, after hearing the leaders of thyssen krupp, will take decisive action, as i hope the commission as a whole will do. the president of the commission and all the commissioners should continue on the path they have taken: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to support them.
mr president, for those of us who had the good fortune, like myself and other colleagues, to take part in the strike and demonstration in terni on friday 6 february, the deeper content of this struggle was very clear: the european union is a community of citizens, a city and a region, united and determined to reject not only a blow to their productive apparatus and their professional heritage, but above all an insult to their dignity. with so many years of union experience behind me, i had rarely felt such tension and pride. the announcement by the thyssen krupp group of the closure of the magnetic department of ast entails the elimination of 900 highly qualified jobs and the consequent alteration of the internal balance at the terno steelworks, and thereby the immediate threat to its structures, to production synergies, to the overall employment prospects: the total number of employees is about 4 500, including the industrial enterprises. but, i repeat, it is also a slap in the face to the regional local government which has been and is still working effectively, also through the structural funds, to create the most favourable production environment for the firm and its investments: the commission has also proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. everything was fine until yesterday; in september 2003, production of non-oriented wheat for electric motors was discontinued. on that occasion, and again in november, the set of programmes designed to consolidate terni as a magnetic pole of excellence was solemnly reaffirmed. but in late january, after just two months, the upside: the production of the processed wheat used for the processors is also closed, and the closure of the plant is announced, with some very heavy internal consequences: the threat to the integrity of the site, which, with its different productions, allows the balancing of market variations, the dispersion of strategic functions such as the commercial one, but also with important external effects, italian but european more generally. magnetite, produced only in terni, is the only special steel strategically important for the energy sector. the commission therefore concludes that the measures in question are not imputable to the state. the news from the meeting held today at palazzo chigi, however, gives reason to the mobilization of the workers and the city and their ability to expand the area of alliances and solidarity. on february 18th, he can finally start a real negotiation, without guns pointed at the union's head. the deadline of 27 february is skipped, the 80 expiring contracts that thyssen krupp wanted to cancel are renewed: the sign that the multinational felt isolated. however, in the face of this preliminary result, the pressure must be maintained and the attention of all the institutions must be kept on. this case, which is a true example of the need for new industrial relations systems and more advanced forms of linking public investment with business strategy, will be the subject of a resolution by parliament on thursday. we are fighting to send a clear message to the court that the case should be resolved in a positive way: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work.
mr president, in 1950 robert schuman launched the idea of a high authority for coal and steel open to all states, thus creating the conditions for a complex and difficult process of european unification, of which we are witnessing the most recent developments. the importance of steel in the european context was already clear. it is not surprising, therefore, that the terni case is very much a part of the debate in this parliament and that opinions on it are largely independent of political guidelines. today the european steel industry is suffering from the decision of the united states, which has imposed duties on steel imports mainly from the european union. the crisis in italy is relevant because it also affects other steel poles, such as the genoa-cornigliano steelworks. in this context, the declaration by thyssen krupp, the owner of the ternano steelworks, to invest in south korea in order to produce steel in asia is of primary importance and raises a number of questions. as is well known, the terni factory was sold in 1994 as part of a privatisation process promoted by iri, with the guarantee of keeping the activities in italy and safeguarding jobs. it is not a company in crisis but a state-of-the-art production facility used to make sheet metal for electronic transformers; furthermore, there are no other factories of the same type in italy. the technology developed at terni, the studies and research carried out in recent years have reached an excellent level. the loss of skills and know-how is the exact opposite of the knowledge economy that president prodi recently spoke about in this very parliament. it is no coincidence that, alongside the workers, managers, cadres, researchers and local politicians have also taken a stand in defence of production and studies related to it. the number of workers involved in the likely redundancies is about 900, not including the effects of the induction. we cannot ignore the fact that the german multinational received funds and tax concessions from the european union when it decided to buy the terni factory. i also wonder what it means, on the world stage, to move production to korea, what are the trade union rights and the technologies of workers employed in southeast asia and how they are respected. at a meeting last december, some european union manufacturers in the footwear and textile sectors made serious demands to this effect to the delegation from china, which is becoming the world's factory. the responses were very evasive and we have no way of thinking that anywhere else on the continent - except japan - the situation is better. especially in a market economy based on competition, rules must... (the president cut off the speaker)
mr president, 900 jobs lost may be just statistics, but they are lives of real people. in terni, the factory is the beating heart of the city's urban fabric. 900 jobs: stories of young and old workers. the steelworks in terni are the history and pride of the town, they are the memories of the bombings suffered in the second world war. the announcement by the management of thyssen krupp that the magnetic steel plant was to be closed saw the whole town - mr sacconi said - take to the streets with the workers. terni is not an isolated operation: in europe arcelor, lucchini and thyssen are preparing to reduce production; the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. massive divestments and the relocation of valuable production to non-european countries are in fact dismantling european production capacity. the community's considerable financial resources over the past decades have been used to dismantle the state-owned steel industry. the subsidies granted to private entrepreneurs, such as riva, lucchini, falk and certain foreign groups, were used to modernise the production capacities and specialisations of the italian steel industry in accordance with the logic of profit. the european social fund and the structural funds have financed restructuring and retraining of staff and have also enabled a generational renewal of the workforce in the sector. in italy today the average age of steelworkers is 30 years old. i believe that in the european union the lack of a coordinated plan to revive the steel industry has contributed to the relocation of individual plants in the various countries - even to the 'avant-garde' - to pursue industrial plans of individual entrepreneurs. the thyssen plant in terni, which is suffering heavy losses in the magnetic steel sector to the benefit of the russian duferco, is contracting production and preparing to move its operations to mexico, korea, china and india. there is no sign of innovation, new research, or a challenge to competitiveness. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's social policy is not undermined by the economic crisis. despite the drastic reduction in employment in italy in the sector, from 100 000 units in the 1980s to 40 000 units today, the steel sector maintains a very good position in the market, because it is the second largest european producer. but to maintain this, industrial choices by the italian government - which seem to be lacking - and a clear commitment to action at european level are needed. steel and electricity are economic indicators of absolute excellence in a country, an economic driver of development. it is therefore essential that the european union introduce systems to reduce pollutants and pursue environmental impact, which are priorities if the objectives of the kyoto protocol are to be achieved. on this basis a new steel industry can be revived. we must find solutions in the field of industrial policy to protect quality production, environmental and working conditions in the steel industry. in this sense, in order to counter threats to employment and the progressive social impoverishment, the commission must commit itself to a revision of the structural funds regulation. in future, the granting of such aid must be based on the requirement that the investment must be geographically limited for at least 10 years, with the explicit withdrawal of subsidies in the event of relocation of production. i also believe that a review of directive 98/59 on the protection of collective redundancies should be launched, which would strengthen the protection of employment by providing not only for procedures for informing workers' representatives but also for the social partners to commit themselves to opening useful and binding negotiations to prevent redundancies or production cuts that are detrimental to employment. the jobs at the terni steelworks must be safeguarded, as must other italian and european sites, and for this reason i believe that a dialogue is necessary between the governments of the member states concerned, the social partners and, in this specific case, thyssen krupp, in order to re-develop a new industrial plan for the relaunch of the magnetic and theinox sector at european level.
mr president, once again italy is in danger of being used as a wasteland. the case of thyssen krupp is the usual case of a multinational which grew thanks to the work of hundreds of italian workers and to the large sums of money made available by the italian state and the european union, and which then decided to close down, in this case in terni, in umbria, risking bringing 900 workers and their families to their knees and also wasting a great deal of professional expertise. as other colleagues have said, the closure was a surprise: no advance notice to the workers, local and national authorities and, above all, no justification, because today the closure seems to be dictated more by questionable choices of company strategy than by concrete technical reasons, since in italy production costs are lower, the quality is higher, the professionalism of the workers is undisputed: for 120 years, they've been working steel in terni. i wonder if it is acceptable that thyssen krupp insists on closing down after having taken from that territory, from those people, everything possible: what about the professionalism, the resources, even the hopes for the future? while we are convinced that large companies multinationals are certainly a source of employment and development for the whole of europe, some considerations need to be made: it is no secret, and no cause for scandal, that certain aspects of this european capitalism are not convincing at all: the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the european union, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support the work of the european parliament in this area. meanwhile, manufacturing is already being moved to india, with an eye to china, where labour costs are paltry and labour and environmental standards are non-existent. moreover, as has already been pointed out by other colleagues, these multinationals often take advantage of privatisations, which unfortunately often result in sales, in order to buy our family jewels at a lower price, as is the case with thyssen krupp in terni. but beyond the polemics, what do we ask of the european commission? firstly, immediate action, as far as it is in its power, to safeguard the steel industry in terni and to avert the danger of the closure of thyssen krupp's plants; secondly, a commitment to verify the effective application of european legislation to protect workers' rights; thirdly, a further commitment to make the disbursement of community funds conditional on employment guarantee agreements and on precise conditions and limits on relocations. we also expect a firm commitment to a strategy for the development of the steel industry throughout europe. finally, should thyssen krupp insist on its decision to close the plant, we ask the commission to verify whether all the competition rules are respected, in particular with regard to any abuse of a dominant position. in conclusion, mr president, while we are pleased with the first positive steps taken today by the italian government, which are also the result of a cross-cutting and convinced mobilization, we think that there is all the more reason not to let our guard down. we believe in the free market, but we also believe that there is no free market without democracy and, above all, without respect for workers and nations.
mr president, commissioner, i think it is important to establish whether the thyssen krupp case in terni was illegal under community law. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he has not already made a statement on the situation in the united kingdom, which is a very important area for the community, and whether he has not made a statement on the situation in the united kingdom. i believe that this is the task of the european institutions, but i also believe that the task must stop here. i do not think it is the task of the european commission, the european institutions or the italian government to make decisions on the relocation of companies operating in europe. the possibility of moving production to india, china or south america has caused outrage. personally, i think this is part of the rules of the game that for many years some countries of the european union, primarily italy, have exploited: the eu has a long tradition of providing its own assets, such as its labour supply, to the international economy. this is not to be disrespectful to the 450 employees of thyssen krupp in terni who are in danger of losing their jobs; it means also remembering the hundreds, maybe thousands, of people who may find work in china and india. it also means assuming - and i am referring to terni, which has already absorbed 10 000 surplus units in the steel industry in recent years - that in italy and in europe, in the areas of excellence, we can and should produce more to export to european and international markets. in conclusion, mr president, i would remind you that italy and europe need the confidence of investors to invest in terni, as elsewhere. to think that the political world can syndicate the choices of investors to this extent would not be a good service, nor would it ultimately benefit employment.
- the next item is the annual debate on the progress made towards establishing an area of freedom, security and justice in the european union (articles 2 and 39 of the treaty on european union) - 2003, on the basis of the oral question to the council (b5-0005/2004) and the oral question to the commission (b5-0006/2004) from the committee on civil liberties and rights and the committee on justice and home affairs. i would remind you that the vote on the motions for resolutions which conclude this debate will take place in the march ii part-session.
council. mr president, i would like to thank you for this opportunity to discuss the work of the justice and home affairs council in 2003. i would particularly like to thank mr ribeiro e castro for compiling the question on which i will base my comments. i want firstly to pay tribute to the work of the greek and italian presidencies in advancing the justice and home affairs agenda during 2003. significant progress was made on a whole range of jha issues, including legislative measures and operational cooperation in compliance with the tampere european council conclusions and the mandate from subsequent european councils. the measure of that progress can be seen in the fact that during that period the council adopted nine regulations, five directives, three framework decisions, two conventions, several agreements with third countries, more than twenty decisions and more than thirty resolutions and conclusions. although the focus of the debate is on progress during 2003, you have framed your questions in the context of the significant events of this year and i will bear this in mind in my response. the irish presidency is very conscious of the significance of this year for the union and also for the creation of an area of freedom, security and justice. the key objectives of our presidency are to make progress on the amsterdam requirements and to take forward work on the extended tampere programme. we will also continue to promote operational cooperation, particularly in relation to police and customs. turning now to asylum and immigration, i would like to mention some of last year’s achievements. in the context of asylum, the adoption of the dublin ii regulation establishing the criteria and mechanisms for determining member state responsibility for examining an asylum application, and the directive on minimum standards for the reception of asylum seekers, were major achievements. progress has also been made on two other key asylum proposals – the procedures directive and the qualifications directive. however, despite the best efforts of the italian and greek presidencies, it was not possible to reach agreement on these proposals. these proposals have been moved forward under successive presidencies and it will now fall to the irish presidency to attempt to resolve the outstanding issues, in keeping with the requirements of the tampere and the amsterdam treaty. we will now make every effort to get agreement on these texts within the timescale specified by the amsterdam treaty. 2003 also saw the adoption of the first legal instruments in the area of legal immigration – the directive on the right to family reunification and the directive on the status of third-country nationals who are long-term residents. the council also intends to move forward work on the directives on the condition of entry and residence of third-country nationals for the purposes of study, vocational training and voluntary services and for the purposes of research. in the area of illegal immigration, the council reached agreement on the directive on assistance in the case of transit for the purpose of removal by air and the decision on the organisation of joint flights. on an operational level, various joint projects were carried out at land, sea and air borders, including the establishment of an ad hoc centre for border guard training. the council also adopted a programme of measures to combat illegal immigration across maritime borders. the strengthening of border controls against illegal immigration will continue to be a council priority in 2004. the council intends to continue work on the draft regulation establishing the european agency for management and operational cooperation at external borders, in keeping with the conclusions on this proposal adopted by the justice and home affairs council in november. i hope that this can be agreed during the irish presidency. i would also mention that readmission agreements were concluded by the commission with hong kong and the agreement between the eu and macao was signed. negotiations are ongoing in relation to readmission agreements with morocco, russia and ukraine. the tampere european council made mutual recognition the cornerstone of judicial cooperation in civil and commercial matters and specified a programme of measures for this purpose. there has been steady progress in the area of judicial cooperation in civil law since then and 2003 was no exception. key measures adopted last year include the legal aid directive and the regulation on parental responsibility. the council also established a general approach on the proposed regulation creating a european enforcement order for uncontested claims. i hope that council and the european parliament can work together effectively to enable this proposal to be adopted in the coming months. discussions will continue within the council framework during 2004 on the proposed rome ii regulations on the law applicable to non-contractual obligations, which also comes under the codecision procedure. i also understand that the commission intends to present a proposal for a regulation to establish a european order for payment procedure in the coming weeks. your question also referred to the work of the hague conference on private international law. in recent years, on the basis of negotiating mandates adopted by the council, the community has been able to participate actively in the work of the conference where there are matters under discussion which concern its competence. in april 2003, on foot of a council decision, member states signed the 1996 hague protection of children convention, both on their own behalf and in the interest of the community. the increased engagement of the community with the hague conference is reflected in the fact that the council has authorised the commission to initiate negotiations with the conference on the possible accession of the community to that body. in the area of criminal judicial cooperation, the council made progress last year, both on measures aimed at promoting cooperation and measures in the area of mutual recognition. among the measures adopted were the framework decision on combating the sexual exploitation of children and child pornography, the framework decision on combating corruption in the private sector and the framework decision on the protection of the environment through criminal law. the jha council also agreed the framework decision on drugs. a decision to sign the agreements between the eu and the us on extradition and mutual legal assistance in criminal matters was also agreed last year. as far as trafficking in human beings and third countries is concerned, the brussels declaration on preventing and combating trafficking in human beings, endorsed by the council last may, provides the basis for a comprehensive five-year work programme. the irish presidency will continue to support commission initiatives to combat trafficking in human beings, bearing in mind the contents of that declaration and the work of the expert group on trafficking in human beings, which was established in 2003. i understand that the european arrest warrant has been implemented by eight member states. the remaining member states are in the process of preparing the necessary legislation. the accession states will have to apply the framework decision on the european arrest warrant from the date of their accession. as regards procedural safeguards, i understand that the commission will publish its legislative proposals in the course of this session. on data protection in the third pillar, i would point out that various instruments already incorporate provisions to ensure the protection of personal data, for example, the schengen convention, the europol convention and the eurojust convention. progress has also taken place in the area of police cooperation. all the member states share the view that europol has a leading role to play in supporting the law enforcement agencies of the european union in the fight against international organised crime and terrorism. it is a key agency in promoting the effectiveness of, and cooperation between, the european law enforcement agencies. the priority areas for europol agreed by council are drugs, euro counterfeiting, terrorism, trafficking in human beings – including illegal immigration – and financial crime. moreover, the police of the member states cooperate closely to fight serious transnational crime on a daily basis. many operational successes have been achieved in recent years, for example, as regards drugs, terrorism, trafficking in human beings and child pornography. europol has regularly been involved in providing support for operations in accordance with the europol convention. last november, the jha council signed a protocol amending the europol convention which included several provisions aimed at improving the functioning of europol. furthermore the council adopted several instruments relating to the operational functioning of europol, including the approval of a number of cooperation agreements between europol and other states. other measures adopted in the course of the year included a decision on the common use of liaison officers abroad by the law enforcement agencies of the member states and a decision amending the schengen convention to allow for the carrying-out of cross-border surveillance on persons suspected of involvement in a criminal offence. the chief of police task force and cepol – the european police college – also continue to play an important role in the development of police cooperation at eu level. i would like to say a few words about the last steps to be taken before enlargement becomes a reality. the accession treaty, which has been ratified by all the parties concerned, provides that, in general, acts relating to the establishment of an area of freedom, security and justice will be applied to the new member states from 1 may 2004. for the schengen acquis, however, there is a two-step process. the acceding states will participate in schengen-related measures on the lifting of internal border controls, including participation in the schengen information system, at a later stage, after 1 may 2004. the accession treaty also includes a safeguard clause. that provides that appropriate measures can be taken, up to 2007, if there are serious shortcomings or any imminent risks of such shortcomings in the transposition, state of implementation, or the application of legislative measures in certain areas of justice and home affairs. i will conclude my comments by briefly addressing your question about making european union business accessible to its citizens. the council acknowledges the importance of transparency in eu business. it is worth noting that the regulation on public access to eu documents, adopted in 2001, promotes transparency and provides for greater participation of eu citizens in the decision-making process. there is a high level of full access to legislative documents and partial access to other documents covering the current negotiations. at present 55% of the approximately 450 000 documents of the council document register are directly accessible to the public on the internet, in all the languages of the eu. the conclusions of each jha council meeting are available to the public, as are the texts of all adopted measures. i hope that my comments have given you an overview of the achievements in the justice and home affairs area during 2003. i thank you once again for giving me this opportunity and i look forward to continued cooperation between the european parliament and the council in this historic year. (applause)
the commission. mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, ladies and gentlemen, this annual debate is not exactly like any other, because it is taking place at a historic moment. ten new member states will soon be admitted to the union, we are in the process of negotiating a new constitutional treaty, we are approaching the end of the european parliament's term of office and the end of the period laid down by the treaty of amsterdam to lay the foundations for an area of freedom, security and justice. i would therefore like to congratulate mr ribeiro e castro not only on the thoroughness and quality of the preparation of this debate but also on the approach he has taken to it. it is not just the balance sheet for one year but a broader view of what has been done during this parliament's term. and the commission, for its part, regardless of what i say now, will contribute to this balance sheet through a communication which it will present to the council and parliament in june this year, in which we will give an exhaustive account of what has been achieved and what has not been achieved in the light of the tampere programme and the objectives of the treaty of amsterdam, but also in which we will put forward some ideas on what could be the basis of a tampere ii programme in the area of justice and home affairs. in its efforts to follow the order of the questions raised by the honourable member, the commission has always sought to keep in parallel the four main aspects of this policy on immigration and asylum, as defined in the treaty of amsterdam: regulating legal immigration flows, promoting the integration of third-country nationals into european societies, effectively combating illegal immigration and defining a common asylum policy and deepening the partnership with third countries to regulate migration flows. as regards legal immigration, the commission has put forward all the proposals to create the necessary legal framework. the proposal for a directive on family reunification and the legal status of third-country nationals who are long-term residents has already been adopted by the council. i hope that by the end of this parliamentary term it will also be possible to adopt the proposal on victims of trafficking in human beings who cooperate with the authorities and who are therefore entitled to a residence permit, as well as the proposals on the admission of students from third countries and researchers from third countries to the european union. if i would like to make one note of disappointment, it is that it is difficult to find a common platform with the member states on the management of legal immigration flows: the so-called admission for work purposes. there is a deadlock in the council on this, the commission proposal clearly does not have the support of the member states and the commission will come back to this issue when it presents, during this semester, the study we have been asked to produce on the link between legal immigration and illegal and clandestine immigration. in the field of integration, the steps taken since thessaloniki have been positive and we are now implementing preparatory projects for a broader strategy of a specific european programme on the integration of migrants into european societies. as regards illegal immigration, the commission has presented three action plans which have been accepted by the council and are being implemented: the first, on the fight against illegal immigration; the second, on the common management of the external borders; and the third, on a community policy on the return or repatriation of illegal immigrants. i also hope that it will be possible to approve quickly the agency for the control and operational coordination of external borders, just as the commission intends to present a new legal instrument: the revision of a common schengen manual on best practices for external border control. in parallel, security issues have gained a very significant weight after the events of 11 september 2001. the commission has already put forward proposals on strengthening the security of certain key documents, such as visas and residence permits, by introducing biometric data, and will present within a month its proposal on the introduction of biometric data in the passports of citizens of the european union. the development of the visa information system, the vis, is also progressing well. as regards the return policy, the commission is basically proposing, in the short term, two types of action: one on financial support, which will have to be discussed further, and the other on an initiative on minimum standards for return procedures. as the president-in-office of the council has already said, important legal instruments have been adopted in the field of asylum: temporary protection, minimum standards for reception, the dublin ii regulation and the practical implementation of the eurodac system, as well as the european refugee fund, which has completed its first phase of existence and has mobilised some eur 146 million over the last four years. the commission is actively working with the united nations high commissioner on new ideas for asylum policy, in particular with a view to ensuring a more orderly and organised entry of refugees into the european union and the possibility of lodging asylum applications outside the territory of the european union, and also with a view to strengthening the protection capacity in the regions of origin of the crises. but there are two fundamental points for asylum policy that are still missing. i am referring to the directive on asylum procedures and the directive on the qualification of refugee status and the approximation of the rules on subsidiary protection. i welcome the fact that i know of minister mcdowell's commitment that these two instruments can still be adopted during the irish presidency, and therefore within the time limit provided for in the amsterdam treaty, so that we can conclude this legislature with the adoption of all the necessary instruments for the first phase of the common asylum policy at european level. the commission will also present a report in spring 2004 on the ongoing negotiations on readmission agreements and we particularly welcome the fact that it has been possible to reach agreement with the council and parliament on the financial programme for the period 2004-08, amounting to eur 250 million, to develop a partnership with third countries for the joint management of migration flows. on civil judicial cooperation, i fully agree with what minister mcdowell has just said to you. the commission's assessment is largely satisfactory and curious because it is probably in the field of judicial cooperation in civil matters, rules on contractual disputes, non-contractual obligations, family relations and the regulation of parental authority that some of the decisions taken by this parliament and this council have the greatest influence on the daily lives of ordinary citizens, but which are usually given little attention by the media. it's much more media-friendly to talk about crime than to talk about the normal lives of normal people. well, it is in the area of the normal lives of ordinary people that the most progress has been made under the amsterdam treaty and the tampere programme and i sincerely hope that by the end of the legislature it will still be possible to overcome some difficulties in the adoption of the directive on compensation for victims of crime. as regards the external dimension, i would like to stress that the commission has asked the council for a mandate so that the community can formally join the conference on private international law, so that the process of communityisation of judicial and civil cooperation can be more coherent with that which is being carried out within the conventions of the hague conference. last but not least, the question of judicial cooperation in criminal matters. i believe that the balance of this legislature is positive. positive in the sense that framework decisions have been adopted on virtually all types of european crime provided for in the treaty with one exception, racism and xenophobia, which continues to cause difficulties at council level. but i would like to stress once again that the commission has been true to the principle that the cornerstone of judicial and criminal cooperation is the principle of mutual recognition of judicial decisions, the most striking example of which is the european arrest and surrender warrant which, as minister mcdowell has said, i hope will be fully in force in all 15 member states by the end of the first quarter of this year and will be in force in the applicant countries from 1 may this year. the commission will shortly present the framework decision on procedural guarantees and we are convinced that the initiative we will take in june this year to present a framework decision on minimum standards for data protection under the third pillar will be equally important. it is essential that criminal judicial cooperation and the strengthening of police and judicial cooperation mechanisms in the fight against crime are accompanied by rules on minimum procedural guarantees in criminal proceedings in all member states and on the protection of privacy and personal data in the same context. it is true, mr ribeiro e castro, that police cooperation is less legislative and more operational. on the legislative side, i think it is proven that the convention on the future of europe is right when it stresses that the instruments of the convention are inoperative in this area: the amendments to the europol convention are still awaiting ratification by the majority of member states; the convention on mutual legal assistance, signed in may 2000, has not yet been ratified by all member states of the european union and is therefore not yet in force. but operational cooperation has been improved and it is essential for europol to be able to fulfil its role, for there to be trust in europol by the police forces of the member states and, above all, for there to be a permanent exchange of information in relation to forms of transnational crime. finally, mr president, i fully share your concern that it is essential to inform the citizens of what has been done. the commission has been carrying out information campaigns on the charter of fundamental rights, on some of the legislative instruments we have adopted which can have a more direct impact on the lives of citizens, but, to be perfectly frank with you, i think it will be in the elections to the european parliament, through the members of the european parliament - who can be proud of what they have done in this legislature in the field of justice and home affairs - that the union can rely on, since they are the best interpreters of the information to which citizens are entitled, because they prove that european democracy works, because they prove that the issues which concern the daily lives of citizens are taken seriously by the bodies of the european union, starting with their legitimate representatives, the members of the european parliament. mr president, it is true that the progress mentioned by the rapporteur, the council and commission representatives - to whom i congratulate myself - has been made, but it must be admitted that it is charter of fundamental rights binding in all areas of union activity and extending the community method to judicial, criminal and police cooperation, would have solved many of the problems that still remain. we hope that this is just a delay. in the meantime, some paradoxical contradictions still hampering the implementation of the tampere programme, especially in the area of immigration and police and criminal cooperation, would have to be overcome. the same governments which in tampere approved a reasonable plan of action often fail to adopt it when it is proposed by the commission and urged on by parliament. and when they do, perhaps because of contingent emergencies, they end up adopting them, they often don't implement them in their own countries: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat racism and xenophobia. it must therefore always be borne in mind that the abolition of internal frontiers favours freedom of movement in the internal market, but also favours criminals if the powers to prevent and punish crime remain confined to national territories. it is therefore time to relaunch an updated political programme for the harmonious construction of the european legal area, a programme which is not limited to partial responses to specific emergencies, and we await with hope the communication which has been promised to us by the commission. in respect for the fundamental right of privacy of our citizens. in this case, however, it must also be said that the commission's inert and conniving behaviour was deplorable. it is indeed time to take mr president, in the near future freedom, security and justice will be particularly critical areas, given the different approaches that the new and old member states have to these problems. there will be obstacles to real operational cooperation between police forces at european level. a real system is needed to defeat mafias and criminal organisations. the italian law, which effectively abolishes international warrants, seems to be designed to prevent the powerful in the palace from being questioned on issues that are embarrassing to them, such as the questionable frequentation of tax havens, the actual illegal export of capital, participation in convenient offshore companies. at the moment, the european arrest warrant has not yet been ratified by my country. after almost 50 years, the attempt to speed up extradition procedures is far from having achieved any concrete results. a closer look at the question of justice requires, then, a preliminary remark concerning the independence of the judiciary. in italy today, there is a desire to return to the legal and political culture of the middle ages, which was forever superseded in the western world by the abolition of immunities and privileges, sanctioned in august 1789 by the french states general. the division of powers in the modern state is now being called into question, first by ordinary ad personam laws, then by constitutional laws which are tried to pass off as ordinary, and therefore not linked to qualified majorities and re-reading. in the middle, the destabilizing attempt to subordinate the judiciary to the executive power, which we see daily put into practice by the italian government with systems unworthy of a civil state. why such grave words in the european parliament? i believe that democracy is not something that is exhibited but something that is lived and that the more people who live it together, the stronger it becomes. to overcome a difficult period for my country, all the european institutions need to keep a close watch on what is happening in our country today, but it can be a bad example, and it can easily be exported.
mr president, i have followed with great interest the debate which is aimed at taking stock not only of this year but of the whole of the legislature in the field of freedom, security and justice. it is a difficult balance sheet to draw up, because it has many faces: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. the political part is the will of the member states. the legal budget shall be supported by the instruments made available by the treaty; the social budget is illogical, because it is dictated by the continuous evolution of the framework within which this area of freedom, security and justice is being created. in view of the time available to me, i shall concentrate on the political budget. there is a declared will on the part of most member states - but unfortunately not all - to extend and consolidate the area of freedom, security and justice, in response to an increasingly evident will on the part of european citizens to open the doors to solidarity and to widen the areas of cooperation in the field of welcoming new migratory flows, in closing the gaps that still exist in the recognition of the right to asylum, citizenship and establishment in any country of the union. in this awareness i like to identify the formidable impetus of the appeal contained in the charter of fundamental rights of european citizens, launched in nice in 2000 and waiting to acquire constitutional dignity. it is a document comparable to a good wine: at first, it did not impress many, as it was considered weak and imperfect, but as time went on, the intrinsic quality of the text emerged more and more, in which we find precise references for all the choices and actions that this difficult historical phase imposes on us. a further step forward will be the adoption at second reading of the directive on the free movement of community citizens, their rights and obligations, for which i am the rapporteur. this is a platform which must be urgently consolidated in order to be able to cope effectively with the new obligations imposed on us by the increasingly massive and chaotic arrival of third-country nationals. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. almost all the political groups accepted the rapporteur's call not to amend the text adopted by the council, which only recognises 50% of parliament's requests. there is also agreement with the commission on this point. we believe that this directive, although perfected, should be implemented within this legislature, preferring to see the glass half full rather than half empty: i mean, better half the claims than a sine die delay. we hope that the council will appreciate this responsible position, so that the citizens will be given at least that part of the reforms which the text contains and which are, in any case, important steps forward in giving legal substance and political dignity to the status of european citizens.

mr president, i am pleased to announce the presence in the gallery of a delegation of members of parliament from the lombardy region, including my daughter elisabetta fatuzzo, who, knowing that i was to speak on the report on agricultural incomes in the european union, reminded me that there were once fruit trees producing apples, pears, cherries, the very tasty cherries, and there were fields of wheat and produce from the garden. when will we again have the pleasure of harvesting these fruits near our home, as we once did? and when will we, like adam, again receive the apples directly from the tree, picked by eve?
we are staying with this report on agriculture. i would like to see research in agriculture progress, progress, progress, so that grapes can be used to produce a wine that does not cause liver disease in those who drink it. and i would like this farming to produce pears that taste like pears, apples that taste like apples, cherries that taste like cherries, peaches that taste like peaches. but above all, mr president, may the mint - which is my favourite flavour - continue to have, more and more, the flavour and aroma of the mint, which i love and want so much.

i went to rome from milan by car. i had to go and see my mother, who i haven't seen for some time because of my commitments as a member of the european parliament. between bologna and florence there is a lot of traffic, what in french is called bouchon; you can't get through: a line of trucks, trains, cars that were stuck. i see mr gargani, the rapporteur of this report, who was there waiting for me. what are you doing here, gargani? he says: i'm here to show you what would happen if my report on the type approval of motor vehicles was not approved. look, a motor vehicle has overturned. so remember, when you come to parliament tomorrow, to vote in favour. it was a dream because i have never met mr gargani, but i think it is right to vote in favour, as i did.
i had a dream, mr. president. i dreamt of mr berlusconi, the president of the italian government; he had an aura, and so i can call him saint berlusconi. berlusconi used to say to me: i know that you are voting tomorrow on the cercas report on working time, which calls for those who are exempt from the minimum working time to be prevented from working too much. look at me . i work all day, from 6 in the morning until 2 in the morning. look how young i am, how active, how ready i am in my work, and also satisfied! my advice then is that you vote against this cercas report, so that everyone can work as hard as i do and be as satisfied and as young as i am. that's when i woke up and realized it was a dream. i felt free to vote in good conscience and, in fact, i voted in favour of the cercas report, even though mr berlusconi had advised me to do the opposite.
mr president, i would have liked to have said what mr menendez del valle said and to have made his analysis, but i will say something else. sister dominique runs the house of our lady of sorrows in abudis, a village near jerusalem, divided by the wall of segregation and annexation, as palestinians and israeli peace activists call it, or a security barrier, as the israeli authorities call it. here is the message from sister dominique: on january 11, construction began on the new nine-meter-high separation wall. it replaces another wall, much lower, built in august 2002 that allowed people to climb over it, when they were no longer allowed to go to jerusalem. thousands of people have climbed over this first wall: children, students, mothers, elderly people; many have fallen, some have even died. two months ago, we had to call an ambulance for a 65-year-old man who had fallen and hit his head. the ambulance arrived a long time later. on the way, the army searched the ambulance, forced the injured man's wife to get out, and when they got to the hospital, it was too late for him. the things that happen around this wall are intolerable. for months, hundreds of people a day have been coming through our property to escape military control, because they don't have the necessary permits. people around us live in fear of being arrested, beaten, humiliated, as all too often happens. the pressure is constant and the living conditions are increasingly miserable. in the face of the general inertia, we feel alone and helpless. we want to speak for those voiceless people who every day for the past two years have struggled to get to their workplaces and their schools or died from lack of medical care. in trying to carry out our mission, we also ran into trouble with the soldiers when we had to hospitalize people because the ambulance was not allowed to pass. the same problem arises when someone dies: the cemetery is on the other side of the wall. the shops are on the other side too. many of our patients are alone, their families can no longer visit them, we don't know what will happen when the wall is finished. most of our elders and employees are from the west bank. of our 18 employees only three have jerusalem id; for two years, they had to climb over the wall and change their route to avoid checkpoints because, even with a pass, the soldiers would turn them back. this nine-meter wall will force us to fire most of our staff and not accept the elderly from the west bank, therefore the poorest. we are more worried and isolated than ever, because of the wall and because the surrounding area has become a military zone. please help us! these are the words of sister dominique, but much worse is the situation of the people of qalqilya, who are prevented by the wall from even seeing the sunrise. the only exit from the city has an iron gate, for fifty thousand people, which the israeli army opens and closes depending on its mood. it's locked after 5 p.m. and no one can get in or out of town. and what about the inhabitants of budrus who, along with many israeli peace activists, have tried to resist the uprooting of their trees and the destruction of their homes in a nonviolent way? they were brutally beaten by the soldiers; an israeli boy is still in the hospital. the wall and the barriers that are being built are actually forcing palestinians to live in ghettos. for security, says sharon, to prevent terrorist acts, not wanting to understand that security cannot be based on the construction of a wall and settlements, but on the possibility for the palestinians to live freely in their state in coexistence with the state of israel. the wall is not just imprisonment: it is once again confiscation of land, it is once again annexation. its line is not on the 1967 borders but penetrates and divides the palestinian territory. meanwhile, the propaganda to get the wall accepted is intensifying. concerned by the negative view of the international community, the israeli authorities are trying to make the wall less monstrous. unfortunately, the proposal, published by the israeli newspaper maariv, is not a joke, as the foreign ministry's public relations team stated: if the wall were painted in bright colors it would be more aesthetically pleasing and the damage to public relations would be reduced. parliament condemned the construction of the wall and called for it to be stopped. the walls - we saw it with berlin - kill freedom. the un general assembly voted for the hague court of justice to give an opinion on its legality. the president of the european council of ministers has very inappropriately stated that he does not consider the court of justice's assessment to be appropriate. why is it that when it comes to the israeli authorities, international law, human rights violations, they must always be dismissed in the name of negotiations that do not exist, and the quartet, instead of pressing for the road map, lets time pass and people continue to die in palestine and israel? it is complicity, because this wall is not security; this wall is territorial annexation, it is colonial conquest. the european union cannot throw international law to the wind. we are taking diplomatic action, we are re-launching the road map, we are doing everything we can to prevent actions that could kill israeli civilians, but we are putting economic and political pressure on israel so that sharon does not take unilateral action and returns to the negotiating table. the international community must listen and give strength, as the council's voice also said, to the geneva initiative, to the palestinian non-violent resistance against military occupation, to the soldiers who refuse to bomb and be instruments of repression in the occupied territories, to the circle of palestinian and israeli fathers and mothers whose loved ones have been killed by the army, by israeli settlers or by palestinian murders and suicides, and who refuse to take revenge. they all show that coexistence and security are possible, in the affirmation of the right of all people to live in peace, with dignity and justice.
madam president, mr president-in-office, mrs sandbæk has said that she wants to tear down the wall. it 's an old story . there is a europe - that of mrs sandbæk - which, for essentially the same reasons, wanted to tear down the maginot line 60 years ago. the famous berlin wall, that wall of shame, was erected and supported by those who today want to tear down the wall of israel. that is what is happening, there is no doubt about it, although you have said sensible things this evening, mr cohn-bendit. it happens so rarely that i won't waste time - yours and mine - to counter your remarks. i repeat: congratulations, because you said sensible things!
