mr president-in-office of the council, as you have heard from many of my colleagues, the reaction is almost unanimous, that is to say, we all consider the council's letter to the high court of justice suggesting that it should not take a decision on the question of the wall in palestinian territory to be particularly serious and inappropriate. in any parliamentary democracy, in the face of such a broad and well-established opinion of parliament, a government resigns. although the institution of resignation is not provided for in the european institutions, i would ask the council to take account of the opinion expressed in this house, namely that it is not the demolition of the wall that is endangering and slowing down the peace process, but the wall itself. this wall, mr president, as you know very well and as many colleagues have said, is first and foremost a dividing wall because it does not restore the 1967 green line but enters into palestinian territory, dividing the palestinian community, the students from the schools, the children from the parents, the farmers from the fields, the sick from the doctors. it is a wall that takes away land from the palestinian community: it penetrates up to 25 kilometres into the west bank; the first block already built, 150 kilometres long, has confined 50,000 palestinians outside their territory, which will increase to 130,000 when it is completed. these figures, mr president, are not manufactured by this parliament but are supplied to us by israeli civil society and israeli non-governmental organizations, who remind us that 670 000 palestinians are at risk of being severely harmed by the construction of this wall and that 14 per cent of the palestinian territory will be taken away from the palestinian authority. faced with these realities, we believe that the wall itself is the cause of anger, despair and hatred. we must remember this, mr president, and we must not be surprised if the result of anger, despair and hatred - god forbid - turns into violence. the day we are again faced with the victims and the figures of such violence, we must also remember how much we have contributed to the despair, hatred and anger of this people.
commission. the commission welcomes this debate and broadly supports the content of various draft resolutions tabled by parliament. whilst the facts are not yet fully clear, the parmalat case is deeply worrying. it is the latest in a growing list of major financial scandals of various types – in various places – over the last few years. the apparent size of this fraud is staggering, and the apparent complicity of a number of people from distinguished, liberal professions together with the failures of regulatory control are equally staggering. scandal upon scandal will cumulatively weaken financial markets like the corrosive drip of a leaking fuel tank. many sensible investors will pull out. economic growth will be affected because the cost of capital will rise – the enron affair has already shown that. so this matters to all of us. my first point is this: the financial services industry had better get its act together, and do so quickly. we need some real industry leadership to stand up and take charge, to clear out the crooks, expose their unscrupulous practices and curb excessive greed. if industry leaders are not prepared to do this, then regulators will have to do much more than perhaps they or we would like. if that is the result, then industry leaders cannot winge about regulation from brussels. they will have brought it upon themselves. industry leadership is lacking at the moment, quite frankly. secondly – we already have a range of policies in hand which will go some way to improving matters in the future: measures in the financial services action plan – such as the market abuse and prospectus directives – which should be rapidly implemented; measures such as the new investment services directive, which will help deal with some of the major conflicts of interest in the investment services business; the new international accounting rules which will enhance disclosure, along with the transparency directive – which we want adopted before this parliament breaks for its elections. all these directives also enhance the powers of competent authorities to act and also to cooperate much more across borders. in my view, that means cooperating as well, in the same way, with foreign regulators – non-european regulators – in particular the sec in washington and also the public company accounting oversight board in the united states. capital markets today – as we all know – are global and regulatory cooperation must be global too to match them. thirdly, in march i shall propose to my colleagues in the commission a revised company law directive on the statutory audit function. it will strengthen controls over the audit profession in the european union with independent oversight; strengthened inspection; stronger ethical and educational principles and high quality audit standards. these four aspects will be contained in the revised company law directive. as a result of parmalat, the revision of the eighth company law directive is likely also to include the following four elements: first, full group auditor responsibility for consolidated accounts of a group of companies; second, obligatory independent audit committees for listed companies – all 7 000 of them in europe; thirdly, stricter auditor rotation requirements; and fourthly, strengthened sanctions. i am accelerating work in three other areas in the corporate governance or company law areas in order to have proposals ready, if possible, later this year. they are the following three areas: the role of non-executive directors; directors' responsibility for company accounts; full disclosure in the company accounts of offshore special purpose vehicles, including the reason why the company uses those offshore structures and a much stricter verification by the group auditor of their content. we are also working hard on the issues of conflicts of interest of financial analysts and looking again at credit rating agencies. the katiforis report is of considerable help in that regard. finally, the role and regulatory control of offshore centres need to be tightened. we are considering the options, although that is not easy. we expect to table in june the third money laundering directive and we expect it to play a significant role. to conclude, this is a very important debate. we support the main lines of this resolution. we are working, resolutely, in that sense. we still wish for strong industry leadership and an appropriate dose of ethics.
mr president, commissioner, the parmalat affair is a systemic crisis because it was made possible by a worldwide network of complicity and omissions, involving banks, financial institutions, external and internal controls, public and private. i think that the parmalat affair represents the negative possibilities of the globalisation of financial markets, whose internationalisation allows fraudsters to easily hide information or manipulate it, demonstrating how a necessary combination of transparency, efficiency and stability is too often theoretical and above all fragile in the face of perverse wills that sink their roots in the absence of ethics. unfortunately, the parmalat affair is absolutely comparable to many, too many financial scandals that have characterized the news of recent times: worldcom and enron for all of us. it is painful and difficult to admit, but something in the global balance has cracked and dragged everyone down the drain, from the unwitting savers to the operators, to those who had to watch and prevent this from happening. we do not want to and we do not have to deal with individual responsibilities, because that is not our job; it is our duty to work to prevent such cases from happening again. it is essential to combat abuse and fraud and to ensure that financial analysts do not end up recommending bad investments to their clients. in the motion for a resolution which we are tabled - and we have also looked at the commission communication to the council and parliament on the modernisation of company law, which was published last may - there are indications which take account of the impact which the parmalat case has had and could have on employees, investors, banks and the proper functioning of the financial system. it is our task to try to reverse this, to try to protect the savings of millions of people and to restore full investor confidence in the financial markets. it is essential that we learn from this case and react in a proportionate way, giving an objective assessment of what has happened, projecting ourselves into the future and not limiting ourselves to a hasty search for scapegoats. it is a question of establishing what has not worked, what are the inefficiencies in the legislation and of remedying them, including by providing for new powers of public control. we may have different political views on the purpose of regulation, on the production and distribution of income, but we should all agree that action must be taken without cheating. perhaps the crisis of legality is not just a problem of men but also of institutions which, perhaps, are unsatisfactory. perhaps their rules need to be studied, rethinked, revised, and for that we need to cooperate. it is the competent financial supervisory authorities of the various jurisdictions of the union that are to cooperate. cooperation is also required with third countries: the european union is not the only one to have a plan, but the united states is also part of this plan of the first importance. i would also urge the commission to monitor any new measures concerning the role of the rating agencies. i would again urge the commission to incorporate in the corporate governance action plan measures to prevent conflicts of interest, whether these relate to investments by banks or other financial institutions in listed companies controlled by in-house entities, or to the cases of financial analysts working for investment banks who advise their clients to invest in companies to which their bank provides well-paid services. shareholder empowerment and participation must also be increased. the importance of ensuring maximum transparency before and after transactions is essential for investors in the financial instruments sector. it is therefore desirable to establish an authority at european level which takes a view, placing itself as a super partes, of european financial markets and which examines cases with cross-border implications, in line with the recent modernisation of european competition policy. i would also ask the commission to adopt as soon as possible a decision on the compatibility of the italian government's rules on european legislation in the dairy sector in the parmalat case. the companies in this sector, which are members and former members of the parmalat group, have long been in a financial situation of extremely unfavourable liquidity, which could lead to a crisis in the whole chain. if the request for aid were to be considered to be contrary to the rules on free competition, it would be the beginning of the end for a sector with a tradition and quality characteristics that are indisputable at european level. the parmalat affair requires a great sense of responsibility, above all at our level, at european level. we have three areas to work on. i think we have drawn up a good text and the next parliament will certainly have the opportunity to cooperate even better with the commission.
mr president, our group appreciates both the speed with which the commission is intervening on this particularly painful point, especially for the italian economy, and the sense of responsibility with which the commissioner has explained his proposals, which are urgent if the figures we are confronted with are true: the €15 billion represents a financial hole equivalent to 15% of the entire eu budget. it is also the result of economic deregulation and financial cynicism, of which, as mr fiori has pointed out, many are guilty: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the tax havens are not used as a source of revenue, and that the tax authorities are not involved in the process of creating the tax havens. many of these tax havens are under the control of eu member states. i'll give you some italian figures: 25 italian companies control 400 offshore companies; of these, 171 are resident in delaware, a small state in the united states of america, and 127 in luxembourg, the nerve centre of the european union. so, what are we doing here in europe? i would also like to propose, on behalf of mr imbeni, an initiative to limit banking secrecy. the nobel prize for economics, joseph stieglitz, points out that the use of banking secrecy is often one of the factors of economic instability and that the european union always, and appropriately, preaches transparency. i would not want us to stop at some shrines! we could propose to the international community to finally give a virtuous sense to globalization and, therefore, a global agreement on banking secrecy, which we take care of, trying to propose a kyoto of the banks  if i may use the metaphor  for an ecology of finance, to finally bring back a fulfilled ethical sense within the global economy. commissioner, you have clearly stated that the national control instruments are no longer sufficient: we urgently need global and supranational rules. financial markets must be governed and conflicts of interest and the perverse interplay between banks and companies avoided. the european union must swiftly review eu legislation, by strengthening sanctions against fraud, by taking action against impunity in tax havens and by establishing rules on the absolute independence of auditors. a subject of such urgency and sensitivity perhaps merits more attention from us - from parliament and the community institutions - than mere communication. we are not proposing a committee of inquiry, but perhaps an inquiry promoted by the conference of presidents and therefore endowed with the authority of an initiative of the conference of presidents, an inquiry to be entrusted, in an informal and voluntary way, to a group of members, which could extend beyond the end of this parliamentary term and whose functions are exploratory and propositional, to understand what has happened, how it could have happened and what needs to be done to prevent it from happening again, to avoid similar events in the future to those of parliament. finally, mr president, commissioner, the commission communication refers to shareholders, management, the relationship between shareholders and management, while little or no mention is made of workers, who are the first to pay the consequences of tragic events such as the parmalat affair. we would like to see a strong and clear voice from this house to protect, guarantee and support the thousands of workers from parma to sicily, both direct and indirect, who are now risking their jobs and their wages. i believe that an ethical sense of the economy, to which we referred earlier, recalling the responsibilities of governments and the european union, cannot ignore the main problem today, which is the protection and guarantee of the right to work.
mr president, commissioner, the parmalat case, like the cirio case in italy and the enron case in the united states or similar cases in great britain or france, is the emblem of the crisis which has been affecting multinational financial structures for some time, the basis of which is the great, disastrous gap between the real economy and the speculative bubble: we have a paper and virtual wealth that is disproportionately greater than the real, real wealth that is based on production and savings. another feature common to the cases mentioned is the lack or insufficient control by the institutions empowered to do so: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial system is in a position to meet the requirements of the single market. it is no coincidence that when these institutions are called into question by savers or consumer associations, they all repeat the same leitmotif: we did not know the real situation, we were unconscious of certain illegal maneuvers. either these institutions are lying or the rules of control are so elastic and so imprecise that their interpretation can be used to defraud large investors, first of all savers, perhaps with the acquiescence of the banks themselves, as we have already mentioned in various questions on the problem of the speculative bubble, which we have discussed long before it burst, but which did not receive sufficient attention, even from the european institutions. i speak of acquiescence because one additional fact that emerges from these cases is the conflict of interest suffered by the credit institutions, always to the detriment of the investors. in order to remedy the shortcomings of the rules, we have already submitted a resolution to the commission in the preceding weeks, in which we asked it to take the initiative of bringing together, at european level, the bodies and institutions responsible for the supervision and control of relations between companies and banks, in order to protect the interests of savers, and to assess the desirability of harmonizing the regulations in force in order to ensure the application of the ethical principle that controllers should not be paid by controlled persons. the obvious illegalities that are tragically affecting the investments of hundreds of thousands of savers offer us an opportunity to break another spear in favour of the independent professional advisers we have already spoken of in this parliament and to invite the commission to study the possibility of creating a european list of such professionals: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the future of the european union's financial services industry. in calling for measures to support the workers in the parmalat chain all workers in the chain and compensation for savers who have been defrauded in some cases twice not only by parmalat but also by those who have recommended certain investments to them we believe, however, that the union must seriously face the possible new disastrous consequences that could be caused by the huge gap between the real economy and the financial economy. until measures are taken to reduce this gap, the risks will always be looming.
mr president, the setting up of a committee of inquiry, which we are asking the european parliament to do, will provide useful information which we have not found sufficiently expressed in the motion for a resolution, and will give concrete and precise indications on the instruments needed to prevent a repetition of cases such as that of the parliament. we think that 5 billion bonds were issued by parmalat finance, based in rotterdam in the netherlands, by a company with a modest capital of a few thousand euros. in reality, these were not actual bonds, but promissory notes, scrap paper, which some banks - even of great importance - recommended to their customers from their own desks, concealing their true nature, which they could not but did not know. concrete rules should be laid down to ensure that subscribers are better informed: the modules should contain a brief description of the product, including - this is our request - a risk sheet, specifying the characteristics of the securities, which is not provided for today. and then there's the compensation. it is useless to cry over spilled milk, we must think of recovering the sums that have been misappropriated and give precise guarantees to savers in the event of default. why should the banks not respond directly and immediately when they have advised, even when they were close to the crash, to buy junk securities? shareholders may also have a supervisory role at company meetings, but for this to be effective, provision must be made for the publication of minutes in the information file of the measure which is the subject of the meeting. in the present case, the judges are already complaining of great difficulties in recovering the sums, for example in us banks, because american rules now give us creditors preferential treatment. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european union is able to take action against the us and its allies in the fight against terrorism. in an economic democracy, which is achieved through the participation of workers, why not provide for the obligation of representation, including control, of the workers-shareholders in the boards of directors of listed companies?
mr president, the parmalat case is difficult to decipher because the responsibilities and the complex and ambiguous mechanisms that made it possible are not fully understood. to consider it a purely italian scandal is reductive. this is a major international affair involving major international banks. they are very clear about the damage that has been done to savers. it is for them that we must think, study and encourage the application of appropriate measures, such as concrete forms of insurance, which can compensate small savers involved in the crashes. what has been described as the biggest financial and accounting fraud ever recorded in europe has dramatically highlighted the serious lack of strict and effective rules to combat the phenomena of false balance sheets of companies which, according to a recent italian law, have been decriminalised. in a single market such as the european one, where, according to the treaties, the constitutional principles of the free movement of persons, goods, capital and services apply, it is essential that the commission intervene to protect european citizens more effectively against this type of fraud, including through community measures that can increase the surveillance of the auditing of budgets for union undertakings. but that's not enough. the committee also calls for the establishment of strict rules at european level to prevent any economic, financial, corporate and personal participation cooperation between companies active in the area of the twenty-five and structures operating in the so-called rogue countries, an expression of tax havens. in particular, a severe economic embargo, binding in this sense, would force the entire international community, including the usa, to adopt virtuous behaviour, capable of preventing the occurrence of unfortunate events such as those we are talking about.
mr president, there are cases in which europe is particularly necessary, in which speed of action is particularly important and crucial in determining whether our institutions are really capable of interpreting the needs of the citizens. this must be done in a visible and decisive way. of course, we are not the united states, commissioner bolkestein, and our capacity to act is much more limited, because it is caught between national jealousies and various veto rights, especially in the areas we are dealing with at the moment. however, in our view, there is scope for action in the parmalat case which the commission should use. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. on the one hand, the commission should make proposals on the behaviour of companies when they operate in so-called tax havens to put their dirty money in. we think that there is a possibility of community legislation which would try to curb and prevent this, because this was one of the fundamental causes of the events in parliament. and that, commissioner, goes far beyond a proposal on recycling. i am afraid that in order to be truly on the side of consumers and savers, the commission, at this very politically delicate time, should be able to formulate a proposal which is visibly on their side and which, i repeat, goes far beyond the proposals which have been set out today. secondly, i would like to hear from commissioner bolkestein some proposals to ensure that the countries of the european union which control these tax havens or have access to them or which have particularly permissive legislation in this respect can be induced in some way to change course. thirdly, as mr jonckheer has already said, what is commissioner bolkestein's view of the union's position on these issues at the world trade organisation?
mr president, as my colleagues have already said, the financial collapse of the multinational parmalat was a very serious event for italy, involving thousands of small italian savers and risking damaging financial credibility and creating a general lack of confidence in our country in the future, even among foreign investors. one of the causes of this financial collapse was, first of all, the certification of the false balance sheets of the parmalat group by one of the most important audit firms, which allegedly concealed the real financial situation of the company. moreover, it is clear that the other national supervisory bodies banca d'italia and consob would not have carried out the necessary checks adequately and in a timely manner. the case also involved a number of italian and european credit institutions, some of the best known on the world financial market, which issued a large number of bonds that were no longer refundable. for all these reasons, the parmalat case has been called the european enron, although it is in fact more serious than the american scandal, since the american gdp is much higher than the italian one. as is well known, the parmalat crash is unfortunately only the latest in a long series of similar cases that have occurred throughout europe. what do the italian and european citizens, who are often victims of this financial adventurism, expect? i believe that urgent and concrete measures are required, both to protect the workers in the parmalat chain in this case and, more generally, to protect and compensate the savers involved in these crashes. in addition, instruments are expected to prevent such operations from occurring again in the future and to limit the exposure of small savers to the greatest extent possible. finally, i would draw the commissioner's attention to two last points: i believe that it would be appropriate to create a european supervisory and control body - a sort of european sec - with supervisory and sanctioning powers, under the direct control of the ecb, to check the efficiency and transparency of the stock market in order to better protect investors. finally, the ban on tax havens should be introduced by means of a blacklist.
i am pleased to note, commissioner, the initiatives you have announced and those which have recently been taken. you had the merit of saying, in the midst of the enron scandal, that europe was not immune to this kind of scandal. i want to make it clear to everyone that the parmalat scandal is not that of a healthy company, destroyed by financial scams; the parmalat scandal, unfortunately for italy, is the case of a shitty and inefficient company, which someone tried to save through financial scams. we have talked a lot today, and rightly so, about controls. i think one issue that needs to be addressed is the role of the central banks of individual countries. in italy, the central bank, with its competence in competition and thus in antitrust matters, has a key role in guiding the activities of banks and, through the banks, within the industrial system. i believe that this is an anomaly which must be eliminated and that europe, the european central bank, the commission and the council must take action. i also believe that we must be extremely careful in balancing controls and sanctions in response to the parmalat scandal. it is good to deepen controls, but let us not clog up a financial market which is vital for european economic development. we also think of penalties as a deterrent to fraudulent behaviour as much as controls. in particular, we must provide consumers with an important tool, the class action tool, under article 153 of the treaty, which would be useful in discouraging fraudulent behaviour by everyone.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the parmalat crash and other disturbing financial crises that preceded and followed it have laid bare the limits of the rules governing financial markets. as in the united states following the enron case and in the united kingdom for the 34 cases of default, the need for strong legislative measures to strengthen the protection of savers and, more broadly, to try to limit the discredit that inevitably takes place in the financial market involved was immediately felt in italy. in these cases, however, the emotional factor must be set aside and, in particular, one must not be swayed by the seriousness of what has happened and the impulses that may result. i also do not think that exceptional measures, with the assumption of supervision, are acceptable, because there is a risk that a new body will be created which could hardly make an additional contribution in an environment which needs to be restructured. the existing provisions must be properly applied and certainly reformed. bankruptcy law can also play a key role: the par condi creditorum and the period of suspicion should also be included in extraordinary and supervised administration. this would strengthen the protection of creditors and the credit system, which is certainly not free from blame in the parmalat case, would not be the arbiter of itself in the actions brought, which would instead be subject to the assessment of a delegated judge. to maintain the high-profile proposals, information on all financial products offered to the public should be strengthened, both in the ex ante information leaflets and in the ex post communications, and the asymmetries in information to the public between the different financial products should also be removed. transparency control should be extended to all, including banking and insurance products with management content offered to savers. the introduction of stricter incompatibilities is inevitable, with the result that the actual independence of the control bodies is undermined. however, more will have to be done for companies that issue bonds: documents will have to be received committing the company, documents saying what it will do, when it will do it and with what investments; the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the budgetary authority is able to carry out its duties. ethics officer. this figure, which is a kind of listener, a signal-receiver, must be able to detect even weak elements of alarm which escape the analysts because they are attracted by the evidence. a reading of the work of the ethics officer will reveal not only the management results but also the external dimension of the company's reliability.
commission. i should like to begin by thanking members of parliament for their comments on what i said at the start of this debate, as well as for all other remarks made about this important issue. i would like to pick up on a few points at the closure of this debate in view of the importance of the subject. firstly, i should like to say something on a truly important aspect of this case: offshore tax havens and special purpose vehicles. the full complexity of the situation in the case of parmalat, as regards the use of offshore financial centres and special purpose vehicles, still has to be clarified. it is clear that the time has come to introduce some real order here. all these types of special operations ought to be subject to three requirements: firstly, they ought to be listed in company accounts; secondly, the company concerned should explain its purpose – why does that company have these special purpose vehicles? – and thirdly, the group auditor ought to be made responsible for checking that what is supposed to be there really is there. there must be greater control over these highly complex corporate structures. along the same lines, the commission will consider increased disclosure requirements in the context of the amendments to the fourth and seventh company law directives. these amendments are due to be adopted by september 2004. the question of the transparency of corporate vehicles and other legal arrangements is under consideration by the commission, both internally within the european union and also in the wider international context. secondly, i would like to say something on auditing. it might well be asked whether there is a systemic failure of the audit function; although i believe that the auditor has an important role to play in ensuring proper financial reporting, it is not the only factor under scrutiny. it is important to take a broader picture of the responsibilities for financial reporting and the functioning of capital markets. apart from auditors, we should also question the role of management, of non-executive directors, of corporate governance arrangements, of credit rating agencies – as mentioned this afternoon- and of investment banks. with regard to auditors, i believe there is a need for a stronger backbone to resist improper accounting and undue commercial pressure by the audited entities. several measures that the commission will propose in its forthcoming directive should help auditors keep their backs straight; for example, independence requirements, strong public oversight, communication with audit committees and serious external quality reviews. we may also ask whether there is an accounting issue. apparently, the existing legislation in italy on annual accounts requires neither a cashflow statement, nor much information about financial derivatives and their impact on the financial position. the fair value accounting directive, adopted in 2001, has been in force since 1 january 2004. that directive requires detailed disclosure about financial instruments such as the fair value of financial instruments, information about their extent and nature, and a table showing the movements in fair value. annual accounts, prepared on the basis of international accounting standards, which require a cashflow statement and also fair values, would have better reflected parmalat's true financial position and might have prevented fraud, at least on the scale which occurred. then there is the question of corporate governance. paragraph 7 of the resolution states that the political guidelines of the action plan on corporate governance are based mainly on transparency and disclosure. that is perfectly true, there are important transparency and disclosure initiatives, but the action plan also contains significant initiatives that are introduced via directives: it suggests appropriate and proportionate binding rules, for example on shareholders' rights and also on directors' liability. regarding independent directors, the action plan announces a recommendation aimed at fostering their role, at least on a comply-or-explain basis. it is therefore true that disclosure is key. but consultations on the action plan have shown that it is doubtful whether an approach based on binding rules on the composition and role of board committees would make much sense. lastly, to wind up this important debate, i should like to quote mr fava, who said that we need 'global rules'. i agree. it is not so easy to achieve global rules. mention was made of the wto: that is a possibility. the oecd does a lot of work in this area and we should be careful not to get ahead of ourselves. i should therefore like to support the oecd as much as possible in its work. mr jonckheer said that this is a golden opportunity to show citizens what we are here for. he is right because, as many participants in this debate have said, it is the small people who get hurt first and who lose their pension rights or their jobs. we must make clear that the european union is taking the necessary steps in order to put a halt to this kind of practice and the forthcoming parliamentary elections perhaps offer a good opportunity to do so. mr jonckheer also asked whether directors will be up to the task. that is a very relevant question. are the measures taken by the commission, the council of ministers and parliament sufficient? as mr lehne and lord inglewood have said, one cannot give any guarantees. it is impossible to give guarantees that something of this nature will not occur – guarantees only exist for vacuum cleaners! that is something we cannot do, but we can make it very difficult for people with ill intentions seeking to continue their work. we are a little bit between the devil and the deep blue sea, on the one hand between mr jonckheer, who says we should make sure this sort of thing does not happen any more and, on the other hand, mr della vedova, mr lehne and lord inglewood, who say it is impossible to completely avoid criminal fraud. however, we can make it harder. the actions undertaken by the commission and by the european union do indeed make it much harder for this sort of thing to happen again. i hope that all members of this parliament will join the commission in making clear to the electorate that the commission is doing its job and that members of the european parliament are also doing their jobs in order to prevent this sort of thing from happening again.
mr president, it is just a question of clarification. commissioner, at the beginning of your speech, you spoke about offshore banking and you listed some criteria. does this mean that you are going to present legislation on that?
in place of the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the very high and increasing number of trade and movement of people within the european union is increasingly facilitating the possibility of individual citizens or businesses being involved in litigation of a crossborder nature. these are cases in which the parties are domiciled in different member states and which therefore present difficulties. the risk in such situations is that the persons concerned will give up their rights because of the obstacles they encounter in bringing legal proceedings in a foreign country whose legislation and procedures they are not familiar with and the costs they have to bear. in addition, in many cases - such as, for example, in small claims - the costs may be even higher than the amount at stake. in other words, it is a genuine internal market brought about by the existence of a common area of justice, where individuals and businesses can access the judicial system of each member state without discrimination or penalty. this is the context in which the two green papers on which this initiative is based are set. the first deals with the transformation of the 1980 rome convention on contractual obligations into a community instrument and its renewal. the transformation of the rome convention into a community instrument under discussion today is in addition to what has already happened to the brussels convention and is certainly useful in order to ensure direct application and uniform interpretation by the court of justice. the community instrument known as rome ii, contained in the recent proposal for a regulation on the law applicable to non-contractual obligations, for which mrs wallis is the rapporteur, should complete the rules on this matter in the future. the need to integrate the three instruments is obvious and suggests not only that the rome convention be converted into a community instrument as soon as possible by adopting a specific regulation, so that the body of rules on private international law is homogeneous in terms of the source of production, but also that, at a later stage, a real codification be achieved, systematically bringing together the community provisions of brussels i, rome i and rome ii, also with a view to the enlargement of the european union. as regards the innovations to be introduced into the contents of the rome convention, it would be possible to introduce the principle that, in the case of a choice of the law of a third state, the primacy of the mandatory provisions of community law, such as the protection of the weaker party, employees or consumers, should be ensured. the scope of application of the convention rules to insurance contracts should also be extended, and certain clarifications should be introduced in the rules for consumer and employment contracts and for electronic commerce transactions, in order to ensure coordination with existing community legislation. the second green paper presented by the commission on the establishment of a european order for payment procedure and a procedure to speed up litigation in small claims is also of paramount importance. for many european citizens and businesses - especially small and medium-sized ones - it is of considerable importance that claims be settled securely and also quickly, especially when the dispute has cross-border implications because the debtor is domiciled abroad or because the judgment has to be enforced abroad. the added value of these procedures, which should be established by community regulation, for the same reasons as those we have explained with reference to the rome convention, would be the possibility of enforcement without recourse to exequatur throughout the union, allowing the recovery of large amounts of uncontested claims or the establishment of cases which creditors would otherwise be discouraged from pursuing. the regulation should also define the entire order procedure, including the conditions for the creditor's request, in order to establish a common procedure with procedural certainty and, possibly, cost certainty. the european order for payment therefore requires some additional considerations: the court has also held that the principle of the protection of legitimate expectations is not a rule of law. given that not all states have such a special procedure in their own rules of procedure and that, where it exists, it differs considerably, in order not to create a disparity of treatment between different categories of creditors, it would seem desirable to leave the parties free to use this instrument also in internal disputes.
mr president, commissioner, it is important that the issue of human rights and democracy has been brought back to the centre of the debate on relations between the european union and the mediterranean countries. almost ten years after the barcelona process, we find that these countries still have a serious democratic deficit, which has also been noted in the repeated undp reports. the question is whether the policy we have pursued so far and, above all, the instruments we have put in place have been adequate to the ambitious objectives set out in the barcelona strategy. article 2 of the clause in the bilateral association agreements has proved to be largely inadequate. but i have a deeper problem: are bilateral agreements sufficient instruments to promote the development of human rights? i believe that these agreements have been instrumental in the international recognition of regimes which otherwise would not have had legitimacy. we must be very careful about this, and i think the commission's communication asks itself this question. the ten points are to be upheld; the european parliament's democracy initiative is an interesting instrument, because it is one of the few instruments that allows us to act autonomously vis-à-vis governments. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would like, however, for this instrument to be more consistent with the rest of our policies, otherwise it risks being a way of legitimising other policies, which are essentially realpolitik. i therefore welcome this positive incentive for countries willing to make a journey; the european parliament has also made a number of recommendations on the future of the euro-mediterranean parliamentary assembly, which may have interesting potential. the most important was the sana'a conference, which brought the issue to the attention of governments and civil society. but i wonder whether we should not have a permanent and unambiguous democratic offensive by the european institutions, based on new instruments. for example, commissioner, why not try to reach a multilateral convention on human rights with these countries, which is also based on institutions capable of guaranteeing them? this would open up a different dynamic from the current situation. finally, i tabled an amendment to article 11 because i think that outsourcing this policy to an external agency is somewhat contradictory to the discourse we have had so far, since it is the european institutions that must have the power to implement this policy. i therefore ask you whether, in accordance with the financial procedures, the commission should not have the appropriate instruments to implement these objectives.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i think it is important to know - if we are to have a policy on this - what is happening in the mediterranean and the arab world: i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals, but i would like to say that i am not in favour of the commission's proposal. i say this because i have the impression that in many of our member states this awareness is rather weak. the previous speaker has already pointed out, for example, the tunisian problem, with a series of very contradictory positions, and it is perhaps no coincidence that two members of tunisian civil society were prevented from participating in the sana'a conference mentioned above: this is the only case i have been able to find in the organisation of this conference. in this respect, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i think we must abandon excessive caution, because it is important to be credible in the facts. we are not credible when, for example, we do not enforce the clauses of article 2: we are not so with governments - who consider us to be quite malleable partners - nor with non-governmental partners, whose trust we lose. i say this because, for example, the review meeting on the agreement with lebanon is approaching and i think, commissioner, that it is important to point out, at that meeting, the lack of progress on the independence of the judicial system in that country and the fact that relations with the european union should be made subject to this condition. apart from individual references to specific positions, i think it is important for the european commission or europe in general to intervene: there is a great demand for europe, perhaps as a counterpoint to other things, and there are great expectations of it. the importance of sana'a was emphasised. why is that important, commissioner? - mr president, i should like to begin by thanking the commission for its persistence; i believe that since the elections in cambodia, the european union, through the commission in particular, has shown that it can maintain a position. i believe, however, that the situation today is extremely delicate and i share mr watson's analysis. while we do not know whether mr hun sen and his party are the instigators of the too many crimes that have been committed, we do know that they have done nothing - neither they nor mr hun sen's government - to arrest even one of those guilty of these crimes. we also know that cambodia today is no richer than cambodia was ten years ago and that for ten years the european union and the international community have poured millions and millions of euros into cambodia. so, knowing all this, perhaps we should draw conclusions. the person who has failed to act as the head of government to prevent crimes, to arrest the guilty and to bring a minimum of wealth to cambodia is called mr. hun sen. i believe that if the commission were to ask the council to give it a mandate to go and talk to the americans, the australians and the japanese in order to make the removal of mr hun sen a condition for the resumption of loans from the asian bank, the international monetary fund and the world bank, it would be possible to demonstrate and make the vietnamese and the chinese understand, and i believe they have already understood this to a large extent, that the problem of change in cambodia is to remove mr hun sen and give a mandate to another member of his party, which won the elections, even though they were not perfectly democratic, by far.
mr president, i would like to return to the subject raised earlier. the decision taken by the commission on 20 february, which amends the designations, denominations, presentation and protection of certain wine products, is important because the problem does not concern portugal alone, but all european wine-growing areas. this decision, which unified the two categories with which we protected our products of the highest quality into a single category to designate quality wines, and which introduces the possibility for third countries to produce and use the mention of our wines, having as their only reference point equivalent requirements, means, from an economic point of view, a crisis for the entire top wine sector in europe. at the same time, it also means that we are giving up a number of quality guarantees to consumers for production in europe. the commission's justification, in particular, that this decision was taken to facilitate negotiations within the world trade organisation, is extremely worrying, because we continue to discourage europe from using products for which we cannot guarantee the consumer.
mr president, i am speaking on behalf of the vice-president of the commission, mrs loyola de palacio, and the italian prime minister, mr silvio berlusconi. i am speaking on behalf of vice-president loyola de palacio because, having been elected by italian pensioners in 1999 to represent them in this parliament, i have unfortunately not heard a single word confirming the policy towards the elderly and pensioners which has so far been carried out with great success by the european commission. i am speaking on behalf of the italian prime minister, silvio berlusconi, because although he knows the secret of the elixir of long life and eternal youth, he has not yet told us, and this means that we will continue to age in europe. this is very worrying to me because many more pensioners need to hear confirmation that the european commission, as well as the european parliament, is on their side against the gluttony of the budget ministers of the national states.
mr president, commissioners, i too find it difficult to hear the refrain or mantra of the lisbon objectives all the time. we are also doing it again and again, trying to convince ourselves that we can do things that we cannot do in europe. we discuss public budgets, we discuss the role that european public administrations and public administrations in individual states can play in promoting the research economy and the innovation economy. we are doing this when the financial perspective of the european union provides for a budget which will still absorb between 40 and 50 per cent of its resources for agricultural expenditure. and by spending on agriculture, what is being done to achieve the lisbon objectives? if we do not have the courage to question this, at the level of the european union budget, it will be very difficult to turn a major wheel, as could be the case with european spending, towards technological innovation and scientific research. speaking of the stability pact, we have seen major governments, from countries that are fundamental to the european union and even more so to the euro area, discuss how to achieve a deficit of 4/5 percent of the public budget. i wonder whether it is not possible in countries such as france and germany - which have public expenditure equal to or above 50 per cent of their gross domestic product - to find in these very large resources the funds needed to make infrastructure investments and to strengthen scientific research and training, particularly university training. an example comes from another large european country. i do not believe, as mr abitbol does, that the uk's better economic performance depends on the fact that the british have kept the pound sterling and have not adopted the euro. a major example comes from the uk, where a commission - not yet the blair government, but a government commission - has identified the possibility of cutting 80,000 posts in the public service, which are deemed to be of no importance to the efficiency of the public service itself, and thus saving 20 billion euros, or one and a half per cent of uk gdp, to be spent on education and then on health and safety. it is within public budgets that we must find the resources, having the courage to make costly policy choices. two other things: prospects for the european union and economic growth. we have read in the newspapers for the moment the text drawn up by a committee of experts of commissioner lamy - it is not yet the words of commissioner lamy - asking that international trade in europe be bound to national values. this means putting the european union on the road to protectionism. is this how we want to grow economically? finally, mr president, commissioners, i would like to turn to the question of immigration. just a month ago, the un secretary-general, kofi annan, came to europe and urged a policy of greater openness, first of all in the interests of europe itself and its economy. what is europe's response? it responds by closing the frontiers of the europe of the fifteen to the workers of an enlarged europe. the workers of the ten countries which will join the european union will be b-class workers; we'll have the underground community workers. this is the answer to immigration, for example. from this point of view too, rather than accepting the challenge of enlargement, europe is closing itself off. and europe is not going in the direction of lisbon, but in the direction of economic growth which is less and less important than that of the united states and, now, also of japan, but it is europe that chooses its own destiny.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in 2003 the european commission was obliged to cut its economic forecasts for the third year in a row. the euro area gross domestic product growth rate declined again in 2003, falling from 3.5% in 2000 to 1.5% in 2001, 0.9% in 2002 and 0.5% in 2003, reflecting a situation of near-stagnation in the european economy. this problem has been the subject of debate in the committee on economic and monetary affairs and has led to the report i am presenting to you today. it is a report which takes account of some important needs, namely, first of all, a proper understanding of what the stability and growth pact represents for europe at the present time. the question that is becoming more and more pressing is whether stability can exist without real growth. we have tried to answer this question because we have realised that what happened when the ecofin council adopted the known position on the problems of france and germany is that our europe looks at a problem of this magnitude in a different and fragmented way. there is no doubt that the stability pact below 3% is an essential condition, especially now that we have to keep our budgets in an organic and orderly way in the face of enlargement. for these reasons, my colleagues in the committee on economic and monetary affairs have been very insistent that the call should go to the countries of eastern europe, which are facing the european spotlight, to put their budgets in order and give us a more favourable view of the problem in the future. however, i also believe that we cannot continue to make a political issue of an important issue such as the stability pact. there are important countries which have invested a great deal and which, in my view, also have the right, indeed the duty, to proceed with structural reforms, in respect of which the necessary time is needed to put the budgets of a state in order. i am referring in particular to france, which has embarked on this path, and to germany, which has paid considerable costs in its attempt to enlarge to the other germany. this is a situation in which we believe that public investment must restart an economy that has come to a standstill and which should also make us reflect on the rise of the euro and the euro/dollar ratio. as mr randzio-plath has said, and i thank him for his cooperation in the preparation of the two reports, europe is faced with a situation in which it must find its own precise, timely and independent dimension from any kind of economic connection with the united states of america. we must use the lisbon strategy, which we are lagging far behind. the understanding is to invest more in research and above all to initiate a policy which will solve the problems of unemployment and which will enable our younger generations, through development, to aspire to secure and practical employment in europe. these are the objectives of the indicative report which we voted and dismissed in committee and which we will vote on tomorrow in this house, with a political and economic forecast which is not at all negative but which, on the contrary, draws on all the work done in these years by the european parliament. we believe that this institution should be given greater weight in all economic analysis and forecasting. the european parliament, for its own sake and for the years to come, must be the protagonist of the major forecasts of economic analysis and complement the valuable work that the commission is already doing. i thank all those who have collaborated and i hope that a fruitful debate can be launched.
mr president, i should first like to make a clarification regarding the speech by mr abitbol: president berlusconi never said that the euro was blocking the economy, but he said something different, namely that with the introduction of the euro, moments of speculation were created that must surely be eliminated. i can therefore say that italy is strongly pro-european, without any ifs and buts, and that in my view the single currency will bring benefits to the european economy in the future. i would like to congratulate mr bigliardo on his report, which is an in-depth study of the european economic situation, but also an effort to give a new interpretation to the stability and growth pact, with a careful look at the public finances of the european union. the economic recovery that started in 2002 proved to be short-lived and certainly did not generate sufficient momentum for economic growth. the european union has had to note, among other things, a rise in unemployment and a gradual deterioration in public finances. at the same time, the strong euro has created difficulties for exports and the slow downward trend in inflation has led to a stagnation in consumption. the study of these issues will make it necessary to ask whether the rigidity of the pact may have had some negative effect on productivity growth and employment growth. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states. the programmes to modernise production processes must also be increased, and more investment in research and development is essential. we therefore fully support the objective of bringing r & d expenditure to 3% of gdp by the end of the decade, of which two thirds, as agreed by the barcelona council, should be borne by the private sector, in order to reward the participation of small and mediumsized enterprises in these processes. we would also be delighted if, at least, they were not included in future in the total expenditure which sets the budget deficit to be contained within 3% of gdp. the economic growth of a territory depends to a large extent on the planned and implemented investments and the priorities adopted. certainly, transit corridors and networks for freight and passenger transport need to receive more attention. the construction and development of networks must include the completion of major axes and the elimination of existing bottlenecks, particularly in cross-border areas, by developing strategic links and interconnections. it should also be remembered that the construction of direct connection infrastructures must take account not only of the central regions of europe but also of the peripheral regions of the community. this would be the real guarantee for a real freedom of movement of people and goods within the european territory and would pave the way for a strategic interconnection with the mediterranean and eastern european countries. of course, projects of this scale involve extraordinarily large financial investments, which cannot be in conflict with the strict rules of the current covenant. it must therefore be read and interpreted in a more flexible way, but it must also be seen as a guarantee of stability in europe. its existence, which was saved and safeguarded by the prudent and intelligent choices made during the six months of the italian presidency, must be protected and confirmed, but with an objectively more realistic view of the european economic framework. mr president, the reports we are discussing express concern at the distance between the lisbon objectives and the results achieved. the council and the commission also share these concerns and rightly insist on the importance of confirming a knowledge-based economy strategy. however, i believe that, in view of the spring summit, there is also a need to reflect on the union's financial prospects and that some questions must be answered. the first question concerns how to strike a balance between the stability policy, expressed in the single currency, and a policy of encouraging research, innovation for small businesses and investment in the large networks, which enable integration between european regions. the second question concerns how to deal with a globalisation of markets which challenges the most modern firms, in the face of the united states and japan, i.e. countries which are at the forefront of research, and traditional firms, in the face of china and economies with low labour costs, social policy and environmental policies. the third question concerns the evolution of the factors of competitiveness, which implies the strengthening of the community's presence in basic research, but also in industrial policy. this community commitment is possible if the financial perspective is consistent with the objectives of enlargement of the union, but also with growth increasingly linked to human capital, the quality of products and the competitiveness of firms operating in an increasingly open market. i believe that the political problem is really about the ability to carry out structural reforms, but also to make the budgetary choices we believe are necessary to give europe a future.
mr president, i would like to stress the importance of the new focus in this parliament on the 75 million people aged 15 to 25 who will be part of an enlarged europe from 1 may and who could make up a larger share if they reach the age of 30. in italy and other countries of the european union, the actual and stable entry into the world of work takes place only close to that. a fundamental consideration is that young adults are no different from adults, but are adults in a different way. they have rights and duties which are partly different and partly similar to those of the others. the gröner report, which is also based on extensive and detailed research, proposes a commitment which is also a gamble: increase awareness and participation of young people in policy. the information we have to manage and provide to them must be clear and specific. it will not be easy to achieve the participation we want, but learning to participate and learning to inform are the best approaches to developing that active citizenship consciousness which is not yet being put into practice by all adults. if those who have not lived in the past have no future, we have no choice but to pass on to the young, who are our future, the fundamental values of our past. in this way, these updated and revised values will be the seeds for tomorrow's growth. we are certainly in favour of the european youth week because it also symbolically underlines the priority commitment to a new impetus for european youth which can count on many assets, such as intercultural learning, environmental protection, sport and the arts, to name just a few. our commitment as adults to combating social exclusion and xenophobia is doubled because it is not words but deeds and behaviour that represent the most impactful educational moment. so, working on young people and for young people also becomes a way of working on ourselves and for this opportunity we must thank our boys and girls.
mr president, this is the longest speech of my life as a member of the european parliament. i have never had three minutes to speak, and now i have been given three minutes - and i am the representative of the italian party of pensioners - to speak on a report concerning young people. i subscribe to and share the report on youth participation and information, but a short while ago, while listening to the speakers' speeches on reports concerning the budget and other subjects, i fell asleep and i apologize for that. in my sleep-wake state, two questions came to mind: but are young people different from old people? and are the elderly different from the young? they say that young people want love, but older people want love and need love too. young people want money, but old people want money and need money too. young people want to travel and dance, but old people want to travel and dance, too. no, there's got to be a difference! in 45 years of working for the elderly, i have heard them repeat many times - with regret - that getting old is bad. i am therefore sure that the european parliament has not only thought of young people but also of the elderly and has prepared a great many reports and communications for the elderly. i shall have to listen to all of them in the last months of this parliament, because so far - unfortunately - very few have been tabled. i've been doing some thinking. is it right to involve young people more in political life and in decisions that affect them? mr president, i wanted to bring to the attention of this house a matter which is shaking the hearts and consciences of the italian people. there is a terrorist named cesare battisti who was convicted in italy of four murders. on april 7th, the french judiciary will have to decide whether to grant the extradition of this terrorist to italy. i have great respect for the judiciary, but i think it would be a grave mistake not to grant extradition, it would be an attack on europe, it would show that europe is not a reality. terrorism is proven to have links in france, italy, spain and young terrorists in italy have struck more than once in recent years, they are educated by italian terrorists who are in prison. not to grant extradition would send a negative signal to these people. i hope that everyone in this house will join in this request. the commission has also been very active in the fight against terrorism, and the left has also been very active, as today a leading member of the italian left has come out very strongly to call for the extradition of this terrorist.

- mr president, i am sorry to bother you and to bother my colleagues again with a matter which they unfortunately know very well: the chechen question. it seems that there is no limit to the depravity of russian policy in chechnya: 17 members of the family of mr umar khanbiev, the chechen minister of health, who had been received by our president, nicole fontaine, and by many group chairmen and many colleagues, were arrested because of mr khanbiev's speeches in our parliament and in many other political forums in favour of a political solution. i think that mr putin's answer, the answer of the president of a so-called democracy, is really vile. i hope, mr president, that you will be able to receive mr khanbiev tomorrow, who will be here in parliament. i also hope that many colleagues who have already met him will express their solidarity with him directly, tomorrow and the day after. i think that our parliament, which has taken extremely courageous and dignified positions on chechnya, must continue. we can't let russia do what it's doing in chechnya. this is completely unacceptable.
madam president, mr mantovani is stuck in traffic and he has asked me to take his place as shadow rapporteur, which i am happy to do. the cairo programme of action has set out important objectives for development policy which, if achieved, should lead to improved living conditions in developing countries. the report is certainly a laudable initiative and a very complex subject to deal with, but in my view it has missed the main objective which was set ten years ago at the cairo conference. as shadow rapporteur for our group, the epp, i believe that all the urgent issues that we consider to be of common interest relating to population change in developing countries, namely the issues of education and access to education, nutrition, hygiene, economic growth and migration, have not been adequately addressed. on the contrary, as is evident from much of the text presented by the rapporteur, mrs junker, the focus has been almost exclusively on issues relating to reproduction and sex education, issues which are of great importance but which are not to be tackled in an ascetic manner and isolated from a broader context, especially if these statements are not fully in line with the conclusions of cairo. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. i would like to remind you that this report, which was approved by 11 votes to 7 in the committee, requires a re-balancing and must, above all, take up again the decisions made at the conference. in order to be consistent with the objectives of the action plan, i believe that recital r bis, which stresses the importance and centrality of the family as an element of cohesion and unity in society, should be welcomed. i also hope, as our group has strongly suggested, that paragraphs 6, 10, 11, 17, 23 and 26 will be reworded in a more balanced way. we cannot accept paragraph 20 without a rewording of it, which in fact promotes the 'tout court' termination of pregnancy without taking into account the national legal framework, and does so even through information campaigns in developing countries for safe terminations of pregnancy. in this delicate area, sovereignty must undoubtedly be respected, and the ethics of each country must also be respected, especially in such sensitive matters as reproductive health and reproductive rights. this is the position of the rapporteur for the opinion, the shadow rapporteur for the ppe, mr mantovani.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have only a minute, which i would like to use on one side to support the amendments tabled by monica frassoni, which specifically concern the campaign against female genital mutilation. it is a campaign which this parliament has strongly supported, indeed started with the help of the commission, and i must say that there are already great successes in the african and arab world. on the other hand, i would like to see at least part of the report reflecting our great appreciation for the arab and african women who, in their own countries, in conditions much more difficult than ours, as you can imagine, are fighting for their rights. today, 10,000 women took to the streets in mogadishu to protest against female genital mutilation. across the arab and african world, very brave women are standing up for their rights. i would like this parliament to give them a sign of great support and great appreciation.
the rapporteur. mr president, this proposal, which was tabled in july 2001, is a proposal for a directive which aims to codify and develop citizenship of the union in the broadest possible context and, in particular, the community acquis on the free movement of residence in the member states for union citizens and their family members. the council's common position - it is fair to say - was drawn up and adopted under the italian presidency. we have only got to the debate today for bureaucratic reasons. this common position of the council departs in some respects from the commission's initial proposal, but it is nevertheless highly innovative in relation to the current acquis. for example, as regards the right of residence, the residence card is replaced by a simple registration. the introduction of the right of permanent residence is unconditional after five years and generally also strengthens protection against expulsion, especially for long-term residents. but first of all, this directive has the merit of consolidating and simplifying the very complex legislation which had been trying to regulate this very delicate matter since 1968. the directive will replace nine directives and amend one regulation. this means that it will henceforth be a kind of single text defining what the rights of european citizens are or will be when they move and reside within the territory of the european union. the definition of a citizen is also given new dignity, no longer simply as a worker, a student or a pensioner, but as a citizen who has a nationality as a person. i would remind you that we are talking about community citizens and their family members, even if they are third country nationals. this directive does not deal with non-community nationals who are legally resident in the territory of the union. the union has finally adopted new legislation. we can call it a step almost historic, but certainly important. it would take longer, too long, to say about the many things that will also change in the daily lives of citizens. here only the most important. unmarried partners. from now on, in countries where domestic law recognises rights equivalent to marriage for unmarried partners, they will be considered to be fully equal to spouses and will therefore enjoy the same rights. i would remind you that at present, registered unions for same-sex and opposite-sex partners are only possible in the netherlands and belgium, where there is even civil marriage for homosexual partners. in countries where there is no specific legislation for registered partnerships, article 3 of the directive will apply. this is a difficult article to interpret and apply, and it will therefore be for the court of justice to interpret and propose it again. the rapporteur considers that a truly historic step forward has been made. for the first time, the rights of unmarried couples are recognized in a community legislative text, which is binding, even though, as i recall, it is in accordance with national legislation. family law. there are some very interesting developments in this field. as regards direct descendants, the directive limits the possibility of being considered as a member of the family within the meaning of the directive to descendants under the age of 21 or dependants. in order to be considered as such, and therefore as members of the family, direct ascendants must be dependants within the meaning of the directive. i regret that no significant progress has been made on this point compared with the current legislation. perhaps parliament would have preferred to leave the question unanswered, at least as regards direct descendants. other innovations concern the right of entry, movement and residence. suffice it to say that this directive simplifies all the existing formalities to the utmost. i said earlier that a major innovation is the right of residence, which is acquired automatically after five years of legal residence. that is the real point, the central point of the directive. there are many more, just remember that expulsion is abolished, but not in all cases. i 'll jump to conclusions . the council decided that expulsion could only be carried out on serious grounds of public policy or public security. in short, i think it was better to accept a compromise, which is certainly not perfect, than the commission's proposal and the improvements made by the committee on freedom, rather than to abandon the directive, which will certainly be a milestone in the construction of european citizenship.
mr evans, we regret that the socialist group supports the outcome produced by the council. we regret that the main demands of the european parliament have not been taken into account, and we regret that the substance of the decision is not such that there is no guarantee that it will be applied only to international traffic. it's about production, it's about selling. if it was international traffic, no problem: is prohibited in all eu countries. the commission's requirement to apply the instruments of judicial cooperation is a requirement which introduces de facto harmonisation, which does not concern only trafficking, but concerns for example the definition of categories of prohibited drugs, which therefore prevents the possibility of national reforms of national drug legislation. what is self-cultivation, for example, the cultivation of plants? the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat trafficking in human beings, and it is not surprising that the commission has taken a number of measures to combat trafficking in human beings. the results, however, are another matter, disastrous, catastrophic, in afghanistan, everywhere, with the financing of terrorism that these laws allow.
