- the next item is the report (a5-0449/2003) by mr paulsen, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council on official controls on feedingstuffs and foodstuffs (com (2003) 52 - c5-0032/2003 - 2003/0030 (cod)).
- mrs klaß, this is not the right time to make such a request, also because any such request would have to be put to the vote in the plenary, which would then decide on it. we cannot make such a request at any time during the sitting. they must therefore make this request before the vote.
- i would like to reiterate - since you too, mrs paulsen, expressed the wish that the vote should be a success - that when it comes to a decision on the vote, as mrs klaß had suggested earlier, it is a possible vote in the plenary that will decide whether the postponement of this vote can be approved or not.
commission. mr president. i am grateful that parliament has accepted our proposal on official feed and food controls so favourably, and that it has endorsed the need to ensure an improved monitoring system of the food and feed chain at national and community level. allow me to underline once more my appreciation for the intense and productive contribution of the various committees and to stress the importance and sensitivity of the issues under discussion today, which have been raised and explored as a result of mrs paulsen's excellent work. i understand the emphasis put on certain aspects, such as increased transparency and the recognition of the efforts of good businesses to produce safe food and feed. the commission regrets that its proposal to introduce penal sanctions is not supported by council and parliament. the commission considers that the use of penal sanctions would be an important strengthening of controls on food and feed operators. the commission disagrees with parliament's and the council's view that article 55(2) and (3) relate to the third pillar. on the other hand, it notes that this issue has already been submitted to the court of justice for determination in another case. in these circumstances and taking into account the overall political context of this procedure, the commission is prepared to withdraw article 55(2) and (3) of this proposal, awaiting the court's determination of the issue and reserves the right to propose again measures providing for criminal sanctions in this field. mrs paulsen raised the question of sensitive treatment of small enterprises and i would refer to the provision in the legislation that provides for a special case to be made in respect of small enterprises. i know from her extensive experience in dealing with food safety issues in this house, that she will be aware that similar sensitive provisions have been included in the hygiene regulation. she will also be aware from commissioner fischler's recent review of the cap proposals that special provision has also been made there for the funding of small and medium-sized food enterprises, particularly in rural areas, throughout the eu. mr parish raised the question of import controls. third countries must give guarantees on compliance with eu law. if these guarantees are not given, imports will be submitted to special controls, or not be allowed at all. imported food and feed will be submitted to import controls upon arrival. that control system is based on risk: the higher the risk, the more intensive the import controls. for example, food that may contain certain toxins will be checked intensively. mr whitehead then raised the question of how we can guarantee that the prior notification system works properly. there is already a system in respect of veterinary controls, where there is a form that deals with this issue of prior notification. a modest adjustment will be made to the existing provisions in relation to the veterinary controls to take this particular system into account. thought has been given to this; my officials have noted this issue as one requiring attention and it will be dealt with at a technical level. mr parish also raised the question of animal welfare. as he probably knows, animal welfare aspects are not included in the wto (world trade organisation) sanitary and phytosanitary code and there is therefore no international consensus on the organisation of controls in respect of animal welfare rules with our trading partners. the commission's proposal is to conclude agreements with third countries on animal welfare. the commission has also raised the matter in the oie in order to bring the matter on to the international agenda. there was a conference in paris a couple of weeks ago that i attended, where the oie has clearly indicated its intention to upgrade the whole question of animal welfare in international trade. this legislation also provides for national control measures to be put in place so that the fao can inspect them and be satisfied that they work properly. concern was expressed about the deflection of trade from one harbour to another and whether this would arise or not, owing to different levels of inspection and the application of less expensive control measures, thus creating an advantage for food business operators. for this reason, the proposal provides for a standard level in the veterinary sector. with regard to control measures, the proposal will contribute to reaching a harmonised level on import controls. i believe that the essential elements to avoid deflection of trade are included in the proposal. mrs klaß raised the question of the system of inspection fees and why they cannot be regulated at a more central level. there are a number of reasons for this and some have been referred to by a number of speakers earlier today. the inspection costs, for instance, are different across europe. this is due to differences in salaries, energy costs, etc. the fees in several member states are regulated at local level, which results in different fees within a single, individual member state. it is impossible for the commission to exercise its control powers in view of the complicated methods for auditing national or regional financing systems. therefore, the proposal on the table is believed to be the appropriate response to this somewhat complex issue. to conclude, i confirm that the commission accepts a substantial number of the amendments that have been tabled. a full listing of the commission's position on each of the amendments is being provided to parliament, and i trust this will be included in the record of this session see annex.. in the event that there are amendments tabled that are not part of the package, the commission will not be able to accept them.
- thank you, lieutenant. this meeting is closed. the vote will take place at 12 noon.
- the next item is the report (a5-0468/2003) by mr fourtou, on behalf of the committee on legal affairs and the internal market, on the proposal for a european parliament and council directive on measures and procedures to ensure the enforcement of intellectual property rights (com (2003) 46 - c5-0055/2003 - 2003/0024 (cod)).
thank you, mrs fourtou. i would like to add a personal thank you, since you spoke of a quick settlement. this way i and my two colleagues on the conciliation committees will have less work to do.
mr president, unlike mr imbeni, i do not wish to compliment myself on the procedure followed because an early conciliation, as the president has called it, is not a good procedure. it is not for nothing that conciliation has its own rules, its own developments, so that many members, and parliament itself, are involved in the debate, in clarifying the doubts about the text that is gradually being formed, and it does not happen that four or five representatives of political groups seize the procedure to come up with a compromise text a few days before the vote, with the order of the cavalry to the members and to the individual members of the groups not to touch what four or five members in this early conciliation have decided. i do not think that this is a good procedure, as president imbeni does. the implications are then found in the substance of this report. it is not true that this compromise clearly limits the scope of application. if that were the case, if that were your intention, then you could approve, for example, amendment no 101 which we have tabled with some of my colleagues, which explicitly limits the scope to intentional infringements for commercial purposes. the truth is that, although criminal sanctions have been removed from the compromise, there are still preventive measures which are very dangerous regardless of how the investigation and the procedure turn out. the danger is that a system will be adopted which will make it so simple, and even partly entrusted to private individuals, to search, seize property and take preventive measures which risk placing the market in a situation of uncertainty and instability, where those who have more lawyers and law firms - the big groups - will be able to intimidate and blackmail small and very small businesses and also the final consumer. it is not true that private copying is excluded from the scope of that compromise, since it is not excluded from the preventive measures. what's this all about? why was it necessary to do all this, this kind of special legislation, emergency legislation; maybe because intellectual property laws are laws that have been largely superseded by digital technology? the risk is that you, who want intellectual property to be respected as much as physical property - and this is also our concern - end up passing laws that are not respected. it is not by police or private police measures that the desired objective is achieved. this is the opposite of what is being done, which is to delegitimize the strength and value of the laws.
mr president, the subject of mrs fourtou is a very complex one: i think that the next parliament will have to work very hard to come up with a framework legislation. i must congratulate mrs fourtou because i think she has struck the best possible compromise. but like all compromises, some limitations in this relationship i unfortunately encounter. for example, while it provides potentially effective tools to combat activities such as peer to peer or fair sharing, it does not fully protect content providers and risks seriously compromising the integrity of any library. it only addresses crimes committed on a commercial scale and not more generally the infringement of intellectual property rights by anyone. it is clear that this point deserves great attention in order to find a balance. it was a long job that took many months. but i remember that from the very beginning the aim in the field of copyright protection has been to combat and stop the phenomenon which, alongside counterfeiting and piracy, allows for the illicit use of copyrighted material to the detriment of the activities of all content providers who legitimately constitute the value chain for the exploitation of audiovisual products. the question of the sum of private uses, reproduced exponentially by the digital broadband detonator or by any other advanced technological platform, will also require further reflection, especially in the future. this will cause a significant, quantifiable, commercial detriment to all content providers who base their business model on the lawful distribution of content, subject to contractual agreement with the right holders. we also need to reflect on the consequences of the new telecommunications legislation. the wording of the proposal for a directive not only misses some important objectives, but it does not appear to constitute an effective deterrent to infringement either, having lost in the long course of the various proposals the possibility of compensation for the double damage, emerging damage and lost profit. i confirm that in the short term, in order to have an effective instrument, we must give the greatest possible support to the rapporteur, mrs fourtou, and that we must do a lot of work on this in the future. the last two points, one in relation to recital 22. it states that injunctions may be taken against intermediaries whose services are used by third parties to infringe only industrial property rights and not intellectual property rights, as provided for in articles 10 and 15. that discrepancy between the wording of the provision and that of the recital is not without significance, since it is intended to support an interpretation according to which the expression 'intellectual property' in the wording of the articles in fact refers only to the assumptions of intellectual property; i therefore call on mrs fourtou to table an oral amendment to the effect that the industrial property right should be replaced by an intellectual property right. i am thinking here of italian producers protected by geographical indications, who in a situation like this could not obtain in other european countries restrictive measures, for example, against traders, transporters or advertisers who sell, transport and advertise counterfeit italian products. a final comment on article 16 is that the commission has not yet adopted a decision on the matter. i think it would be very important that the last sentence of recital 23a be reintroduced as the final paragraph of this article. in the absence of such specific legislation, it might be assumed in many countries that if the court has waived, following the payment of compensation by the bona fide possessor of counterfeit goods, the right to order the sale of counterfeit goods to be prohibited and the goods to be excluded from the commercial circuit, any commercial use of the goods is permitted. if we think about the whole medical field, for example, this becomes absolutely unacceptable.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i should also like to thank mrs fourtou for her work, which she has done with great autonomy and determination, precisely because the fight against counterfeiting and piracy must be one of the priorities of the european union. creativity, which is one of the main factors of competitiveness in our economic system, is being undermined by counterfeiters because they are attacking the industrial and intellectual property rights which are the ones that allow the proper remuneration of creativity itself. counterfeiting and piracy often end up affecting consumers too, who are led to buy goods of lower quality than the original. the damage they cause is therefore not only to the individual rightholder but to society as a whole. the directive on the enforcement of industrial and intellectual property rights is therefore an important tool in the fight against this phenomenon and must be adopted quickly before the accession of the new countries, where counterfeiting and piracy are particularly widespread. the text negotiated with the council, on which there should be broad agreement in parliament, represents a balanced compromise which should allow the directive to be adopted in a single reading. the current european and national legal framework on intellectual and industrial property is respected. the conditions are being created for its effective protection and for a more vigorous fight against illegal trade. the compromise rightly provides that the most important provisions of the directive apply only to commercial practices. we cannot think of punishing people who download music from the internet just for themselves. the request for information from the alleged counterfeiter by the alleging counterfeiter must be proportionate and justified and only be made on the order of the judicial authority. the directive also does not affect the rules on confidential information and the processing of personal data or the right not to testify against oneself. it specifies the administrative and civil penalties which, by directly affecting the economic activity carried out unlawfully, have a direct effect in preventing counterfeiting, penalties which must be both dissuasive but also proportionate and progressive in relation to the unlawful act committed.
mr president, i take up the point made by the rapporteur, mr medina, who rightly said that the proposal in question and his report as a whole are not against the commission, but are aimed at harmonizing - and i believe that this term is more appropriate than ever - and at regulating better legislation. i, who was the coordinator of the parliamentary committee which reached agreement on legislative simplification (this interinstitutional agreement), cannot ignore the importance of the debate which took place in my committee, the legal affairs committee, on mr medina's report. this debate began before we worked on the interinstitutional agreement and has continued since. the report we are discussing is therefore the presupposition and consequence of this interinstitutional agreement, which, if it had been followed - let me say this, mr president, ladies and gentlemen - by the european constitution, i believe we would have had a legislature characterized by this strong connection and by this willingness to take account of the three institutions (commission, council and parliament) around which the european reality, the european community, revolves. i agree that the interinstitutional agreement is not everything. it is an agreement which lays the foundations for a completely different role for parliament, an agreement which must go forward. for this reason, the rapporteur rightly says that the improvement of community legislation must go beyond the interinstitutional agreement. the interinstitutional agreement is therefore a prerequisite, especially for the right to be consulted automatically, as mr medina rightly states in his report, in the fields of co-regulation and self-regulation, that is to say, the right of parliament to be informed immediately by the commission, to suspend definitively the application of any voluntary agreement which it has not accepted. i believe that these are the two points on which the report has its own structure and on which there can be further agreement in the next legislature, so as to lay the foundations for a harmonious arrangement between the three institutions representing europe.
mr president, israeli government circles continue to fuel a political campaign to portray the position of the european union and the institutions as unbalanced, that is to say, as being more concerned with understanding and supporting the rights of the palestinian people than those of the israelis. all this is false, and it is a fact that israel has occupied palestinian territories since 1967 and that the european institutions, including this parliament, have repeatedly called on the state of israel to respect the rules of international law. the european institutions have basically chosen a constructive partnership, with the hope of achieving the best results in this way. as long as there are forces like those which gave birth to the geneva agreements, we must continue to cultivate this hope and to invest politically in this perspective. since 1995, the eu-israel partnership has developed under an association agreement which provides, inter alia, for preferential conditions for israeli products on the european market. however, since 1976 israel has been determining the origin of its products without making any distinction between those from its territory and those from the occupied territories, in constant and repeated violation of community law, international law and the geneva convention itself. we are at a critical juncture in this matter today. the protocol on rules of origin is also about to be amended to allow for pan-european enlargement on the one hand and an israeli-jordanian system of trade on the other. but is it possible to envisage such an enlargement without having resolved the serious dispute with israel? in 2000, the council reaffirmed the respect for international law under the fourth geneva convention in trade relations with israel. in 2001, commissioner patten had considered the continued preferential exports from the settlements to be a violation. in 2003 the council decided to find a bilateral agreement with israel. at this point, commissioner, i wanted to ask you three questions, but after your answer, i put two more. the first: i understand that you said that the commission is not prepared to accept any agreement which does not provide for the cessation of this illegal practice. however, how does the commission intend to approach this debate with the council, as it seems that some member states do not share this view? finally, is the commission prepared to consult this parliament before taking any decision on this matter? (applause from the left)
mr president, i think that this whole debate is offensive to the european left in terms of its history, culture and identity. talk about a subject like that? but why, for what reason? we are not talking about something to contribute to peace, to bring the parties to the table, but about a legal trick that has no effect, not even economically. what does this lead to? what are we going to do with the new labels tomorrow morning? and then you think that tomorrow morning there is really a major violation of international law with respect to this subject? or will we have to talk about international law being violated by the statements made by abu ala within our institutions a few days ago, in which he defined hamas as a general political force and not as a contributor to international terrorism? that is a violation of international law, not a discussion of such matters. moreover, the settlements, which are so much fought over and brought to the table in this debate, in relation to the products they produce, are not helping to bring peace to an end, that is for sure! it is certainly terrorism that is preventing the parties from coming to the negotiating table. let's talk about something serious. i say to all of you: we contribute to peace. i say to the commission: we are not being ridiculous in discussing this, when we still have to clarify the question of the funds allocated to humanitarian aid: what were their real destinations? i think when it comes to discussing legal matters, in court, the dialogue ends; or it hides behind legal pretexts. well, let's try to avoid that.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i must confess that not only in the speech by mr turchi, on which we have no doubt a consensus, but also in the speeches by mrs morgantini and mr laschet, i find an atmosphere different from that which we have experienced so far. the commissioner's speech is a speech which reiterates a position of the commission, i would say to some extent automatically. the commission would have said the same thing, i fear, if we had discussed the situation in vietnam in its usual way: the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter, but it is not yet ready to take a decision on the matter. but today i think the commission's tone is somewhat justified, for the same reasons as those put forward by mr morgantini and others. to want to be exact in the context of an overall truth that eludes me is a mistake of method. i understand passion, but not love for the truth; the french communist colleague, who speaks of the wall of shame. how much shame should your party denounce, which is silent even now in 2004. the real problem, ladies and gentlemen, and i will close by saying that at the end of this parliamentary term we are continuing to forget that the historical reasons which have enabled us to make the european path we have followed so far are based on the statement - summed up very well in the ventotene manifesto by altiero spinelli - that the container nation state cannot produce democracy, freedom, progress or truth, and that is why we continue, for example, to present the question of the palestinian national state as the problem we must solve for the palestinians and the israelis, which is not appropriate, and i also believe that israel lacks imagination, vigor and rigor in this regard. the israeli parties are like yours, they are european. i want to end by saying: we hope that, at the end of this parliamentary term, the federalist ideals, the federalist method, will be established with the utmost urgency by the accession to the european union of israel and the palestinian state that you wish to see, a state based on freedom and not on prevarication.
mr president, i am very right to say that i have said something very stupid and i would like to correct it immediately. i had thought and imagined that mrs morgantini could speak like mrs napoletano. i apologize pasqualina, and we give morgantini what is morgantini. it continues to be the war we want to get out of, from a point of view which i do not share.
the rapporteur. mr president, i simply wanted to point out that since 1996, with the nassauer report, this parliament has rejected all the council's proposals concerning europol, because the council does not accept the communityisation of europol and the financing of europol from the community budget. this means that europol is not subject to any democratic or judicial control. since the council is absent and the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs is asking, as it has already done, for the council's proposal to be rejected under rule 68 (3), i ask that the proposal be put to the vote immediately.
the rapporteur. mr president, just to ask you and ourselves what the rules on privacy are for, if we as the european union accept that they are not respected in any way. my report proposes not to amend the legislation on privacy, because it is not a matter of legislation, it is a matter of application. an example that many of you know: for one year now, the european union and its member states have agreed that the us security authorities can access the transatlantic flight booking terminals without any information, authorisation or regulation. so many of us are taking it out on the united states, when it is really a matter of rules which we have, could and should enforce and which we do not enforce, with commissioner vitorino telling us that we will soon set up a european system to do the same thing. so you can see why we don't enforce the rules: because we're already preparing to accommodate those privacy-infringing mechanisms. i hope therefore that we shall be able to vote unanimously on a report which denounces these situations. the problem is that when it comes to going to the court of justice, to enforcing the rules, the european commission, the national governments, the privacy authorities and, i fear, this parliament too, are very, very weak and inadequate.

correlator is used. mr president, commissioner, mr president-in-office of the council, ladies and gentlemen, i was the tobacco co-rapporteur for this important report on the reform of the common organization of the market in certain mediterranean products. as is well known, the european commission's explicitly stated objective is to eliminate tobacco production in europe. this is the first case of forced suppression of a production which has historical roots in our continent and which is not in a situation of market crisis. i would like to recall here the main and most obvious consequences of adopting the european commission's proposal. no reduction in the consumption of tobacco products, no positive effect on the joint fight against smoking. no positive effect on member states' tax revenues, which amount to between eur 15 and eur 65 million per year in the european union, considering only taxes on the final consumption of tobacco products. a sharp deterioration in the eu's trade balance as a result of increased imports and reduced exports, with a negative balance of more than eur 800 million per year, similar to the current eu spending on support for tobacco growers. four hundred and ninety thousand new unemployed workers in the supply chain, with one hundred and ten thousand farm owners who, beyond the temporary transposition of a decoupled amount of production, would still lose their jobs as there is ample evidence of the absence of economically viable agricultural alternatives in tobacco areas; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial instruments are used to support the development of the tobacco sector. other consequences: the closure of hundreds of european primary processing and special processing enterprises whose existence depends on the local availability of agricultural raw materials, with the consequent very significant reduction in taxes paid by enterprises and their employees and an even more serious shortfall in social security contributions; the desertification of large rural areas in the poorest areas of europe, whereas 80% of european tobacco is produced in objective 1 areas; the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the association of the overseas countries and territories with the european economic community. for the reasons and arguments briefly set out above, it is clear that the approval of the commission's proposal is, in addition to all other considerations, contrary to the objectives of the cap as laid down in article 33 of the treaty, which, as such, is not mentioned as the legal basis for the proposed reform. i would like to conclude by appealing to all my colleagues in this house, particularly those from the regions of central and northern europe, since it is well known that tobacco cultivation affects only certain areas of southern europe. hundreds of thousands of families in the european union, whose future is tied to ... the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the opinion which the agriculture committee of our parliament adopted by a large majority on the basis of my report, the commission's proposal on olive oil is considered to be a good starting point. our resolution therefore seeks to achieve improvement objectives, but also to make significant corrections. we intend to maintain the income of virtuous olive growers until 2013, to protect the environmental and socioeconomic values of olive growing, but more than the commission proposal does, we are strengthening the quality and food safety factors, the fight against fraud and the transparency of labelling, again specific support for young people who must be encouraged to start olive growing. as regards the aid system, in our assessment the extreme variability of socioeconomic and environmental conditions suggests that the percentage of decoupling should be made flexible, starting at least at 60%. we believe that the option of increasing this minimum can be considered and exercised only when there is no risk of exhaustion, of abandonment of the crop. for reasons of simplification and to obtain much more substantial resources than those provided for in the commission's proposal, with reference to quality improvement, we propose to unify the aids to olive groves and quality into a single measure and to entrust their management to producer organizations because the fragmented size of our olive-growing farms would not allow for an adequate relationship with the market. we also believe that member states should have the option, as in all other productions, of postponing the entry into force of the reform until december 2005 and 2006. we believe it is particularly important that there should be appropriate rules to ensure adequate remuneration on the world market for all mediterranean olive growing, overcoming the conflictual competition between the countries of the basin, as called for by the recent euro-mediterranean conference of agriculture ministers in venice on 27 november 2004. in other words, we need clearer, more transparent legislation, as is the case with the producers, but also and above all with the consumers, who demand and have a right to clear labels and correct information, in order to better orientate themselves towards quality production with a health-related content, as scientifically proven for extra virgin olive oil. parliament therefore once again called for better classification of oils, a ban on mixing olive oil with other fatty substances, the obligation to indicate on the label the origin of the olives, with the consequent definition of the origin of the oil, in relation to the plant, the place of collection and not the place of pressing and processing, and greater transparency in the inward processing trade, to avoid that this mechanism leads to the designation and sale of community oils obtained from oils imported from countries outside the european union. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. i have analysed the amendments tabled by other colleagues. i can only say in brief that i am in favour of amendment no 91 by mr perez, which aims to maintain the prohibition of switching to other crops, as in the case of member states opting for the mechanism of regionalisation of aid. i am, however, opposed to other amendments which have already been rejected by our committee in its vote. i would like to conclude by saying to the members of the european parliament: i have found in the text for the vote, amendment no 87, tabled in committee by mr redondo. in my opinion and not only in mine, there are valid reasons for incompatibility with the vote of our committee - and it is surprising to see this in the text before our eyes - as well as reasons for the contrast between this amendment and the productions of most of the member states. i am confident that in the final vote we will be able to demonstrate the overall consistency of this opinion and thus make clear the credibility of our parliament in the negotiations with the council and the commission.
mr president, commissioner, my personal views on the reports we are voting on tomorrow differ in some respects, such as my own assessment of the mediumterm reform of the community's agricultural policy. i have accepted the compromise presented by president daul for a reason of good sense, as a good farmer, because i think it is a very balanced compromise. what i found difficult to understand - knowing his technical and political skills - was, for example, the commission's proposal on tobacco, because the proposed system of total decoupling would lead inexorably - not only in my view but also in the view of important european universities - to the complete disappearance of production with the employment consequences mentioned earlier by the rapporteur, mr berlato. i also fail to understand why, unlike all the other productive sectors for which specific production-related payments have been maintained or member states have been given the possibility of implementing partial forms of decoupling, the tobacco sector has been the only one in which the commission has opted for total decoupling. the reasons given were, among other things, health reasons, ignoring the fact that the dismantling of tobacco production in europe does not contribute to the fight against smoking and that the lack of european production would be immediately replaced by more imports from the rest of the world. so in the committee we have worked very sensibly to maintain a common organization of the tobacco market which can ensure prospects for the development of the sector in the long term. we have worked for budgetary stability in favour of producers, for equal treatment of all categories of producers in a new common market organization compatible with the needs of stability in the agricultural sector and with the permanence of tobacco growing in europe, also for strategic reasons. the reform proposed by the commission is broadly acceptable in the olive oil sector. although i do not like the decoupling system, as you know, i think that this principle can be accepted in the case of permanent tree cultivation such as olive cultivation, provided that it serves to reduce the bureaucracy of the support system considerably and above all allows us to work with measures in the field of quality, traceability, conversion and promotion, in order to encourage further quality development. we also used common sense on the olive oil regime; we have obtained a guarantee of long-term financial support for farmers. we have worked on the historical payment entitlement, which refers to the average of the payments made to the individual producer per car produced during the reference period, and above all we have introduced and insisted on financial intervention in support of quality production, traceability, market monitoring, promotion and strengthening of producer organisations and specific environmental measures. i think that the proposal which will come from parliament and the commission on olive oil will be very useful for this sector and i hope, commissioner, that you will accept the suggestions which come to you from the agriculture committee of parliament on the tobacco sector.
mr president, i will not repeat myself on tobacco, but i find it difficult to believe that commissioner fischler believes that the commission's proposal on tobacco is motivated by health reasons. it is difficult to think of this and not think that 500 000 jobs are at risk in europe. if the link between the spread of smoking and the reduction or elimination of tobacco production were true, i would be the first person in this house to support the commission's proposal. but it 's not like that . if we accept the proposal, we will only cause harm to the citizens of the union and to tobacco producers without obtaining any real help and contribution to the fight against tobacco. i therefore ask the commission to take account of this position, which has been put forward by several parties in this house, both by the political forces and by individual members. i am of course expressing a personal view, but one which i believe is extremely widespread in this house, as we shall see in the vote tomorrow. as regards the olive oil proposal, however, i think i can say that the approach of the commission proposal is quite acceptable. the need for transparency, the attention to quality, the environmental and social value of this production, seems to me to be covered in the commission proposal and have been further reassessed in the opinion of parliament's committee on agriculture. the choice to make the decoupling rate flexible, the possibility that the national states will have, as i hope, to redefine these percentages, seems to me to be a positive osmosis between the rigid coordinates of the commission and the choices of the member states, a wise decision which will enable national governments to adopt the most appropriate options for territorial realities. the fight against mixtures remains unchanged and valid, in order to guarantee transparency and consumer protection, to combat fraud, to reduce inward processing and to achieve labelling that really enhances the value and indicates the place of production and not the place of processing. in this way the monitoring agencies will be able to continue to play a role. i would simply ask commissioner fischler that after the council vote, with the opinion of parliament, the regulations which will be adopted are consistent with the decisions, which we have unfortunately not seen in the past.
mr president, i believe that the adoption of the report tomorrow will be a clear and positive signal in relation to the partial decoupling method, a solution which would avoid the dismantling of tobacco production in europe in a very short time, thus safeguarding 50 000 jobs, of which 135 000 in italy alone. the adoption of this text would also open a new phase of negotiations within the council and with the commission, so that the demands of the operators in the sector can be fully met. it would be desirable to re-examine the conditions for the eligibility of decoupled aid by calculating it, in addition to the three-year historical average, also on the basis of the additional shares acquired by the producer after 1 january 2001. the future negotiation would also make it possible to identify precisely the recipients of the resources for restructuring and conversion. first, the aid should be clearly targeted at tobacco farms; secondly, the aid should be channelled through producer associations and be allocated, only in the second place, to the territorial areas. i would like to conclude by asking president prodi, taking note of parliament's opinion, which we hope will be positive tomorrow, to give the european commission's new mandate a new direction.
mr president, commissioner, i will not repeat what my colleague, mr enzo lavarra, said because i fully agree with his report on olive oil, but i would like to say a few things about tobacco. i would like to point out to the commissioner that there is not only a problem concerning the depressed areas of the mediterranean, a problem of monoculture which is difficult to recover and convert, but also and above all a problem of social cohesion, as well as a problem of the industry and therefore the loss of thousands of jobs which puts at risk the social cohesion of this reality. if the question of agriculture, and especially the cap, must be seen in a political light, as we want europe to do in all the matters that determine its development, then we must make an effort, commissioner, an extra effort to recover something from mediterranean crops, because i believe that too much has been done in favour of continental agriculture, for france and germany. it's a problem. no one says it, perhaps no one has the courage to say it, but someone has to do it, and so i defend mediterranean crops; i say that the health approach that was put forward here is absolutely hypocritical: if we don't use kentucky, which is a healthy leaf, we'll go and get tobacco from turkey, where the leaves are not healthy because they're riddled with pesticides. the health of the citizens will therefore be compromised and the states will not ban smoking and will continue to levy excise duties.
mr president, i join the many criticisms that have already been made by other members of this house concerning the proposal from the commission on tobacco, perhaps even attempting to explain in more detail the reasons for this refusal in the light of the justifications that have been put forward. one of them even spoke of savings for the community's agricultural budget, but it will be quite the opposite, because european manufacturers will have to buy tobacco from third countries, with the result that costs will rise, employment will fall and the negative balance of the community's trade balance will be weighed down. the conversion hypothesis, which has already been rejected by many here and which i do not share, is a solution that can be proposed for other crops, but certainly not for tobacco. there are no viable and cost-effective alternatives because tobacco is grown on small plots. the only crops available are those from the mediterranean, which are already protected by community production quotas and prohibitions. tobacco, on the other hand, is supported by the great economic efficiency of its production costs. an hour's work for tobacco costs the community budget eur 5.7. for comparison, durum wheat costs 21.4 euros, and maize 12.8 euros. fighting cancer, then, is a really untenable motivation. in fact, the possible abolition of european tobacco to help fight cancer is totally ineffective. as has been said, 80% of the tobacco consumed in europe is imported. the commission was more judicious in its approach to olive oil. the proposal he has put forward on this subject certainly seems acceptable, especially if it is perfected by maintaining the commitment to leave ample room for manoeuvre to the member states in deciding the extent of decoupling, which in any case is already acceptable in the 60/40 report issued by our committee. the commission will be able to provide the necessary information to the member states in the event of a dispute.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am well aware of the importance of this debate on the future of europe. i would like to take this opportunity to express an urgency which we radicals feel very strongly about and to which my colleague olivier dupuis has devoted more than thirty days of his hunger strike. we believe that this is an urgent need for europe's present, which unfortunately was not the urgent need of europe's recent past. i would like to return, mr president, to the chechen case. this debate is certainly important for the future and for the institutions, but if we ignore the present, the horrors of the present, we lose credibility when we want to organise a different future. i am not saying that the european parliament should be the only one to be able to do this, but i am saying that the european parliament should be able to do it. his brother magomed, the minister of defense, surrendered to prevent further bloodshed. since 29 february, in spectacular operations, dozens of armed masked men from russian forces and chechen militias have surrounded villages, entered houses, beaten, insulted, threatened to kill the khambievs to the last; they kidnapped and took with them any members of the khambiev family they could find on the spot. they've been missing for a while. in benoi, the khambiev's home village, 700 men arrived in armored vehicles, who, after beating the inhabitants and destroying everything they could destroy, kidnapped seven people and demanded the surrender of umar and magomed. i believe, ladies and gentlemen of the council, ladies and gentlemen of the commission, that what is happening in our europe must be part of our political present, and this parliament has repeatedly expressed itself in this way, from a humanitarian and sometimes even a political point of view. concerns have already been expressed by the us administration. we ask that the red cross be allowed to visit the khambiev family and the minister of defence khambiev, in accordance with the geneva conventions. these are geneva conventions to be respected, mr president. we shall put a question to the council immediately, but i think it is our duty to at least react to these methods. not only by radical non-violence, but by european political presence, by respect for the geneva conventions. we even got saddam hussein to have the red cross visit him. it is unthinkable that even this cannot be achieved.
mr president, the europe of reunification, the europe of 25, 27 or 30, cannot function with an institutional system designed for a union of six or 15. without the adoption of the constitutional treaty, we risk, at best, a europe that is unrestrained, weak, politically incapable of acting and of being a protagonist on the international stage, and, at worst, a political collapse of the union, that is, a dangerous retreat from the great strides made in recent years. that is why i am convinced that the irish government's action, which is discreet but effective, should be supported. we must certainly do it quickly, before the vote on the renewal of the european parliament, but we must also do it well, as president giscard d'estaing said yesterday in the committee on constitutional affairs. we cannot accept any compromise on the downside which would undermine the valuable work of the convention. i think it is right to start from the results of the italian presidency, which has managed to secure the agreement of all the countries concerned on 90 per cent of the proposed text. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the council's decision is implemented. but the question is not merely formal or technical: the real problem to overcome is the selfishness of some, which must be addressed anyway. we must discuss this, but if we want a europe that is politically strong and a leader in peace, this selfishness must be defeated. one last comment, mr president of the council: we must not forget to include in the preamble an explicit reference to the judeo-christian roots of europe. we cannot think of building the great house of 500 million citizens without remembering the foundations on which the common fatherland must rest.
mr president, the next council's agenda will include the lisbon strategy, the union's financial perspective and the resumption of the constituent process. i shall confine myself to the latter, since i consider it decisive. this parliament has been the stronghold of federalist policy, as the president-in-office has acknowledged, and we have appreciated his prudence and tenacity today. we also agree with your concern: time is running out on the constituent treaty. we are at a historic turning point, not only because the reunification of europe makes it more difficult and at the same time more necessary to have a constitutional charter, but also because the next parliament will see a major generational renewal of the parliamentary groups which have built the european community. the future of europe would be threatened by the stalemate of the intergovernmental conference, and a new failure, instead of untying the knot of the democratic deficit, would increase the danger of having a democracy without voters, threatened by populism. failure would also be a bad signal for the objective of making the european economy more competitive, since this objective requires more economic integration, more social cohesion, more democratic transparency. however, we share the view of commissioner kinnock: the european parliament has been a party to the convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms since the beginning of the century. we therefore ask the council to revive, by its decisions, the enthusiasm for a more democratic europe, united in its diversity and capable of a common foreign and defence policy.
mr president, i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the tobacco sector is not affected by the new measures. we believe that it is imperative to abandon the present community policy, which is unnecessarily costly for european taxpayers and consumers, and which is harmful to the environment, to the competitiveness and innovation of the agricultural sector itself and to the economies of the poorest countries. in this context, the reform of the common agricultural policy proposed by the commission, which is based on a decoupling of subsidies from production - albeit an insufficient one - can be a step in the right direction. we do not, however, support discriminatory treatment beyond the cultivation of tobacco, which is clearly based on a 'prohibition policy' on tobacco abuse. we also agree with the rapporteur that the abolition of aid to tobacco growers is certainly not the same as the fight against tobacco abuse.
mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report, which is a very good report, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done in the committee on agriculture and rural development. as this was a proposal which would also have affected the position of italian oil in certain disadvantaged areas, our aim was to reach agreement on a partial and flexible disconnection of 60% of the aid, with the possibility that the states might raise this percentage. the national alliance supported and together with other groups, the introduction of a quality criterion in addition to the landscape and social criteria, with measures such as production with a protected designation of origin, organic production, hand-picking and other non-quantitative criteria. the quality of the product gives consumers a guarantee and protects the specific agricultural characteristics of some european countries. we are therefore pleased that the italian characteristic of producing high-quality olive oil is being concretely recognised, and we welcome the support schemes for olive growers which maintain employment in depopulated rural areas and provide important guarantees for european quality olive oil. that is why i voted in favour of mr daul's report.

correlator is used. mr president, slovenia has, as we know, the best accession parameters among the ten applicant countries; the only negative point was the recognition of foreign qualifications. the recent law has resolved the situation. today, foreign securities are equated with slovenian securities. there will certainly be problems, but there is no alternative, as commissioner verheughen rightly said this morning, the man who has great merit in this enormous political and diplomatic operation. the favourable situation in slovenia depends on its size, but also on the care and ability with which the european acquis has been tackled and on the strong consensus of the parties in government and the opposition. the next objective is already to return to the euro area, a goal which seemed difficult years ago when inflation was quite high, but which now seems to be achievable in a reasonable time, given that inflation fell to between four and five per cent at the end of 2003. slovenia has thus been able to convert some of the large plants left over from the real socialism into small companies which have since become quite dynamic. in foreign policy, the question of relations with croatia remains open: the croatian parliament has recently ratified the agreement on the division of the assets of the successor countries of the former yugoslavia, an agreement signed in vienna in 2001 but only now ratified by the croats. in this legally defined framework it will therefore be easier to resolve other problems, including the croatian unilateral declaration on the exclusive marine ecological zone in the adriatic, which is also criticised by the european union as unilateral and not reached through an agreement between all the parties concerned. the general climate between zagreb and ljubljana has, however, improved recently and the values of security, stability and prosperity to which slovenia aspires are those of the european union. accession to the eu mr president, in the minute i have, i would like to speak about romania and the nicholson report. this report was drawn up in committee with an approach which was then corrected at the last minute, to my personal satisfaction and that of the whole committee on foreign affairs. we must send a firm but at the same time hopeful message to the romanian people and government. romania must proceed more seriously, more rigorously on the road to reform, but it must also get on the train of accession, the train of the european union. we cannot send a message of discouragement, a message that pushes romania out of this perspective. it is important that our family should be extended to romania, a country which needs the european union, which needs democracy, which in some areas is also eager for democracy despite the existing problems. we therefore vote with conviction for this new version of the nicholson report, in the hope that it will encourage the structural reforms needed for romania's accession to the european union in 2007.
mr president, with regard to slovenia, some serious questions remain unresolved, firstly, as regards the moral and material compensation of the victims of the exodus caused by the terrible ethnic cleansing which culminated in 1975 with the forced emigration from certain municipalities, such as piran, capodistria and pristina (formerly area b), and secondly, as regards the historical memory of the phoenicians and the unresolved question of the protection of the minorities of italian and venetian culture. in europe, the protection of minorities is not optional, and slovenia should finally take concrete steps to ensure that those who defend and promote their culture in that country, as is their right, are motivated by legitimate intentions and objectives and are placed, so to speak, under the moral and political protection of the european union. i also express my solidarity with the just demands made in parallel against the czech republic by those who defend the rights of the sudeten exiles, victims of another horrendous ethnic cleansing, which remains a stain on european communism.

council. – the council does not have any specific information regarding the issues raised in question h-0075/04 regarding the transfer of passenger data to nasa by northwest airlines, and is thus not really in a position to answer fully the questions raised by the honourable member. the council understands that the honourable member has also referred his question directly to the commission, which is the competent body in relation to most of the issues. however, i would say that detailed enquiries of this kind come within the competences of the data protection authorities in the various member states. the competences of the data protection authorities in this case would be those linked to the specific flights concerned. for instance, for data collected concerning a flight between boston and london gatwick, the united kingdom authority would be the competent authority, whilst for data collected on a flight between boston and amsterdam, the dutch authorities would be responsible. as i said at the outset, i have not enough material to give a more comprehensive answer to the member. i am sure the member will understand that, within the range of material i have, this is as comprehensive as i can be.
i understand, of course, that the council did not have the opportunity to obtain information, but i must also point out to the council that there was plenty of time to obtain it. we are talking about disclosure procedures, that is, procedures for access to documents, procedures which make the documents themselves public. we have given you all the specifications of the documents in question and i find it very strange that there has not been time to read them. it is very clear from these documents, for example, that northwest has, as is well known, a cooperation agreement with klm, and that therefore the aircraft booking databases have a huge overlap also with regard to air traffic within the european union itself. you say that this is a matter for the commission. this is not true, because international agreements are commissioned by the council. the council has mandated the european commission to determine the adequacy finding of the protection of privacy in the united states. the political responsibility therefore lies with the council. does the council therefore consider that this point should be included in the current negotiations or does it think otherwise?
i see that the results of the inquiry also confirm what i have said. besides, whenever there are foreign citizens who go to israel to go to palestine - because the palestinians have no borders to control - they are constantly subjected to this procedure and we ourselves have experienced movement impediments. there is another issue, besides our freedom of movement, which i think the council should take into account: it is palestinian sovereignty. in the current situation, the palestinians are not only no longer able to receive ngos, officials or citizens, but are in fact prisoners, as is happening now with the wall inside palestine. the oslo agreement, which provides for palestinian self-governing areas, is therefore totally frustrated by israel when it also prevents foreign nationals from moving. i therefore believe that the council must take this aspect into account and above all take concrete action to ensure that israel stops this restriction on freedom of movement, and that we can, in this case, respond in terms of reciprocity. if we have problems getting into israel, then perhaps the citizens of israel will have problems getting into europe.
mr president, commissioner, i too believe that the principle of mutual recognition is a fundamental element in the advancement of the single market. i would like to focus, however, in the few seconds i have, on the much debated question of water. the water sector, the water services sector is and will be crucial in europe too. i think it would be unreasonable to forgo the efficiency contribution that can be made by liberalisation and competition in this particular sector. it is not the ownership of water that is at stake, nor, as someone has said, not in this house, is it the privatisation of water, but only the opening up of the market for water services, which are often managed inefficiently by public, local and national monopolies. to those who say, "come and see the railways in britain", i say, "come and see the railways in britain". come and see the public aqueducts in italy, even the big aqueducts, the waste of water resources, the waste of public money. i do not say that it should be a dogma, but i do believe that opening up public services to competition and the market is even in the direction of a social europe.
the commission (es) madam president, first of all, as a spanish member of this house, exceptionally, i would like to thank all those who have spoken in solidarity with the victims of terrorism in spain and with the terrible effects of the attacks that have occurred in my country. as i said earlier, we have to get back to our work and i will try to answer your concerns. firstly, we must not forget that the issue we are dealing with here is a matter for the member states to organise their own systems of social solidarity. it is important to stress the point mrs doyle is making. it's a real problem, you know. however, it is also true that there is no harmonisation of these matters at community level. we can make progress through the coordination of national legislation - no doubt. this obviously means that each member state must adopt national legislation in accordance with the others. the open method of coordination can help us in this respect, but we do not have a single solution. there is perhaps a middle way - which is being worked on and can be advanced - namely mutual recognition, which would lead to a situation of total harmonization, but to a situation of recognition which could allow us to solve some of the problems. secondly, if we start from the fundamental fact that this is a decision and a national competence, it is clear that any element of coordination has to be done with fixed pre-established criteria. these objective criteria are what we have tried to include and to which i referred earlier in my speech. so in some cases we accept some of the solutions you propose and in other cases we think that the solutions you propose do not solve all the problems. the basic idea in the subject we are talking about is based on special advantages based on the principle of residence. in some cases, therefore, it is envisaged that if you change your residence, the advantages of one country will be replaced by the advantages of another. these advantages are subject to objective conditions, depending on the characteristics and the social security systems defined in each country. i understand that this may mean, in some specific cases, a loss of relative advantages in relation to the country of origin, reflecting the differences that exist between nationals of the various states of the union. so, from my point of view, there is no loophole in the regulation, there is coherence throughout the system, which does not mean that there cannot be specific problems and that we must try to solve them. i think the margin here is much more apparent at national level than at community level. and in this sense, the right way forward would be to adopt national measures to correct these kinds of problems or bilateral agreements between countries to resolve some of these difficulties. if not, of course, within the community, we can apply harmonisation where possible - in many of these cases it is not - we can make progress in coordination through the open method of coordination, we can make progress on the subject of mutual recognition to avoid those problems of free movement of persons to which many of you have referred. we are working on that basis and will continue to do so in the future.

the commission (es) mr president, i thank mrs jöns and her colleagues for the excellent report they have presented to us, which contains a number of interesting proposals and recommendations which deserve the commission's full attention. mrs jöns, we particularly appreciate your report's commitment to the application of the open method of coordination to improve health care for elderly people in the countries of the union. the commission plans to adopt a communication shortly with a proposal to apply this open method of coordination to healthcare issues. we will build on previous experience in this respect, because we believe that it is essential to provide assistance in this area, which is key both to tackling the problems of demographic ageing in our countries and to maintaining high and adequate levels of social protection. the first condition for launching this open method of coordination is that member states adopt a set of common objectives which do not allow a useful framework for the reform process of health-related issues to be established. in line with the conclusions of the joint report presented to the spring european council last year, we will focus on three areas: health care, accessibility and quality and financial viability. we will also have to consider the second aspect to which you have referred: monitoring. monitoring to be carried out by appropriate comparable indicators. without such indicators it is very difficult to assess the implementation of the various measures by the various member states and to see whether this learning process, which we are carrying out jointly, achieves the intended objectives. we will also develop detailed proposals for work up to 2006, when we hope to start a new simplified policy coordination process covering healthcare, pensions and social inclusion. if we are to establish this work plan, it is essential to exploit the synergies, the interrelationships in the current public health strategy and the community action programme that is linked to it. for best results, it is essential that the indicators receive strong support from this work programme. otherwise we will not have the necessary references to know the situation in each country. we hope that the council will debate the commission's proposals during the year and we believe that in this respect the report you are presenting to us today is extremely timely and useful and will be a fundamental contribution to this debate. the commission has also carefully examined the conclusions of the high-level reflection process on patient mobility and will shortly present a follow-up communication. your report, mrs jöns, deals with a wide range of issues relating to social protection, public health, the free movement of persons and services in the internal market. all of them are important instruments for defining an overall policy within our internal market competences of free movement in the public health programme and the open method of coordination applied to these issues. the commission has to pay particular attention to these different instruments so that they are coherent and consistent with each other. the fact that they are of a different legal nature does not mean that we do not have to maintain full consistency between them all and the commission will spare no effort to move in this direction. we will always remember - and this is a fundamental point - that the primary responsibility in these matters lies with the member states, especially when we talk about health and care for the chronically ill. our work on these specific points is to support to the maximum the work carried out by our member states. i thank you very much for your contribution, which will no doubt be of the greatest use to the commission.
mr president, i thank the rapporteur, mrs jöns, and also on behalf of mr mantovani, for her excellent work and for her excellent cooperation on this sensitive and urgent matter. as our group's shadow rapporteur, mr mantovani, who in january 2003 authored the report which launched the debate in the european parliament on the future of health systems and care for the elderly, reaffirmed the central importance of the key challenges, in particular the supply, quality, containment and coverage of healthcare expenditure in europe. as the commissioner also said, the ageing of the population, advances in medicine, increasing life expectancy and the growing demand for healthcare services, together with unprecedented mobility and information, are all contributing to a significant overall increase in the costs of the healthcare system, which is sometimes unsustainable for current economic conditions and especially for public administrations. according to the latest report of the european union's executive on the social situation, the proportion of the elderly aged over 75 will rise to 27% by 2010. in the next 15 years, the number of very old people, those 80 years of age or older, will increase by almost 50 percent. in parallel, the dependency rate of the elderly is also likely to increase in this context. changes in the over-80s will be more extensive and more rapid than in any other age group. this trend will also apply, in principle, to the ten new countries which will shortly rejoin the present member states. indeed, with the exception of poland, slovakia and cyprus where the working age population continues to grow, albeit slowly , demographic ageing is already well established and tending to increase in all other countries. much will then depend on the effectiveness of health strategies and policy choices at european, national and local levels. changes in this regard are not only necessary but also urgent. although at present the provision of healthcare, and of care in general, remains essentially a matter for the member states, we realise that such a trend, with its considerable and complex implications, cannot be met effectively and comprehensively at national level alone. last january, when the mantovani report on health systems in europe was adopted, there was a broad consensus in this parliament in favour of strengthening community cooperation in improving health services, which i believe is the only way to deal with the ageing of the population in particular. this trend will also require european society to adapt and citizens to change their behaviour and habits. these multiple problems, which concern different policies and are relevant both to the community and to the national sphere of full subsidiarity - think of the mobility of patients, the completion of the internal market, consumer policy - must be felt more and more urgently with regard to the decisions of the european court of justice and then find a new area in which to strengthen european cooperation, so that concrete results can be achieved as soon as possible. there is a need for deeper cooperation and the development of common standards, both qualitative and quantitative. the european union's objective, set in lisbon four years ago, was to become the most competitive economy in the world by 2010. today, in the face of low growth and unfavourable economic conditions, this objective is particularly difficult to achieve and will be missed if it is not tackled seriously, as will the other challenges ahead.

madam president, i thank the rapporteur for her work and for the excellent motion for a resolution she has tabled, a motion which i fully support. it is quite clear that the united states, like any other country that is serious about fighting terrorism, has the right to make every possible ascertainment of the identity of the people entering its territory, the purpose for which they enter, and the length of time they stay there. to this end, the european parliament has repeatedly called for and continues to call for an international agreement defining which personal data of air passengers should be automatically transferred to the us security agencies in advance, which additional data may be requested, by whom it may be used, for how long it may be stored, what the responsibilities of airlines are in the event of errors in the transcription or transmission of data and, above all, what remedies are offered to passengers in the event of errors and what remedies are available in the event of a violation of their rights. despite lengthy negotiations, the unilateral commitments made by the united states do not provide the required guarantees or an adequate level of protection: because these commitments do not constitute a binding international agreement, but rather it is explicitly stated that they do not create any rights for any public or private party; because direct access to the various airline archives generates a large amount of personal data, which varies according to the practices of the individual airlines, is collected for private purposes and may be different if collected for security purposes: for example, anyone who fails to leave the united states on a return flight could be considered a suspect. now, we're all used to buying round-trip airline tickets simply because they're often cheaper than one-way tickets. if we were to answer a police or customs officer's request for the date of our return, we would give the exact date and not the one on the air ticket that we do not intend to use for the return; but the date to be taken into account shall be that which appears in the airline's records. do you see the serious misunderstandings that can arise from the uncontrolled use of data collected for commercial purposes for security purposes? the same can be said about the accuracy of the data as the correct and complete spelling of names. i personally am still awaiting a reply from the council to an urgent question concerning a serious incident involving an italian citizen who was arrested on arrival in the united states and sent back to europe by mistake without being able to give any explanation. a european manager who is the victim of such a misunderstanding, and is unjustly considered a suspect, can see his career destroyed. and how can he get justice? it is wrong and dangerous, even for the effective fight against terrorism, to rely on indiscriminate collection of data not specifically selected for security purposes, and it is unfair not to provide for rapid and effective tools to ensure that those concerned are able to rectify erroneous data. i would remind you that article 8 of the charter of fundamental rights of the european union states: everyone has the right to access and rectify the data collected about him/her. the commission's decision does not respect community law or the principles of the union. the commission is exempted from the powers conferred on it by article 25 of the data protection directive when it declares that the united states provides adequate protection for the rights of european citizens. it is not true that a more adequate result cannot be achieved. the same american associations for the defence of fundamental rights are calling for better legal protection in their own country.
commissioner bolkestein, we will never agree on the balance between security and freedom unless we first agree that this balance must be implemented in accordance with the law. commissioner bolkestein, for the last 13 months the laws of 15 countries in the european union and also community regulations have been violated every day. you didn't take care of that. there was no need to enter into such a negotiation in the weak condition of those who are willing to give up respect for and application of their own law. of course, we are in a weak position today because we entered the negotiations in a weak position. the american security authorities cannot in any way require airlines to transfer personal data illegally. this is only possible because you, commissioner, all of you, the european commission, the national states, most of the privacy watchdogs, have accepted all of this. the alternative could be: a solution, for example, which would involve the preparation by the american security authorities of light visa mechanisms, a mechanism whereby the individual would provide personal data, not to the airline for commercial purposes but to the security authorities. you have not wanted to do this because, like the united states, you are preparing to allow the use of data collected for commercial purposes for security purposes. this is against the european convention on human rights. fighting terrorism by tolerating lawlessness for thirteen months is truly a picture of this europe; the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. this is not how we build the europe of counter-terrorism!
mr president, i must say frankly that emotionally my speech will be different from what i would have done before 11 march this year, that is to say before the tragic events in spain. let 's face it . we had partly accepted or had become accustomed to the tragedy of 9/11, and so the question of the data requested by the united states had also become part of a normal debate on the question of legitimacy. i think we have to start with an assumption: the citizens must, however, be clearly informed in advance that they will be faced with this type of requirement and therefore with this type of control. however, citizens must be equally clearly informed about the use and destination of information, in particular information which goes beyond narrow personal generalizations and may reveal, for example, membership of a religion, a political movement or sensitive tastes or habits. i would like to remind you of directive 9546, which i quoted earlier in connection with a request for clarification to commissioner bolkestein on the linguistic census in south tyrol: i hope, commissioner, that by the end of the night, sooner or later, i will have an answer. there is a principle that applies here too, namely where article 6 (1) (c) says that the personal data collected must be adequate, relevant and not excessive in relation to the purposes for which they are collected and for which they are subsequently processed. i think that this 'not exceeding the objectives' opens up a very wide spectrum of debate. the objectives are well known: the fight against terrorism; and then -- i'll be honest -- a lot of the confusion, at least inside me, is overcome, and i can also overcome the confusion about the number of agencies that have access to the data, whether a system of immediate access is used, about the lack of appeal procedures, and a lot more. if i think that all this serves a purpose as we know it - the fight against terrorism - i consider it a necessary sacrifice for a higher purpose.
