the rapporteur. mr president, i would first like to thank my colleagues for their amendments to this proposal: the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a regulation on the application of the principle of proportionality to the treatment of thirdcountry nationals who are not nationals of a member state. this measure amends the convention implementing the schengen agreement and the so-called common manual to that end. on the surface, it would seem to be a mere bureaucratic measure; the aim of the new regulation is to ensure, in view of the forthcoming enlargement and the enormous extension of the external borders of the european union, a more rational management of the crossing of those borders, in particular with a view to ensuring legal immigration and to effectively deterring and combating illegal immigration and the criminal phenomena associated with it. the aim, therefore, with a view to the complete abolition of checks at the internal borders of the member states, is to transfer these checks to the external borders by strengthening judicial, customs and police cooperation with so-called compensatory measures that can guarantee security in the territory of the european union. efforts to rationalise and harmonise border control systems are important: the gradual establishment of the schengen area has led to different procedures for border controls in each member state. this has also had a negative impact on the checks on the fulfilment of the conditions of stay, especially of short duration, of third-country nationals on the territory of the member states. the commission has therefore put forward this proposal for a council regulation with the aim of establishing the obligation and common rules for the systematic affixing of a stamp on the identification document of these citizens at the time of checks at the external borders. it is clear that this is one of many operational instruments, but the systematic affixing of a stamp on the travel document can make an effective contribution to the fight against illegal immigration. finally, i would like to point out that this regulation is certainly only a first step towards a more complete reform of the system, which includes the systematic affixing of a stamp and therefore serious checks, also when leaving the territory of the schengen area.
mr president, i simply want to say that the present situation is illegal and that a reform proposal from someone who has not been able to enforce the form so far is not credible. the united states has the right to ask for any data they want before flights; but this must be done through procedures that comply with our rules. the united states, if it wants, could even reintroduce a visa requirement: they can do that. what they can't do is take the data they collect for commercial purposes and use it for security purposes. and it's not about the bad usa! no member state, no european government could do that. i do not think we can tolerate a non-eu member state doing what a member state cannot do. mr president, the programme on democracy and the development of democracy in third countries is a small programme in terms of resources - we are not talking about large investments compared with the community budget - but it is very important in terms of content, because it is the only instrument we have for developing a policy on human rights, women's development, support for democracy, monitoring of elections, without necessarily going through the bilateral relationship with governments. it is the only instrument of its kind we have, so we must make it effective, and we cannot always see the effectiveness of our interventions. i therefore believe that it is right for parliament, in respect of the various prerogatives of the commission and parliament itself - because it is not up to us to manage - to be more closely involved, both in the planning of the measures and in the verification of the results, and above all to ask whether the instruments for implementing this programme are as flexible as reality requires, because of course reality is not as rigid and bureaucratic as our rules are sometimes. that is why the amendments we are tabled to this regulation are important and, above all, the discussions we will be able to have with the commission will be important. commissioner patten has written to us about the other regulation, for which there is no codecision procedure, proposing to involve parliament very closely in the new approach to the revision of these regulations from 2006 onwards. i therefore believe that our amendments are only the beginning of a process of deepening which will lead to the optimum in 2006, or at least to a much more timely and effective intervention than we are able to achieve today through this instrument.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, not everyone is given the opportunity to live through a historic moment like this: i am convinced that the european parliament will be able to take part in the debate on the future of the european union, and i am convinced that the european parliament will be able to play a part in the debate on the future of the union. it is a great privilege that we should be proud of and grateful to all the men and women who have brought us to this day. europe is not united today: it is united for the first time, and it is united today as it has never been in its history, because it has never been on the basis of democracy and the free will of the people. these days, watching television or attending the celebrations for the enlargement, i am often surprised by the memory of my grandfather roberto: born in 1899 in a small town in the province of mantua, italy, he was one of the last boys to be sent to the front during the first world war. it is also to him and to all like him that this day, today, must be dedicated. only three generations, and here we are, celebrating an unthinkable and long-unthinkable event. how many of us today have these same memories and these same pains, only that until now they were marked by flags of different colors? today our joy has the same colors and the same wonderful music. but this unification, and this is something that we all need to know and we all need to tell ourselves, is not complete. it is also unnecessary to deny that the joy we feel today - council. mr president, if jean monnet were standing here today, surveying this parliament he would have good reason to rejoice. he would see representatives of 25 countries and 450 million people sitting side-by-side, striving together for the common welfare of the people of europe. he would see a parliament in which parties operate on a cross-national and sometimes pan-european basis, where representatives from across our continent find as much common cause with colleagues from other countries as they do with their own compatriots. if monnet were to look beyond this parliament today, he would see countries which for centuries had been bitter enemies working hand-in-hand as convinced partners. he would have reason to rejoice. like monnet, spinelli was a man of vision. his vision of a democratic and transparent citizens' union gave impetus to the idea that a constitutional treaty would enhance the union's credibility. it gave impetus also to the idea that we the citizens deserved a union that was comprehensible to us and working in our interests. monnet hoped to make war between the nations of europe not just unthinkable but materially impossible. today we can see that the dream is closer than ever to becoming a reality. john hume, the nobel laureate and member of this house, once said: 'our european union has become the most successful peace process in europe's long history'. the two men – monnet and spinelli – were different in many ways. monnet tended to work quietly behind the scenes, using his legendary international networking skills to build the widest case for a new europe. monnet laid the foundations for this enterprise. spinelli, on the other hand, was a much more public figure, a vigorous opponent of fascism in the first half of the last century; in his later days an elected member of this parliament. they shared, however, a vital similarity: they were men of action, determined to harness their vision of a democratic, peaceful and prosperous europe to the means of advancing it in the real world. what was it about these men that has allowed the ideas they promoted many decades ago to create the impetus which, even after their deaths, propels all of us forward to continue their work? perhaps monnet's biographer, françois duchêne, described his subject best when he wrote that monnet's secret 'came from a combination of creative and critical faculties. he appealed to the romantic in people through the idealism of his goals, and to the expert in them through the realism of his means. ' making a new, peaceful europe was monnet's greatest dream. twenty-five years after his death we continue to build on the foundations he laid. spinelli, like monnet, believed that, without radical positive change, europe would be condemned to repeat its past fatal errors and endanger its future existence. in 1947 he suggested that 'nothing is sadder than the fact that the ideal europe, the cradle of law and liberty, constitutes only part of the geographical area of europe. what is more', he added, 'that area is certain to shrink and european civilisation to become a mere historical memory, unless we can at least unite what at present remains of it.' the european union has risen to this challenge. what drove both men was their experience and their abhorrence of war. however, as they sought to communicate ideas and goals that would help rescue europe from itself they could hardly have thought that from the rubble of the second world war such a strong, united europe would come about. sixty years ago europe was struggling with the last phase of the most destructive war in its history. against this background the european union, in spite of its imperfections, is a heroic endeavour undertaken against great odds. it has a record of assisting peace and prosperity among european nations and beyond, when history seemed to insist that such a goal amounted to little more than a flight of fancy. last saturday we witnessed an extraordinary reversal of history with the formal ending of the artificial divisions of europe. the enlargement of the european union to 25 member states is a tribute to the potency of a dream. it is also a tribute to the endurance and courage of many millions in central and eastern europe who often suffered for dreaming those dreams aloud. the budapest uprising of 1956, the prague spring of 1968 and the baltic singing revolutions of 1990 have contributed to the construction of a europe at the outset of the 21st century that few europeans would have dreamed of throughout the previous 2000 years of our troubled history. the french writer victor hugo said in 1849 that a day would come when 'all you nations of the continent, without losing your distinct qualities and glorious individuality, will merge into a higher unity and found the european brotherhood'. that day is upon us. now is not the time for complacency to find a home in the new europe. we cannot simply stand back and admire our handiwork. now is the time for new ambitions. on saturday the president of ireland said: 'our continent is ancient but our union is young'. we still have work to do and ambitions that remain unfulfilled. mr president, as you said, one of those ambitions must be the constitutional treaty and its very early completion. we must be careful to protect the freedom, justice, human rights, and political pluralism that that are the bedrocks of our union. we must continue to build on the peace that monnet saw replacing the deadly rivalries of his generation and their ancestors. we must also ensure that the new needs and circumstances of the union are reflected in its basic law. on the 20th anniversary of the adoption by parliament of spinelli's draft constitutional treaty we must work to agree a new treaty that will improve the basis for democratic discourse in our union, that will build on the success of our union, and that will make the union more readily understood and effective. the first major task we face together in the new union will be to agree the new constitutional treaty. in march we decided to reach agreement at the latest by the june european council meeting – just six weeks away. early agreement on a new treaty that allows the union to develop and prosper is the best way to commemorate the work of jean monnet and to mark the 20th anniversary of this parliament's draft constitution which was presented by spinelli. that is the challenge that faces us over the next few weeks – working together we will meet that challenge even as we meet here today, representatives from the 25 member states meet in dublin to hammer out details in relation to the treaty. we wish them well in that work. the whole of europe is looking to them to fulfil the dream of spinelli and monnet. (applause)
vice-president of the commission. (es) mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has had the excellent idea of paying tribute to the memory of jean monnet and altiero spinelli at the last session of its fifth term as a house elected by universal suffrage. although they are not the only founding fathers of europe, jean monnet and altiero spinelli represent perhaps more than any other, by their vision and their concrete action, the incredible evolution that europe has experienced since the second world war. jean monnet taught us what we had to do, and in the declaration of 9 may 1950 he wrote: 'europe will not be built all at once or in a joint effort: it will be built through concrete achievements, which first create a de facto solidarity'. this simple phrase showed the way forward, the way we have been going since the 1950s: concrete achievements to create a de facto solidarity. the famous policy of step by step. i am convinced that the enlargement of the union on 1 may, which will not be the last, is a striking example of this principle. but this simple statement also indicates the final destination of this path: europe, the joint work. solidarity is now a reality and europe is preparing to adopt its constitutional treaty. jean monnet lays the foundations of the present european union and sets out the method for achieving it. with his own concrete achievements, in particular as the first president of the ecsc high authority and, above all, as a constant and determined inspirer of the further development of european integration. the committee of action for a united states of europe, which he promoted for 20 years, gave rise to a large number of fruitful ideas which have subsequently been put into practice. i would like to remind everyone of your support for the direct election of the european parliament, which is what made it possible for us to be where we are today, with a parliament that really has legislative and supervisory powers. jean monnet died 25 years ago, shortly before the first european elections, which elected altiero spinelli to the european parliament. like monnet, spinelli offered europe his vision and his achievements. i remember that you were a european commissioner before you became a member of the european parliament. today we are celebrating the 20th anniversary of the adoption by the european parliament of the draft treaty establishing the european union, commonly and rightly called the 'spinelli project', for spinelli was not only the rapporteur but also the inspiration behind it. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. the 1984 draft thus launched the process in the union which led to the successive revisions of the treaty of rome, the single european act, the maastricht treaty, the treaty of amsterdam and the treaty of nice, and subsequently to the constitutional reflection which is enshrined in the framework of the last european convention. the spinelli project was innovative in several respects, eight years before the maastricht treaty, it advocated the creation, on the basis of the european community, of a genuine european union. several key provisions of the maastricht treaty derive directly from the spinelli project, such as those relating to citizenship of the union, respect for fundamental rights and the introduction of the principle of subsidiarity in the allocation and exercise of the union's competences. the amsterdam treaty included the idea of providing for the possibility of imposing sanctions on a state in the event of a serious and persistent breach of the democratic principles or of the fundamental rights on which we are founded. there are other provisions which announced 20 years in advance the draft constitutional treaty drawn up by the convention. i would refer in particular to the explicit expression of the primacy of union law, the introduction of the concept of union law, limited to the fundamental principles of union action, the commission's task of starting the programming of union activities or parliament's final word on budgetary matters. finally, it may be necessary to wait until a new phase of european integration begins before introducing some additional provisions. i am reminded in particular of article 82 of the spinelli project, which provided that when the treaty had been ratified by a majority of states representing two thirds of the total population of the union, the governments of the states which had ratified it would meet immediately to decide by common accord on the procedures and the date of entry into force of the new treaty, as well as on relations with the states which had not yet ratified it. this is something that in a union of 25 or more states will inevitably have to be considered. in any event, the spinelli project proposes the idea of founding the union on a basic treaty which would be a true constitution for europe and thus become the 'work of the whole' which jean monnet considered to be the inevitable consequence of concrete achievements. these two men, so different in culture and temperament, maintained a collaboration throughout the 1950s. the visionary spinelli thus proposed concrete solutions to the pragmatic monnet, who in turn expressed his vision of europe in his proposals to the various governments. this mixture of pragmatism and idealism is what is most needed in the present european situation. the challenges are different, but the method is still valid. we need will and determination, generosity and ambition for a europe of peace and prosperity, of values and civilization, working for a world of solidarity: our europe. (applause) i would like to thank
mr president, i should like to begin by thanking you for your words of appreciation to the committee on constitutional affairs and to those who have chaired it over the past five years. you see, mr president, it is important that the history of the building of a united europe, which began more than 50 years ago, should become part of the common memory of the peoples represented in this parliament today: it is a story to be reconstructed and retraced through the dates and milestones that marked it and through the figures of its protagonists. today we celebrate two who were not men of government, who did not sign treaties, who did not operate in the spotlight reserved for the powerful, but who were great prophets and builders, both jean monnet and altiero spinelli. both were convinced that europe, divided and bloodied by two devastating wars in the first half of the 20th century, had no future except in its unity, in overcoming nationalism, antagonism and hostility between national states. they pointed the way to the search for a common european interest, to the creation of institutions capable of expressing and pursuing it through the exercise of shared sovereignty. if it is true that monnet and spinelli took different approaches and strategies to advance the cause of european unity, it is even more true that their ideal was the same and that it became for both of them the commitment of a lifetime. the strategy of jean monnet and the great french statesman robert schuman, whose valuable adviser he was, was based on the integration of production, starting with coal and steel, between the economies and the markets of the countries participating in the project, as a strategy for the gradual advancement of european construction. it was described as functionalist, aimed at laying the concrete foundations for peace in europe, peace first of all between france and germany, and gave birth to the communities which remained in operation until the maastricht treaty of 1991. the strategy of altiero spinelli, on the other hand, was characterized as a constitutive strategy, the political foundation of european unity according to a federalist design, and found support in the vision of a great italian statesman: i'm not sure. but at critical moments, for example in 1955, when the process of integration seemed to be stalled after the failure of the attempt to create a european defence community, the efforts of monnet and spinelli, though following different paths, converged in the same direction. "monnet and i", spinelli wrote, "are pulling the cart like two stubborn donkeys: he hoped to get a new initiative from the governments. i, in the hope of getting a new impetus from the movement, a new impulse from below. despite our own skepticism and all the obstacles, we will win. and so it was. that prophecy will come true: the europeans won, the tenacious builders and fighters like monnet and spinelli. we have now reached, mr president, goals which even they did not dare to hope for, and the dream of a european constitution can finally be realized: the european parliament adopted the draft resolution by a large majority on 14 february 1984, following the work of altiero spinelli. we are also celebrating that date, which we are celebrating today, as the birth date of the constituent process. twenty years later, the dream has become a vital necessity for the new great europe. the constituent process must finally be completed by the approval of the draft adopted by the convention on the future of europe. there's no stopping it, there's no going back. that draft constitution is not perfect, but it represents the meeting point between governments and parliaments that has been worked so hard to achieve. it could be improved by drawing on the spinelli project of 20 years ago, for example where it was established - mrs de palacio has already mentioned this - that the treaty would enter into force as soon as it had been ratified by a majority of the states and of the community population. the text of the convention could be improved; the european union must not be weakened, because then the newly born union of 25 will be in danger of paralysis and crisis. and then no government, no government among those sitting in the intergovernmental conference will withdraw its word, will withdraw the consent expressed in the convention: this is the appeal that the european parliament once again makes to everyone. this is the best way to honour altiero spinelli and jean monnet in action, without rhetoric. (applause) i would like to thank
mr president, madam vice-president, mr president-in-office of the council, the europe of jean monnet and altiero spinelli was not in europe. in europe, people and institutions were united: nazi berlin, fascist rome, vichy paris, and spain and portugal, they were united! the europe of the homelands was not the europe of the vingt-et-un manifesto; it was only the europe of the holocaust. in the history of europe, there has been only one time when the people of europe have been united: we are not just a jewish people from all over europe, a people of diversity, a homosexual or a roma people, a people of the holocaust. this was europe! and the europe of feasting, feasting, hanging, and what i don't even want to keep naming anymore... then europe was in prison or murdered, our europe: he was with thomas mann, einstein, maybe marlene dietrich. he was with don sturzo, with salvemini, with fermi. europe was outside europe, and we can only reclaim those roots if we conceive of a europe based on european reform and not on the counter-reform of von papen and cardinal pacelli; the europe of the white rose, the europe of reform, and not of the mystifications of communism, fascism, nationalism or the roman taliban. we must be clear about this, mr president. you're about to celebrate something that has nothing to do with spinelli's drawing; tomorrow you will approve the europe of the fatherlands between social democrats and gaullists. europe spinelli saw it in the council, as in america in the american senate, not in the project but in the design. until we have that american federalism and the federalism of the united states of europe, i believe we will find ourselves like the popular front in paris, sitting on red flags and trenches, betraying spanish legality, making the pyrenees the place of death in madrid, while fascists and nazis came to spain to kill. then i prefer to remember salvador de madariaga and many others: this is altiero's europe! i beg you, mr president: i would like to ask the commissioner to ensure that the ventotene manifesto, with the project we are talking about, is published not only in all our european languages but also in arabic and chinese, because they are in dire need of that aspiration, that story and those faces today. president roche, there is a fundamental difference between the work on the constitution in the convention and that in the intergovernmental conference, which i fear will have only a clear and negative impact on its outcome. the whole convention was visible, the crises and other moments. it was the result of a complicated process that led to a barely sufficient compromise. but your work remains secret, and we have to rely on some informant to find out what is going on, because unfortunately our representatives and their officials also like to play at being diplomats and therefore do not tell us, as they should, what is really going on at the intergovernmental conference. i tell her that what's really going on here doesn't please me at all, and my group even less. behind a facade of optimism, which sprays a kind of reassuring molasses on the media and public opinion, there is, in my view, in reality, a further possible victory for those governments, old and new, which are happily ignoring the european interest. i think the commission shares this view, and after much ceremony and diplomacy, it has presented us with a line which is going in a completely different direction from that which is about to be agreed in dublin. if we read the text that your officials are working on today, mr president, we will see that we are faced with an exercise for which altiero spinelli would have repeated the story of 'the old man and the sea', which, as you will recall, returned to shore with its biggest fish and was then eaten by sharks. president roche said he didn't want to go into details. what a shame! if he did, everyone could see the price we are about to pay, among others, for blair and his referendum: firstly, the maintenance of unanimity on justice and home affairs, with a good deal of peace on the fight against terrorism, which is likely to see an interesting episode today with the question of the transfer of the rights of european citizens to the united states as regards the passenger agreement; secondly, the strengthening of the interpretative clause of the charter of fundamental rights, introduced by the united kingdom in thessaloniki, which virtually renders the charter of fundamental rights and its cognition virtual; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. today, therefore, we are faced with a radically different situation from that of yesterday: yesterday was the day of the dream come true; i would not, of course, like to see us preparing ourselves today to pick up the pieces of a dream that has already been shattered. i believe that the first duty we have today is to let the people know that the terms on which the presidency has chosen to negotiate are the same terms which this parliament rejected in december; the second is to start a serious debate about what the consequences of a 'no' vote on a referendum should be, which i think should be very clear: anyone who says 'no' to a referendum on the constitution, as spinelli said, is out of europe.
mr president, the europe that is being born these days is putting an end to the sad era of the great dictatorships that bled our continent in the last century. the new europe is not the europe of enlargement, however; it is the europe of reunification. finally, they are becoming partakers of a great area of peace and freedom, countries and peoples who have had to endure the communist dictatorship. welcome, colleagues from the countries of eastern europe! but we cannot think of a federation of nation-states that does not have a fundamental law governing the relations between institutions and citizens: this is why it is important to sign the constitution as soon as possible, based on the principles of freedom, subsidiarity, the centrality of the person and the promotion of a social market economy. europe cannot, however, renounce its judeo-christian roots: i am the bridge that connects the west with the east, i am the real link that connects half a billion people; the commission's proposals are the guarantee of the secularity of the institutions, and here we recall the evangelical precept: "render to god the things that are god's, but to caesar the things that are caesar's". we ask that this reference be in the preamble of our constitution, as it is in the flag: do you know that the twelve stars represent the twelve tribes of israel and are worn on mary's head? why not take the text of the polish constitution for example, as the european people's party has repeatedly urged during the debate on the convention, where there is an explicit reference to the word god? that is an example which the friends who are now members of this parliament can give us, an example which we believe the whole of europe should follow.

- the next item is the continuation of the general debate on the statements by the council and the commission on enlarged europe and its neighbours.
commission. mr president, one of the tasks of the enlarged european union will be the continuation of the enlargement process. our common target is to carry through the accession process for bulgaria and romania in 2007. this of course depends on whether both countries complete negotiations and all necessary internal reforms. 2004 will also be decisive for turkey and the report and recommendation to be brought forward by the commission in the autumn will be one of the last big decisions of our mandate. turkey's chances depends on its fulfilment of the political criteria, as was the case for all the other candidate countries. the sequence is clear. first the political criteria and then the negotiations. as regards the western balkan states, as you are aware, the thessaloniki european council last year fully supported the european perspective of the western balkan states becoming an integral part of the european union once they meet the conditions for accession and the so-called political and economic criteria and they can demonstrate that they are ready to assume the obligations of membership. two weeks ago my colleagues, commissioners patten and verheugen, presented to you the commission's opinion on croatia's accession and the commission concluded that croatia fulfils the political criteria for membership and has recommended the opening of negotiations. this is now likely to be taken up by the european council in june. this house has always shown its full support for and engagement in the enlargement process. my colleague commissioner verheugen would have liked to have been here today. in march he debated with this house the last steps in the accession of the ten new member states which joined us last week and on the accession prospects for romania and bulgaria, as well as the current state of play for turkey. less than two weeks ago, he reported to this plenary on the final preparations for the accession of cyprus, recalling once again the clear preference of the eu for a united cyprus to join the union. as we have all seen in the meantime, this desire on the part of the union was not fulfilled and, as a result of democratic referenda held on the island on 24 april, the annan plan for the reunification of the island was rejected. the commission deeply regretted that the greek cypriot community did not approve the comprehensive settlement of the cyprus problem, but we have to respect the democratic decision of the people. the commission warmly congratulated the turkish cypriots for their 'yes' vote. this signals a clear desire by the community to resolve the island's problem. last week parliament's committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy engaged in a lively debate in the wake of the referenda result and on that occasion commissioner verheugen stressed the commitment of the eu to come forward with comprehensive proposals to put an end to the isolation of the turkish cypriot community and to facilitate the reunification of cyprus by encouraging the economic development of the turkish cypriot community. the commission was invited to do so by the council at its meeting in luxembourg on 26 april, and financial support totalling eur 259 million will be earmarked for this purpose. the commission has already initiated the necessary planning to that end and intends to bring forward comprehensive proposals on trade and aid measures within the coming weeks. a new spirit of cooperation between the two communities will be necessary for the implementation of the announced measures. at this stage, i can only recall the position of the commission that the turkish cypriots must not be punished by this situation and that we will help them to overcome their economic isolation, as well as continue to bring them closer to the union. let me turn now to our neighbours outside the enlarged eu. last autumn this house had an opportunity to debate the european neighbourhood policy. this policy has been developed as a response to the new opportunities and challenges created by enlargement. together the union offers an ambitious policy to our neighbours in the east and the south, based on shared values and common interests, and aiming at preventing the creation of new dividing lines in and around our continent, and at expanding the area of peace, stability and prosperity. the european neighbourhood policy is distinct from the issue of possible accession to the eu. though we are not closing doors, the european neighbourhood policy is not about future enlargements since it is addressed to neighbours who do not currently have the prospect of accession. at this stage, we will build on the existing institutional framework of our relations, the association or partnership and cooperation agreements. differentiation is a key notion to the european neighbourhood policy. it results from the different situations of each partner, as well as of its relationship with the union. the european neighbourhood policy is based on the principle of joint ownership in line with the european union's approach that political and economic reform cannot be imposed and that successful cooperation can only build on mutual interests and the principle of equality of partners. in the course of the last months, we have been working out the various aspects of this new policy and we are preparing, in close consultation with partners, a first package of action plans to be jointly adopted with them. these actions plans will set out the agreed priorities of our relationships in key areas, as well as mechanisms to ensure their timely implementation. next week the commission will present a strategy document on the european neighbourhood policy, together with country reports on this first group of countries. the action plans will provide new political momentum to the union's relations with partner countries. the action plans will focus on political dialogue and reform, trade, market and regulatory reform, cooperation in the fields of energy, transport, the information society, the environment and people-to-people contacts. the content and priorities agreed with each partner country will differ and depend on its particular circumstances. the plans will identify key actions in a limited number of priority fields and include a clear time horizon. the action plans are expected to be adopted later this summer.
council. mr president, i welcome the opportunity to contribute to this debate on cyprus on behalf of the council. three days ago, on 1 may, a new chapter was written in the history of europe. it is no exaggeration to say that the accession of ten member states marks a truly historic moment. in effect, it brings an end to the tragic post-war divisions in europe. it does not, however, bring a complete end to divisions within our continent. the republic of cyprus has taken its place as a full member state of the european union. it is no secret that the clear preference of the european union was for the accession of a united cyprus on 1 may. we greatly regret that following the outcome of the referenda in cyprus on 24 april, it was not possible to achieve this objective. i would like today to echo the strong words from the meeting of the general affairs and external relations council in luxembourg on 26 april. the council indicated its determination to ensure that the people of cyprus will soon achieve their shared destiny as citizens of a united cyprus in the european union. we accept, however, that the people of cyprus have made a democratic choice. on 24 april, the people of both parts of cyprus voted in separate referenda on the settlement plan presented by the united nations secretary-general. the referenda were the culmination of a long and detailed negotiating process led by the united nations. it is important today, therefore, that i again emphasise in the european parliament the deep gratitude that the european union has for the determined and sustained efforts in the search for a comprehensive settlement of the cyprus problem by secretary-general annan, by his special adviser, mr alvaro de soto, and by their colleagues. they worked closely, and tirelessly, with the parties for a long period. we also recognise the very positive contributions made by the governments of greece and turkey. all in this house know and have reflected on the results of the referenda and there is little point in going further into the analysis at this point. the turkish cypriot community has now expressed its clear desire for a future within the european union. the council is determined to end the isolation of the turkish cypriots and to facilitate the reunification of the island by encouraging economic development. last week, the council took an important step with the adoption on 29 april of the regulation on a regime under article 2 of protocol 10 of the act of accession. the regulation was necessary for a smooth accession process. it provided the terms under which the relevant provisions of european union law are applied to the line between the northern part of cyprus, in which the application of the acquis communautaire is suspended, and the areas in which the government of cyprus is in control. the terms of the regulation will facilitate trade and other links across the line while ensuring that the appropriate standards of protection are maintained. i believe that the agreement reached on this regulation reflects clearly the desire of the council to send a signal of encouragement to the members of the turkish cypriot community that its future lies within a united cyprus and within the european union. the next step will be the presentation by the commission of the comprehensive proposal requested by the general affairs and external relations council. that will have a particular emphasis on the economic integration of the island and on improving contact between the two communities within the european union. the council has recommended that the eur 259 million that had been earmarked for the northern part of cyprus in the event of a settlement should now be used for this purpose. i welcome the intention of the commission to bring forward its comprehensive proposals within the coming weeks. there is a strong sympathy and a sense of respect in this parliament for the people of cyprus, of both communities, as they seek to overcome the legacy of a divided history. i have said here on more than one occasion that coming as i do from a divided island we understand very fully how difficult it is to heal the rifts. i regret that the accession celebrations in ireland on 1 may could not have included a celebration of a united cyprus in the european union, in fact i do so on a particularly personal note, because the celebrations for cyprus were held in my own home town and it would have been marvellous if we could have welcomed all of the people of cyprus there on that occasion. however, those who did come were very welcome. the statements and actions by the council since the referenda clearly confirm that the european union remains strongly committed to providing tangible assurance to the turkish cypriot community that its future will be in a united cyprus within the european union. on a personal note, i shall unfortunately have to leave before this debate ends. this is likely to be the last occasion on which i address this parliament. i want to thank very sincerely all the members of this parliament for the extraordinary courtesy and for the forbearance they have shown me during many debates in this chamber. i have seldom enjoyed an experience more than i have enjoyed addressing this parliament. i am not sure whether that enjoyment was reciprocated, but nonetheless, from my point of view my duties here were very pleasant and they were made all the more pleasant by the fact that i was able to make new friends and renew old acquaintances. in recent days this parliament has come in for some of the most uncalled for and scurrilous criticism, criticism which it does not deserve. this parliament serves the people of europe well. it is a model of parliamentary performance. it gets through its work in an efficient and effective way. undoubtedly it is not entirely made up of saints, but then very few human institutions are absolutely perfect. however, insofar as a parliamentary assembly is concerned, this comes as close to perfection as any assembly that i have ever addressed. it has been my pleasure to work with the members of this parliament. i wish those who are going into retirement all the very best for their retirement. i hope that they enjoy the years ahead of them. for those who are going into the election campaigns, i wish them well too, for safe campaigning and a very happy result. go raibh míle maith agaibh. mr roche, the president is not normally allowed to clap, but this time i joined the applause of the other members for your very beautiful and kind words to a parliament which has had the opportunity to admire the work of the irish presidency at a very difficult moment. after these festive days, we naturally hope that there will be a new occasion for celebration - at least for a large number of members of the european parliament - when the european council takes place in june. but let me say that your thanks and kind words are returned. the groups in this parliament and the parliament as a whole have really appreciated - both in the individual meetings and thanks to your presence here in parliament - the great capacity of a small country to contribute to making these wonderful days of celebration go by in the best possible way and to making this 'first time' in history of a union of peoples, states, nations and citizens a reality. i would like to thank you very much, mr roche, and ask you to convey my thanks to the whole of the irish presidency.
mr president, i also want to thank the presidency, and mr roche in particular, for the way in which he has handled our dealings together. i also enjoyed it very much. i must say that sometimes i find it difficult to attack him because he is so nice and so it is difficult to say how much i disagree with the irish presidency. i thank him very much and it was a pleasure for my group too to work with the irish presidency. i have very little time so i just want to concentrate on the question of cyprus. we all share the sadness at the disappointing result of the referendum. i understand and i respect the response of the greek cypriots. i want to believe that they want a solution and that they are not happy with the status quo. but now it is time for them and for us to act. we have to act quickly and positively to ensure that agreement can still be reached this year. the greek cypriots should tell us their ideas to ensure that enlargement is completed and the rest of the european union must assist and remind them that this business cannot remain unfinished. i have two small suggestions. the first is for the commission and the council. to say that eur 289 million have been earmarked is quite good, but how much time elapses between earmarking and spending? i became very worried when i heard commissioner nielson talking about legal problems and complications. could the commission and the council give us a timetable for committing this money and solving the european union's formal problems with northern cyprus? the government of the republic of cyprus has a problem with the implementation of the law governing the european elections in cyprus. in order to be able to vote, greek cypriots, turkish cypriots, latin maronites and armenians have to be registered on the electoral roll for the european elections. unfortunately, the cypriot parliament took a decision requiring all cypriots to register themselves on the electoral roll. as a result of this procedure, only 503 out of an estimated 80 000 turkish cypriots are eligible to vote. this is too few. if the cypriot parliament decided to include on the electoral roll all persons who have either an id card or a passport issued by the republic of cyprus, 40 000 turkish cypriots could vote. i have two requests to the government of cyprus. firstly, people from both communities who are in possession of an id card or a passport from the republic of cyprus should automatically be entitled to vote. secondly, the government of cyprus should re-open the electoral roll over the next three weeks and make a public announcement in both communities and in both languages to ensure that those able to vote in the european elections do so.

madam president, allow me to extend my greetings and thanks to the irish presidency. the new europe of 25 has an important prospect: to take care of what's going on around her. the scenario is very challenging: the direct borders will affect countries with serious problems, such as ukraine and belarus, the latter governed by a government so authoritarian that it has justified the interruption of all official relations; the european parliament has also expressed its support for the peace plan proposed by mr mashkadov, which parliament has given its full support. and then the south, turkey, now on the eve of negotiations that could lead to its accession; and the middle east, the gulf, the maghreb and the mashreq. to tackle this challenge, europe must give itself a credible political face and a coherent strategy: at institutional level, only the constitution can ensure progress in this direction; on the political front, parliament, in its report on the future of an enlarged europe, adopted last november, insisted on the need for political dialogue with all the new neighbours, warning against piecemeal and economistic actions based solely on the liberalisation of markets. political dialogue, building common rules, sharing the same values and rights, foresight and respect for the views of other countries in dealing with complex issues such as immigration: this is the way forward if we are to avoid the risk of a rift between the union and its neighbours. let us not allow the walls we have torn down within us to rise up against our new frontiers!
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. the euphoria which has broken out in this parliament and which i also feel among the leading european politicians can be shared, but i naturally wonder: is this enthusiasm also present in the population? europe is without doubt a success story without parallel, i mention only the keywords of peacekeeping and trade frameworks. europe was born as an economic area and, above all, as the hope of creating peaceful cooperation in europe after 50 years of war. what is happening today - and this is perhaps the new big challenge - is that we are too small as individual states to position ourselves in a globalized world, but we are strong enough as a whole. but the whole thing will only succeed if we are able - and this parliament is also called upon to do this - to create a european consciousness in 450 million european minds. i doubt that it is there at the moment, and we will see how high the turnout is in the european elections. we need a constitution, we need a common foreign policy, but we must not lose sight of the value of the europe of the regions. we must, of course, as a community of values, bring values out as well as goods. only if we in europe are able to regain leadership in the areas where we were strong, especially in knowledge, will we have the credibility to export values such as quality of life as a whole, peacebuilding and sustainability as the basis of responsible policies. but with the aspirations that we have built up in our prosperous democracies in recent decades, it is not europe that will grow, but unemployment, poverty and corruption. that is why i believe that we, as individuals, regions, states and the eu as a whole, must set higher standards for ourselves. not the community, the state, the eu, but each part in itself. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that's what makes us strong!
mr president, i appreciate the insistence with which the commissioner has returned today, at this stage of the closure of the legislature, to the need, as the last colleague who spoke mentioned, to have among the primary objectives the defence of the european way of life. there was a liberal wind, a unique thought that seemed to want to erase the idea of justice that in europe and in our countries gives dignity to politics. i am glad that the commissioner has made this point. in italy there is a booklet, a beautiful speech by president prodi, distributed in libraries, which indicates in this model of life what the great social democracies, christian thought for the social doctrine of the church, have built in our countries since the early 1900s, with different political experiences; and today we are different. i think that this should be our primary objective, and in 20 years we will be remembered not only for our history, for our christian roots, for the beauty of our cities, but also for having built a model of human relations among the most advanced in history behind us. i would like to conclude by expressing a concern today, at the end of this parliament's term of office: the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. of course, we must defend change, but be careful not to disrupt the security of young people! i wish the colleagues who will follow good work.
mr president, commissioner, in these five years the creation of an area of freedom, security and justice has occupied a large part of the work of the committee on civil liberties and justice, and we can say that important reforms and concrete steps have been taken towards the recognition of ever wider rights for citizens. now, as several colleagues have said, the borders of this new european union have widened far to the east and deeper into the mediterranean sea. the experience gained and the measures taken so far must be transferred to these new frontiers, in order to guarantee the safety of the citizens: schengen will only be an acceptable development when we have certain guarantees, more certain than those we have today. border control is a common problem - we must stress this again - not a private concern of individual external states. the instruments for achieving these synergies are europol and eurojust, which must be supported with conviction and, finally, with appropriate means. only then can we all together counter the dangers that threaten the area of freedom, security and justice: the european union has a duty to help the countries of central and eastern europe to develop their economic and social cohesion. but above all, a commitment: the fight against terrorism. finally, there is a challenge, perhaps as important, for those of us who will be here in the next parliament: the european union is committed to the achievement of genuine european citizenship, speedy asylum procedures and genuine free and equal movement for all. i would like to conclude by thanking commissioner vitorino, without being sarcastic, for his speech and for his experience in this parliament. when i came here in 1994 i was told that to be a good member of parliament i should criticise, possibly argue with the commission every day. i couldn 't . i am not a good member of parliament, commissioner, or you are a good commissioner. i think that's the right answer.

mr president, we cannot but take a positive view of the irish presidency, which has worked effectively and has brought two major political results for the whole of the european union: the agreement on the first constitution and the choice of the new president of the commission, which parliament will have to vote on tomorrow. the work on the constitution has been absolutely positive. we have only one great regret: the preamble does not explicitly refer to the christian roots of the european union, which are the real bridge between western and eastern europe. we regret this and the european people's party will continue to fight so that europe never forgets its origins. however, the work of the irish presidency was positive and brought to a conclusion the work done in an outstanding way by the italian presidency which had reached agreement on 95 per cent of the work of the convention. it is no coincidence that this result has been recognised by all the countries of the european union which have decided to have the treaty signed in the capital of italy - the city of rome - which is not only a landmark in italian history but also an important landmark in the history of europe and the history of the european union. the second important and positive result was the choice of the new candidate for the presidency of the european commission. this is a result that respects the vote of 13 june and, at the same time, it is a recognition of the balance of the portuguese prime minister, a president of the commission who represents the renewal and a new way of interpreting the leadership of the executive, increasingly unattached to the small events of individual countries and increasingly bent on creating a europe allied to the united states, but also politically strong and a protagonist on the international stage.

historic event, but i do not hide my concern at the declining voter turnout in each election. at the moment, citizens have difficulty identifying with the european union, which they see as too distant do alone. in this eurorealist vision we think of a europe which actively deals with the problems of economic recovery in order to combat unemployment and increase development, a europe which is able economics and finance, on which we base all our policies, belong to the last century and are outdated. we must identify new ones, adapted to the realities of the third millennium. europe must be united in the fight against terrorism and serious crime and in giving impetus to the growth of democracy in those countries where it is still denied. human rights, the dignity of the person, respect for freedom and the pursuit of peace cannot be merely documents signed by the three community institutions, but must become economic, political and cultural action towards those parts of the world where suffering and misery are tragic realities. in this context, we see the great urgency of a mediterranean policy which, after having laid down principles in the past, is now finally translated madam president, the reasons why we are voting against barroso's nomination are very simple. we are in a somewhat paradoxical situation. we are coming from elections in which virtually all governments have been defeated, not because the people did not understand or were not informed, but because there has been a mass criticism of choices such as those of the war which europe has partly made and partly not fought. we are facing a criticism of liberal policies, a profound social malaise and a social, economic and environmental crisis, which are due precisely to the failure of these liberal policies, starting with the stability pact and privatisations. a certain way of constructing europe is in crisis and rhetorical emphasis is not needed to resolve this crisis. in the face of this, a right-wing, conservative choice is made and attempts are made to propose a logic of compromise in the name of so-called governance, which is a compromise between bureaucracy and governments without addressing the problems. we think, on the contrary, that a change is needed for a europe which draws its strength from movements calling for the rejection of war, social policies, democracy and citizenship of residence. frankly, all of which candidate barroso does not represent.
president-in-office, ladies and gentlemen, i represent men and women on our continent who want a free political europe, independent of the geopolitical and geoeconomic interests of the united states today and of anyone else tomorrow. we want to protect our markets and our agricultural, industrial and service industries, which are threatened both by imports from outside europe and by relocation, the result of financial and economic globalisation, which has so far been opposed by few for reasons of sovereignty and identity, first of the nations and then of europe itself. we want to see the development of demographic and family policies, which are part of a wider social protection framework. we hope that the policies of precariousness of work and thus of the existence of individuals will be brought to an end. in order to have a strong, credible and independent commission which, as you have said, interprets the needs and expectations of a europe which must be social and cultural, it is necessary to work in the direction of the above mentioned and thus aim at economic policies which revise the stability pact. this is not reflected in what you have announced, in your actions and intentions. we shall vote against your presidency because the policies you pursue and represent have already proved to be atlanticist and hyper-liberal. on several occasions you have publicly supported us policy and have spoken in favour of a further transfer of powers from the states to the union, a union which claims to centralise and manage the future of the peoples of europe without respect for traditions and identity. it is also in favour of enlargement to include countries such as turkey, which have nothing to do with our culture and history. we shall therefore vote against this presidency, which represents policies which are in the interests of the banks, of global finance and of states whose interests and policies are in conflict with those of the nations of europe.
i would like to ask you two questions, mr president-in-office, to which i would like to have an answer, or at least a less vague answer. the first is information. as you know, the constitutional treaty, which will be signed in rome on 29 october, clearly states the value of pluralism in information. on 22 april, the european parliament adopted an important resolution on the risks of infringement of freedom of expression and information in the european union. paragraph 77 of this resolution calls on the commission to submit a proposal for a directive to safeguard pluralism and media pluralism in europe and, paragraph 78 calls on the commission to submit proposals to ensure that members of national governments cannot use their own holdings in broadcasting companies for political purposes. i should be very grateful if you would first state in your reply whether you agree with the contents of this resolution and, if so, within what time limit you intend to take the measures requested by this parliament. the second question concerns iraq, a country i have known well since 1991, since the gulf war. i would like to point out that the division of europe on the iraq question has been a serious weakness for the supporters of multilateralism and peace, two words which you used in your speech yesterday. i believe that all the european institutions must return to work with great commitment and determination so that this weakness can be overcome as soon as possible. since this point has not been dealt with with the necessary clarity in your report, i ask you to clarify what concrete initiatives you intend to take to achieve a common european approach to peace and to ensure that europe plays a prominent role and is actively involved in the process of civil and democratic reconstruction in iraq. i've been back from iraq for two weeks and i can tell you that the message from the iraqis is one: we want more europe and we do not want to be left alone in the face of the americans.

i have to ask the question. the reason why i, on behalf of the committee on transport and tourism, am submitting this oral question to the commission and the council, which i hope will somehow take up my question, is because of a fundamental concern. in these years of attempts to reduce air pollution and its effects on climate change, the greenhouse effect and the consequences mentioned just a few minutes ago, the international civil aviation organization has been one of the bodies that has been building a policy to combat air pollution. this policy also provided for the use of economic instruments - so-called market instruments - and voluntary measures, as well as the possibility of trading pollution rights and introducing taxes and fiscal instruments to reduce pollution. unfortunately, it seems that the policy of the international civil aviation organization has recently taken a different approach. despite the decisions taken at the time, which basically assigned this organization a very parallel task to that of implementing the kyoto agreements, which was to provide for the elaboration of guidelines for the organization aimed at ensuring that the different countries or the different organizations such as the european union could then implement the same standards, we are faced with the possibility that at the next assembly of the organization, to be held between 28 september and 8 october in montreal, an amendment would be tabled which would eliminate the possibility of using market instruments to combat pollution produced by aircraft engines, thus taking a step backwards in the whole policy. that's bad enough as it is. as we discuss this, as the conviction of the problems we face grows stronger, as the evidence of the relationship between aircraft emissions and the consequences for air quality grows, this step back would be a particularly serious fact. in fact, despite the progress made in the work on perfecting the organisation's guidelines, which are intended to enable the various countries to adopt, intervene or build the market for the exchange of emission rights and possibly use fiscal instruments, we are faced with an amendment, sponsored by the united states but not only, which would tend not only to stop this policy of the organisation, but even to prevent the european union, for example, from taking measures in this direction. i therefore ask the commission and the council whether they are aware of this danger, whether they share this concern and, consequently, what they intend to do to prevent this step backwards. in the alternative, i ask them what measures they intend to take to maintain the european union's right to apply such measures, to build an emissions trading market or to introduce tax measures to discourage the use of highly polluting fuels within the european union or elsewhere. i would like to ask in summary what we think we can do to ensure that the international civil aviation organisation continues its policy or, in the alternative, that the european union at least has the right to pursue it to the end.

that crimes do not go unpunished. we are facing a huge tragedy - one million displaced people, 200,000 refugees, 30,000 dead - in the face of which the international community has intervened with great delay, calling for a ceasefire and the opening of peace negotiations. in the camps we visited, we did not see death from hunger. emergency aid has arrived except in rebel-held areas, where aid mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, minister, the proposal for reform of the stability and growth pact is one of the most significant of the prodi commission's recent initiatives at a time of uncertainty in the union's economy. the choices made in recent years have ensured stability and kept inflation under control, but growth has remained low and there are no binding rules for implementing the lisbon objectives. europe's growth must be high quality and return to rates of at least 3%. the reform of the covenant can be one of the means by which we can break out of this lethargy. the commission proposal, presented here by commissioner almunia, is a good starting point but needs to be improved. the member states must make this debate a real choral debate like the one that accompanied maastricht. the debate must be structured around three key points. firstly, the future pact must be 'lisbonised' by giving more weight to all the criteria relating to structural reforms, innovation and investment in research and development. the proposals relating to the golden rule must be recovered, but on the condition that the investments covered by the 3% ceiling are established and managed at community level and do not end up in a mere market between the interests of the different countries, as mr klinz has just said. secondly, as regards debt, the trend and not the stock should be the decisive criterion. thirdly, there is a need for clarification on the credibility of the pact, which was seriously undermined by the ecofin decision in november and the final text of the constitution on the balance of power between ecofin and the commission, which favours national governments more than the text of the convention. in conclusion, i would remind you that if the political knot on the credibility of the pact and the commitment of the countries to respect it is not dissolved, the whole question of merit risks being merely academic.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner almunia, every day in our debates we say that in order to be competitive in the world economy, europe needs more economic growth, more material infrastructure and more material infrastructure and more reforms. every day we say we must implement the lisbon agenda, but the results are in plain sight. the current stability and growth pact, as it is structured, is not able to respond to growth, the infrastructure gap or the demand for reforms. it is just a poor substitute for a common economic and growth policy which does not exist. it was wanted by the strong countries because they didn't trust the weak countries, but in the end the strong countries were the first to break it. so there is something wrong and we have to honestly acknowledge it. as is well known, the three strategic objectives of growth, infrastructure and reform can be summed up for policy makers in a formula that economic theory calls the golden rule, which allows deficit financing for investment spending with intergenerational value and requires, at the same time, financing current expenditure without generating budget deficits. so far, european bureaucracies and governments have said no to the introduction of the golden rule for fear of opportunistic behaviour on the part of the states. these are legitimate fears, but fears do not make europe. in order to avoid national opportunism in the possible application of the golden rule, it is important that there be clear european objectives and clear european rules, as well as equally clear funding with ex-ante checks on their consistency and ex-post checks on the results obtained, leaving no room for accounting tricks or bullying, already too often tried in the recent past even by the so-called rigorous countries. why not try the golden rule? if the reform of the pact does not include the golden rule, the ecb is right in saying that it is better to leave it alone and leave the pact as it is, stupid and open to attack by the strongest, à la carte, but also useless if not harmful, since the timid interpretative corrections, variously sponsored, on the flexibility of the medium-term deficit as well as on the sustainability of the debt are of little use. commissioner almunia, i wish you well in your work and i urge you to use all the courage necessary at such a difficult time for the building of our europe. do not listen to the many concerned and disguised sirens who risk wrecking not only the credibility of the pact but, along with it, the credibility of the whole of europe.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to say to my colleagues from the people - obviously not to mr brunetta, who has quite different ideas on this subject - that the pact is not being changed to lessen its rigor. that would be a mistake. the covenant must be changed because it has contradicted itself. its mechanisms are in balance if nominal growth in the economy is around 5 percent. our european potential growth is consistently below this figure, so the pact does not hold. if you leave it like that, the debt will rise accordingly. the commission's proposals are good. they can be enriched and strengthened with regard to lisbon and with regard to the strengthening of european integration, that is to say by avoiding risks of disarticulation of policies at national level. if, for example, precise mechanisms are identified to make the pact fully and automatically cyclical, to support european investment programmes or to give predefined margins of flexibility to countries facing structural lisbon reforms, all this would not mean reducing rigor, but making rigor effective and oriented towards growth and integration. finally, it is right to look more closely at debt, not at the stock of debt but at the dynamics of debt. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the council has informed us that it is being forced to withdraw from our debate for imperative reasons. so here's how we're gonna do this: i shall give the floor to mr zalm, minister for the council, who will report on the impression and content of the debate, and then the debate will continue and conclude with the intervention of commissioner almunia.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, although this is not the subject of the debate, i take this opportunity to express my regret that the preamble to the constitution did not include a reference to the christian roots of europe. that said, i can only share the need to give the maximum popular information to the draft constitution and to define a coordinated timetable for the ratification of the constitution itself, hoping that all european citizens may have the right and the opportunity to participate in this constituent phase through popular referendums in each member state. we must not be afraid of participation and democracy, because only then will the new europe of the people, of rights and of the constitution be a truly strong and authoritative europe.
commission. mr president, i wish to begin by thanking all members for this lively, controversial and important debate. the commission has been informed by the portuguese permanent representation in brussels that the 'women on waves' association, amongst others, appealed against the decision to refuse entry of the vessel into portugal. as i said earlier, on 6 september 2004 the administrative and fiscal court of coimbra concluded that there had been no violation of community law. this decision is the subject of a further appeal. the commission intends to seek information on the precise motives and implications of the decision of the portuguese government. member states enjoy discretionary powers to determine the scope of the concepts of public policy, public security and public health on the basis of their legislation and national case law. however, measures restricting freedom of movement adopted on those grounds must comply with the provisions of directive 64/221/eec, which limits member states' discretionary powers. as i have already stated, the commission believes that any member state adopting a decision restricting the free movement of persons must respect fundamental rights, including freedom of expression, as general principles of community law. commission. mr president, i wish to thank the honourable members for the debate and mr davies, in particular, for his kind words in favour of the conservation of the rare species of swedish commissioners! i will comment briefly on some of the questions raised. firstly, on the issue of enforcement and information exchange, last week member states agreed to step up activities in this area. i should like to tell mr sjöstedt that we are totally against secret ballots and i can confirm that we will vote against that. mrs auken mentioned sea turtles. the convention accords the strictest protection to sea turtles and we support the proposed protection for fresh–water turtles as well. the location was decided at the last conference and it will not be changed now. on the great white shark, madagascar and australia have informed us that, in the light of widespread legal and scientific objections, they have amended their proposal, removing the demand for the zero quota. we can now give this proposal our unqualified support. finally, we welcome parliament's initiative in bringing forward this resolution. we are pleased that so many of the views expressed on issues such as bushmeat, napoleon fish, minke whale and ramin, are in accordance with the community consensus. ideally, we would have preferred to see a resolution that balances the need for greater protection of some species with the sometimes conflicting considerations of promoting sustainable use and, above all, respecting the scientific integrity of the convention. this would be in the best interests of cites and of nature conservation in general. nevertheless, i am pleased that parliament has prepared a resolution in such a short time and i thank you for the debate.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the situation in burma, a country which is living under an oppressive, violent and very bloody regime, is well known. however, while the incidents of the imprisonment of aung san suu kyi and the inability of the lnd, the party that won the elections, to take political action are well known, other very serious facts are less well known, such as the recruitment of child soldiers, the introduction of forced labour for a large part of the population and, which is particularly sensitive to women in this parliament but i believe also to all civilians, the abuses being perpetrated on women from ethnic minorities. there is a body of documentation at the united nations which i believe should also be examined by this parliament. in this context, we believe that the request of the asean countries, which have already associated burma with their forum, to invite it to the next asem summit, which sees the european union engaged in a dialogue of cooperation and political exchange with the asian countries, should be accepted. i believe that the foreign ministers have already, in an informal meeting, unexpectedly - if i may be so bold - given their assent to this participation. i believe that the european council must act differently to restore a situation in which it cannot see the european citizens sitting side by side with their heads of state and government and the representatives of a country so bloodthirsty, with such a violent and repressive regime. we therefore call on the european council to adopt a different position, one which, through the asean countries, can convince burma to make a change.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, it is incredible to discuss the maldives when we all think of the name as a holiday and a paradise. in fact, there is a systematic violation of human rights on these islands. we are facing a situation in which women and children are deprived of their fundamental freedoms and, above all, a process of mass and arbitrary detention of many citizens is under way. i believe that the european union should promote a widespread initiative to support a campaign to raise awareness among european citizens in our territory so that they do not go on holiday to the maldives before the dramatic situation of human rights and the systematic violation of democracy in that territory has been clarified. i believe that a strong resolution is needed to ensure that the maldives are indeed a paradise, but a paradise where democracy and respect for human rights prevail.

mr president, at the beginning of your mandate we voted in favour of the directive in the hope that some crucial points would be resolved. unfortunately, there are still many to be dissolved. the immigration and human trafficking have been dealt with in a marginal way. the fight against terrorism has not been a real priority, despite the signs that even before 9/11 made clear the need for with the consequence that international terrorism has now also become mass media terrorism. it has learned to use new technologies against us, which should be used not only for economic purposes but also to spread the principles of freedom, democracy and human rights. there are rules for the press, for television press, for printed matter, but not for the internet, and this is an open problem.
